    docker-compose.yaml (Local Development):
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    [FILE: docker-compose.yaml]

    version: "3.9"

    services:
      db:
        image: postgres:16-alpine
        container_name: anihpj-db
        environment:
          POSTGRES_DB: anihpj
          POSTGRES_USER: anihpj_user
          POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${DB_PASSWORD:-devpassword}
        volumes:
          - postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
        ports:
          - "5432:5432"
        healthcheck:
          test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U anihpj_user -d anihpj"]
          interval: 10s
          timeout: 5s
          retries: 5

      web:
        build:
          context: .
          dockerfile: Dockerfile
        container_name: anihpj-web
        command: python manage.py runserver 0.0.0.0:8000
        volumes:
          - .:/app
          - static_volume:/app/staticfiles
          - media_volume:/app/media
        ports:
          - "8000:8000"
        environment:
          - DATABASE_URL=postgres://anihpj_user:${DB_PASSWORD:-devpassword}@db:5432/anihpj
          - DJANGO_SECRET_KEY=${DJANGO_SECRET_KEY:-dev-secret-key-change-in-prod}
          - DJANGO_DEBUG=True
          - DJANGO_ALLOWED_HOSTS=localhost,127.0.0.1
        depends_on:
          db:
            condition: service_healthy

    volumes:
      postgres_data:
      static_volume:
      media_volume:

    [END DOCKER-COMPOSE.YAML]

    Docker Build & Run Commands:
    ────────────────────────────
    # Build the image
    docker build -t anihpj:latest .

    # Tag for registry
    docker tag anihpj:latest your-registry/anihpj:v1.0.0

    # Scan for vulnerabilities (we'll cover this in Chapter 23)
    trivy image anihpj:latest

    # Push to registry
    docker push your-registry/anihpj:v1.0.0

    # Run locally
    docker run -d -p 8000:8000 --name anihpj-app anihpj:latest

    # Check logs
    docker logs anihpj-app

    # Execute shell (for debugging — avoid in production!)
    docker exec -it anihpj-app /bin/bash

    Security Notes on This Dockerfile:
    ──────────────────────────────────
    ✅ Multi-stage build: builder tools NOT in final image
    ✅ Non-root user: anihpj user with no sudo privileges
    ✅ Virtual environment: isolates Python dependencies
    ✅ Minimal base image: python:3.11-slim-bookworm (not full Debian)
    ✅ No build tools in runtime: smaller attack surface
    ✅ Health check: Kubernetes can detect unhealthy pods
    ✅ .dockerignore: secrets (.env) NEVER enter the image
    ✅ Layer caching: requirements.txt copied before source code
    ⚠️  Gunicorn workers=3: adjust based on CPU cores available
    ⚠️  Secrets via env vars: use Kubernetes Secrets in production (Ch 19)

    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │               🧠 THE CKS SECURITY PHILOSOPHY & MINDSET                       │
    │        Before we dive into tools and techniques, understand the WHY          │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    THE 5 CORE TRUTHS OF KUBERNETES SECURITY
    ────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                              │
    │  TRUTH 1: KUBERNETES IS INSECURE BY DEFAULT                                  │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────────                                       │
    │  Out of the box, a new Kubernetes cluster:                                   │
    │  ✗ All pods can talk to each other (no NetworkPolicy)                        │
    │  ✗ Containers run as root (UID 0 by default)                                 │
    │  ✗ All syscalls are allowed (no Seccomp profile)                             │
    │  ✗ Secrets are stored as base64 in etcd (NOT encrypted)                      │
    │  ✗ API server has no audit logging                                           │
    │  ✗ ServiceAccount tokens have cluster-admin by default                       │
    │  → Every single one of these is a CKS exam topic. You fix them ALL.          │
    │                                                                              │
    │  TRUTH 2: SECURITY IS A CONTINUOUS PROCESS, NOT A CHECKBOX                   │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────                    │
    │  You cannot "install security" and walk away. Security is:                   │
    │  • Continuous scanning: new CVEs appear every day                             │
    │  • Continuous monitoring: attackers try 24/7                                  │
    │  • Continuous improvement: your threat model evolves                          │
    │  → CKS tests your ability to SET UP and MAINTAIN security controls           │
    │                                                                              │
    │  TRUTH 3: THE ATTACKER ONLY NEEDS ONE HOLE                                   │
    │  ────────────────────────────────────────                                     │
    │  You must plug ALL holes. The attacker only needs to find ONE.                │
    │  This is why defense-in-depth matters:                                        │
    │  • If RBAC fails → NetworkPolicy still blocks                                 │
    │  • If NetworkPolicy fails → Seccomp still blocks                              │
    │  • If Seccomp fails → AppArmor still blocks                                   │
    │  • If AppArmor fails → Falco still alerts                                     │
    │  → CKS exam tests your ability to LAYER defenses                             │
    │                                                                              │
    │  TRUTH 4: COMPLEXITY IS THE ENEMY OF SECURITY                                │
    │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────                               │
    │  The more complex your security setup, the more likely you'll misconfigure it.│
    │  • Simple RBAC rules > complex RBAC rules                                     │
    │  • Default-deny + specific allow > permissive rules                           │
    │  • Built-in PSS > custom admission webhooks (when possible)                   │
    │  → CKS tests your ability to make SIMPLE, EFFECTIVE security choices          │
    │                                                                              │
    │  TRUTH 5: YOU MUST VERIFY EVERYTHING                                          │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────                                           │
    │  Never assume a security control works. Always verify.                         │
    │  • Created RBAC? Run kubectl auth can-i                                      │
    │  • Created NetworkPolicy? Run ping from a blocked pod                         │
    │  • Set Seccomp? Try a blocked syscall and see if it fails                     │
    │  → CKS exam GRADES on verification — if you don't verify, you lose points    │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    THE CKS EXAMINER'S MENTAL MODEL — WHAT THEY ARE ACTUALLY TESTING
    ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  The CKS exam isn't testing if you've memorized YAML. It's testing if you     │
    │  can THINK like a security engineer and APPLY the right control.             │
    │                                                                               │
    │  For EVERY question, the examiner is checking:                                │
    │                                                                               │
    │  1. CORRECTNESS: Did you apply the RIGHT security control?                   │
    │     ── Example: They ask to restrict syscalls → you choose Seccomp (correct)  │
    │                                                                               │
    │  2. COMPLETENESS: Did you configure ALL required fields?                     │
    │     ── Example: RBAC → verbs, resources, apiGroups, namespace ALL correct     │
    │                                                                               │
    │  3. VERIFICATION: Did you show PROOF it works?                               │
    │     ── Example: After creating NetworkPolicy, you ran a test to verify        │
    │                                                                               │
    │  4. PERSISTENCE: Will your fix SURVIVE a pod restart/node reboot?            │
    │     ── Example: YAML file saved (not just imperative command)                 │
    │                                                                               │
    │  ALWAYS ASK YOURSELF:                                                         │
    │  "If I were an attacker, how would I bypass what I just configured?"          │
    │  "If I were the examiner, am I 100% convinced this is secure?"               │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    [THE CKS SECURITY DECISION FRAMEWORK — which tool for which problem?]
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                              │
    │  PROBLEM: "I need to..."                                                     │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...restrict which syscalls a container can make                             │
    │    → SECCOMP PROFILE (Ch 15)                                                 │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...restrict which files/paths a container can access on the node            │
    │    → APPARMOR PROFILE (Ch 14)                                                │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...ensure all pods run as non-root, drop all capabilities                   │
    │    → POD SECURITY STANDARDS — Restricted (Ch 17)                             │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...restrict which pods can talk to which other pods                         │
    │    → NETWORKPOLICY (Ch 6)                                                    │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...control who can do what via the Kubernetes API                           │
    │    → RBAC (Ch 9)                                                             │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...detect when someone spawns a shell inside a container                    │
    │    → FALCO (Ch 29)                                                           │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...record every API call made to the cluster                                │
    │    → AUDIT LOGGING (Ch 28)                                                   │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...ensure only scanned/signed images are deployed                           │
    │    → ADMISSION CONTROLLER + IMAGE SCANNING (Ch 23-24)                        │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...protect secrets from being read directly from etcd                       │
    │    → ENCRYPTION AT REST (Ch 19)                                              │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...give each pod a unique, minimal cloud IAM role                            │
    │    → SERVICE ACCOUNTS + IRSA/WI (Ch 10)                                      │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...measure my cluster against industry security standards                    │
    │    → CIS BENCHMARKS + kube-bench (Ch 4)                                      │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...run containers with kernel-level isolation                                │
    │    → gVisor / Kata Containers (Ch 21)                                        │
    │                                                                              │
    │  ...encrypt ALL pod-to-pod traffic automatically                              │
    │    → mTLS / SERVICE MESH (Ch 22)                                              │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
