Chapter 1: Introduction to Kubernetes Security
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    1.1  Why Kubernetes Security Is Different
         - Kubernetes is powerful — and that power creates a large attack surface
         - A single misconfiguration can expose all workloads in the cluster
         - Real-world breaches: Tesla, Capital One, Shopify — all had K8s incidents
         - Security is not an add-on — it must be built into every layer
         - The "assume breach" mindset: act as if attackers already got in
         [ANALOGY CARD: Kubernetes cluster = a city. Security = locks on every door,
          camera on every street, guard at every gate]

         [DAY IN THE LIFE — what CKS security controls protect 24/7 for anihpj]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕐 00:00 — Automatic Trivy scan runs                                │
         │     → Detects new CVE in python:3.12 base image                     │
         │     → Grafana dashboard turns RED                                    │
         │     → PagerDuty alert fires (Ch 23, 28, 32)                         │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕖 07:30 — Developer pushes code → opens PR                         │
         │     → GitHub Actions: kube-linter + kubesec + trivy scan            │
         │     → Build FAILS: container runs as root!                           │
         │     → Developer fixes: adds USER 1000 to Dockerfile                  │
         │     → Gates pass → PR merges → cosign signs image (Ch 25, 26)       │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕘 09:15 — New version deployed to staging                          │
         │     → Kyverno admission: image verified ✅ (cosign signature valid)  │
         │     → PSS Restricted: pod is non-root, drop ALL caps ✅              │
         │     → OPA: image from approved registry ✅                            │
         │     → Pod starts successfully (Ch 17, 18, 24)                       │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕚 11:45 — Traffic spikes on jobpost listings                       │
         │     → HPA scales anihpj from 3 to 8 replicas                         │
         │     → Each new pod: same security context, same policies              │
         │     → NetworkPolicy auto-applies (label selector)                    │
         │     → Zero manual intervention needed (Ch 6, 35)                    │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕑 14:20 — Attacker probes the anihpj pod from internet             │
         │     → Attempt 1: SSH to port 22 — blocked by firewall                │
         │     → Attempt 2: Direct HTTP to anihpj:8000 — blocked by NetPol     │
         │     → Attempt 3: CVE exploit via crafted POST — Seccomp blocks it   │
         │     → Falco logs all 3 attempts → security team reviews (Ch 6, 29)  │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕔 17:00 — Audit review catches a mistake                           │
         │     → Intern accidentally ran: kubectl exec anihpj -- bash          │
         │     → Falco alert: "Shell spawned in anihpj container"              │
         │     → Audit log shows: user=intern, verb=pods/exec                  │
         │     → Incident review: no damage, policy reminder sent (Ch 28, 29)  │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🕙 22:00 — Nightly kube-bench CronJob runs                          │
         │     → All checks PASS except 1 (new audit policy not applied)       │
         │     → Alert sent → DevOps will fix in the morning (Ch 4, 32)        │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🛡️  24 hours. Zero breaches. Every layer held. That's CKS.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [TRADITIONAL IT vs KUBERNETES SECURITY — what changes]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TRADITIONAL IT SECURITY           │  KUBERNETES SECURITY               │
         ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  One server = one firewall rule    │  1000 pods = 1000 potential targets│
         │  Static IPs and hostnames          │  Ephemeral pods, rotating IPs      │
         │  Update OS once a month            │  Images rebuilt & deployed daily   │
         │  Users log in via SSH              │  Pods authenticate with SA tokens  │
         │  Audit logs in /var/log/auth.log   │  Audit logs via K8s API policy     │
         │  Firewall blocks at edge           │  NetworkPolicy inside the cluster  │
         │  Manual security reviews           │  Policy-as-code enforced at admit. │
         │  App runs as its own Linux user    │  By default: runs as root! ← 🔴   │
         │  Secret in /etc/app.conf           │  Secret in etcd (unencrypted!) ← 🔴│
         │  Patch = update one binary         │  Patch = rebuild + redeploy image  │
         └────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE COST OF A K8s SECURITY BREACH — why this matters for anihpj]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Scenario: anihpj/jobpost is running on Kubernetes without CKS     │
         │  hardening. An attacker finds a path in.                           │
         │                                                                     │
         │  💸 FINANCIAL IMPACT                                                │
         │     Average cloud data breach cost: $4.45M (IBM 2023 report)       │
         │     Crypto-mining on stolen compute: immediate resource cost        │
         │     GDPR fine for user data leak: up to €20M or 4% annual revenue  │
         │                                                                     │
         │  🏢 BUSINESS IMPACT                                                 │
         │     jobpost user emails exposed → user trust destroyed              │
         │     Company credentials leaked → all cloud resources at risk        │
         │     Service taken offline → job seekers cannot access listings     │
         │                                                                     │
         │  ⏱️  TIME TO DETECT (without CKS controls)                          │
         │     Average: 204 days to DETECT a breach                           │
         │     Average: 73 days to CONTAIN it after detection                 │
         │     With Falco + Audit Logs: minutes to hours ✅                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [KUBERNETES SECURITY EVOLUTION TIMELINE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  2014   K8s released. Security barely considered. No RBAC.          │
         │   │     Anyone with API access = full cluster access.               │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2017   RBAC introduced (v1.6). PodSecurityPolicy added.            │
         │   │     First attempt at admission control.                         │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2018   Tesla breach. Crypto-mining via exposed dashboard.          │
         │   │     Industry wakes up to K8s security risks.                   │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2019   CKS exam created by CNCF. Security becomes a career path.  │
         │   │     NetworkPolicy gets wide adoption.                           │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2021   PodSecurityPolicy DEPRECATED. Audit logging becomes std.    │
         │   │     Supply chain attacks surge (SolarWinds effect on K8s).     │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2022   Sigstore (cosign) reaches v1.0. Image signing goes main.   │
         │   │     Pod Security Standards (PSS) replaces PodSecurityPolicy.   │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2023   eBPF-based security (Cilium, Falco) becomes standard.      │
         │   │     Zero-trust networking widely adopted.                      │
         │   ▼                                                                  │
         │  2024+  SLSA, SBOM, keyless signing = new baseline requirements.   │
         │         AI-assisted threat detection in containers emerging.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WHY K8s SECURITY IS UNIQUELY CHALLENGING]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🏙️ KUBERNETES CLUSTER (your city)                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🧠 Control Plane — the mayor's office                │  │
         │  │  API Server exposed on network → attackers target it  │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🏘️ Worker Nodes — the city blocks                   │  │
         │  │  Pods run as root by default → privilege escalation   │  │
         │  │  Containers share OS kernel → container escape risk   │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🌐 Networking — the city roads                      │  │
         │  │  All pods can talk to each other by default           │  │
         │  │  East-west traffic is unrestricted → lateral movement │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  📦 Images — delivery packages                        │  │
         │  │  Images pulled from internet → malicious code risk   │  │
         │  │  No scanning = you don't know what's inside          │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.2  The 4Cs of Cloud Native Security (The Layered Model)
         - The security model: Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code
         - Each layer builds on the security of the layer below it
         - If the cloud is insecure, cluster security won't save you
         - Every layer must be independently secured
         [DIAGRAM: 4 nested rings — Cloud (outermost) → Cluster → Container → Code]

         [THE 4Cs — NESTED SECURITY LAYERS]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                          │
         │    ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │    │  ☁️  CLOUD                                     │    │
         │    │  IAM policies, VPC security groups, firewall  │    │
         │    │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐    │    │
         │    │   │  ☸️  CLUSTER                          │    │    │
         │    │   │  RBAC, NetworkPolicy, API server sec │    │    │
         │    │   │   ┌───────────────────────────────┐  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │  📦 CONTAINER                 │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │  Image scanning, Seccomp,     │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │  AppArmor, non-root           │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │   ┌──────────────────────┐   │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │   │  💻 CODE              │   │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │   │  SAST, dep. scanning, │   │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │   │  secrets in code, TLS │   │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   │   └──────────────────────┘   │  │    │    │
         │    │   │   └───────────────────────────────┘  │    │    │
         │    │   └──────────────────────────────────────┘    │    │
         │    └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                          │
         │  ⚠️  A breach in an outer layer compromises ALL inner   │
         │      layers — security is only as strong as its weakest │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CKS DOMAIN COVERAGE PER LAYER]
         | Layer     | CKS Topics Covered                              | Domains |
         | Cloud     | IAM, firewall, node OS hardening               | 3       |
         | Cluster   | API server, RBAC, ServiceAccount, NetworkPolicy| 1, 2    |
         | Container | Seccomp, AppArmor, SecurityContext, PSS         | 3, 4    |
         | Code      | Image scanning, signing, supply chain           | 5       |

         [4Cs APPLIED TO anihpj — real examples per layer]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LAYER     │ THREAT EXAMPLE                 │ CKS CONTROL APPLIED   │
         ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
         │ ☁️  Cloud  │ Node EC2 instance has admin    │ Minimal IAM role for  │
         │            │ IAM role → attacker reads all  │ node (S3 read-only)   │
         │            │ S3 buckets including backups   │ NodeRestriction plugin │
         ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
         │ ☸️  Cluster│ anihpj ServiceAccount has      │ RBAC: create minimal  │
         │            │ cluster-admin → attacker reads │ SA with no API perms  │
         │            │ all secrets in all namespaces  │ automountSAToken:false │
         ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
         │ 📦 Container│ anihpj runs as root → CVE     │ runAsNonRoot: true    │
         │            │ in Pillow → write to /etc/     │ readOnlyRootFS: true  │
         │            │ → backdoor persists in pod     │ Seccomp + AppArmor    │
         ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
         │ 💻 Code    │ python:3.10 base has curl CVE  │ Trivy scan in CI/CD   │
         │            │ → remote code execution via    │ Multi-stage build     │
         │            │ crafted HTTP requests          │ cosign image signing  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [SECURITY DECISION FLOWCHART — which 4C layer does this belong to?]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  You identify a security concern. Ask:                              │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Is it about cloud infra (VPC, IAM, firewall)?                      │
         │    YES → ☁️  CLOUD layer → fix at cloud provider level              │
         │    NO  ↓                                                             │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Is it about K8s API, RBAC, nodes, networking, policies?            │
         │    YES → ☸️  CLUSTER layer → fix with RBAC, NetworkPolicy, etc.    │
         │    NO  ↓                                                             │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Is it about how a container runs (user, caps, filesystem)?          │
         │    YES → 📦 CONTAINER layer → fix SecurityContext, Seccomp, AppArmor│
         │    NO  ↓                                                             │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Is it about the software, libraries, or secrets IN the image?      │
         │    YES → 💻 CODE layer → fix with scanning, signing, dep updates    │
         │                                                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.3  Security Principles You Will Apply Throughout This Guide
         - Principle of Least Privilege: grant only what is needed, nothing more
         - Defense in Depth: multiple layers of security; no single point of failure
         - Zero Trust: never assume traffic inside the cluster is safe
         - Immutability: containers should not change after being deployed
         - Audit Everything: know what happened and when, with logs
         [ICON CARDS: 5 cards, one per principle]

         [5 CORE SECURITY PRINCIPLES]
         ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
         │ 🎯 Least     │  │ 🧅 Defense   │  │ 🚫 Zero      │  │ 🧊 Immutab.  │  │ 📋 Audit     │
         │ Privilege    │  │ in Depth     │  │ Trust        │  │              │  │ Everything   │
         │              │  │              │  │              │  │              │  │              │
         │ Grant ONLY   │  │ Many layers, │  │ Trust no     │  │ Containers   │  │ Log every    │
         │ what is      │  │ no single    │  │ pod, user,   │  │ should not   │  │ API call,    │
         │ needed. No   │  │ point of     │  │ or service   │  │ change once  │  │ access, and  │
         │ more, no     │  │ failure.     │  │ by default.  │  │ running.     │  │ anomaly.     │
         │ less.        │  │              │  │ Verify all.  │  │              │  │              │
         └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘

         - The anihpj application context for security:
           • jobpost app serves public job listings — it must not expose secrets
           • Django admin interface — must be restricted by RBAC and NetworkPolicy
           • PostgreSQL database — must not be reachable from the internet
           • Media uploads — must be scanned for malware
           • API keys in settings.py — must be in Secrets, never in code

         [anihpj SECURITY REQUIREMENTS → CKS CONTROLS MAPPING]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  anihpj REQUIREMENT                    │  CKS CONTROL THAT SATISFIES IT       │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  jobpost user data must not be exposed │  Secrets encryption at rest (Ch 19)  │
         │  Django admin on /admin/ restricted    │  RBAC + NetworkPolicy (Ch 6, 9)      │
         │  postgres not reachable from internet  │  NetworkPolicy + Ingress config (Ch 6)│
         │  Media file uploads scanned            │  Image + file scanning, Falco (Ch 23)│
         │  SECRET_KEY never in git/env           │  K8s Secrets + Vault (Ch 19, 20)     │
         │  App must not run as root              │  SecurityContext: runAsNonRoot (Ch 18)│
         │  No unnecessary Linux capabilities     │  capabilities: drop ALL (Ch 18)      │
         │  Container should not modify itself    │  readOnlyRootFilesystem (Ch 18, 31)  │
         │  Images must come from our registry    │  OPA / Kyverno admission (Ch 24)     │
         │  All deployments must be signed        │  cosign + Kyverno verifyImages (Ch 26)│
         │  Know if someone execs into the pod    │  Audit logging + Falco (Ch 28, 29)   │
         │  Pod-to-pod traffic only as needed     │  NetworkPolicy zero-trust (Ch 6)     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

         [5 SECURITY PRINCIPLES → HOW EACH APPLIES TO anihpj DAILY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🎯 LEAST PRIVILEGE                                                   │
         │  anihpj pod's ServiceAccount → no API permissions (automount=false)  │
         │  CI/CD ServiceAccount → can only update Deployments, nothing else    │
         │                                                                       │
         │  🧅 DEFENSE IN DEPTH                                                  │
         │  anihpj has: NetworkPolicy + PSS + SecurityContext + Falco + TLS     │
         │  If NetworkPolicy fails → SecurityContext still prevents root exec    │
         │  If image is vulnerable → Seccomp still blocks dangerous syscalls    │
         │                                                                       │
         │  🚫 ZERO TRUST                                                        │
         │  Default-deny NetworkPolicy in anihpj-prod: nothing in, nothing out  │
         │  Each connection explicitly allowed: ingress→anihpj, anihpj→postgres │
         │                                                                       │
         │  🧊 IMMUTABILITY                                                      │
         │  readOnlyRootFilesystem: true on all anihpj containers               │
         │  Changes go through Git → CI/CD → image rebuild → new pod            │
         │  Attackers cannot persist changes inside a running container         │
         │                                                                       │
         │  📋 AUDIT EVERYTHING                                                  │
         │  Audit policy logs: all secret reads, all pod exec, all deployments  │
         │  Falco rule: alert on any shell in anihpj container                  │
         │  Trivy Operator: continuous CVE scan of running images               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [🧅 DEFENSE IN DEPTH — THE ONION MODEL applied to anihpj security]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  IF AN ATTACKER BREACHES ONE LAYER, THE NEXT LAYER STILL STOPS THEM      │
         │                                                                           │
         │    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
         │    │  LAYER 0: CLOUD / INFRASTRUCTURE                             │     │
         │    │  🔒 VPC + Security Groups + Cloud IAM + Firewall             │     │
         │    │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │     │
         │    │  │  LAYER 1: CLUSTER ACCESS                                │ │     │
         │    │  │  🔒 TLS + mutual auth + RBAC + Audit Logging           │ │     │
         │    │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  LAYER 2: NETWORK SEGMENTATION                   │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  🔒 NetworkPolicy: default-deny + explicit allow │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  LAYER 3: POD ADMISSION                    │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  🔒 PSS Restricted + OPA/Gatekeeper        │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  LAYER 4: CONTAINER HARDENING         │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  🔒 SecurityContext + non-root +      │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │     drop ALL caps + readOnlyRootFS    │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────┐ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  LAYER 5: KERNEL ISOLATION      │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  🔒 Seccomp + AppArmor          │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  LAYER 6: RUNTIME DETECT │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  🔒 Falco + Audit Logs   │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  ┌────────────────────┐ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │  LAYER 7: RESPONSE │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │  🔒 AlertManager +  │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │  PagerDuty + IR    │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │                    │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │  Your anihpj data  │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │  is SAFE here      │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  │  └────────────────────┘ │ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  │  └──────────────────────────┘ │ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  │  └────────────────────────────────┘ │ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │ │     │
         │    │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │     │
         │    │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │     │
         │    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
         │                                                                           │
         │  🎯  KEY INSIGHT: An attacker must breach ALL 7 layers to reach data.    │
         │      If even ONE layer holds, the attack fails. That's defense-in-depth. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [🛡️ BEFORE vs AFTER — anihpj cluster security comparison]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ anihpj BEFORE CKS HARDENING    │  ✅ anihpj AFTER CKS HARDENING       │
         ├────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
         │                                    │                                      │
         │  🔓 API: anonymous=true            │  🔒 API: anonymous=false, RBAC only  │
         │  📋 Audit: OFF                     │  📋 Audit: all requests logged       │
         │  🌐 Network: all pods talk freely  │  🌐 Network: default-deny + explicit │
         │  👤 Container: runs as ROOT        │  👤 Container: UID 1000, non-root   │
         │  ⚡ Capabilities: ALL (38+ caps)   │  ⚡ Capabilities: NONE (drop ALL)    │
         │  🗄️  Secrets: base64 in etcd       │  🗄️  Secrets: AES-256 encrypted      │
         │  🔍 Scanning: NONE (blind deploys) │  🔍 Trivy scan + cosign verify      │
         │  🚨 Alerts: you find out on Twitter│  🦅 Falco + AlertManager = minutes  │
         │  📦 Base image: python:3.11 (1.2GB)│  📦 Multi-stage build (180MB)        │
         │  🐚 Pod exec: anyone can           │  🐚 Pod exec: audit logged + Falco   │
         │  🚪 Lateral move: unrestricted     │  🚪 NetworkPolicy stops lateral moves│
         │  🔄 Updates: manual, ad-hoc        │  🔄 GitOps + auto-scan + auto-sync   │
         │                                    │                                      │
         │  ⚠️  BREACH LIKELIHOOD: HIGH       │  🛡️  BREACH LIKELIHOOD: VERY LOW      │
         └────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 2: The Threat Landscape — What Attackers Target
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    2.1  How Kubernetes Gets Attacked — The Attack Surface
         - Exposed API server: the most common attack vector
         - Misconfigured RBAC: users and pods with too many permissions
         - Vulnerable container images: outdated packages with known CVEs
         - Secrets in plaintext: environment variables, YAML files in Git
         - Privilege escalation: running as root → breaking out of container
         - Lateral movement: one compromised pod → reach other pods/secrets
         [ATTACK MAP DIAGRAM: external attacker → API server → pods → secrets]

         [KUBERNETES ATTACK MAP — common attack paths]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🌐 INTERNET                                                 │
         │       │                                                       │
         │       │  attack vector 1: Exposed API server (no auth)       │
         │       ▼                                                       │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
         │  │  🧠 API SERVER  (port 6443)                         │      │
         │  │  ← attack vector 2: Weak RBAC / stolen credentials │      │
         │  └────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘      │
         │                       │                                       │
         │       ┌───────────────▼────────────────┐                     │
         │       │  ☸️ PODS running on workers      │                     │
         │       │  ← vector 3: Root containers    │                     │
         │       │  ← vector 4: Vulnerable images  │                     │
         │       │  ← vector 5: Mounted secrets    │                     │
         │       │                                 │                     │
         │       │  compromised pod →              │                     │
         │       │    steal ServiceAccount token   │                     │
         │       │    → lateral movement           │                     │
         │       │    → reach other pods/secrets   │                     │
         │       └─────────────────────────────────┘                     │
         │                                                               │
         │  ← vector 6: Malicious images from registry                  │
         │  ← vector 7: Supply chain (compromised base image)           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.2  Real-World Kubernetes Security Incidents
         - Tesla (2018): Kubernetes dashboard exposed → cryptomining
         - Capital One (2019): SSRF attack → IAM role → S3 data breach
         - Shopify (2020): Internal pod → lateral movement → data leak
         - SolarWinds (2020): Supply chain compromise → compromised images

         [4 REAL BREACH CARDS — TIMELINE, ATTACK PATH, LESSON]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🚗 TESLA — 2018                                                    │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  ATTACK: Kubernetes Dashboard exposed publicly, no password          │
         │  PATH:   Internet → dashboard (admin role) → create crypto pod       │
         │          → use AWS credentials found in cluster env vars             │
         │  IMPACT: Compute hijacked for crypto-mining, AWS data at risk        │
         │  FIX:    Ch 5 (secure dashboard) + Ch 9 (RBAC) + Ch 19 (Secrets)   │
         ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  🏦 CAPITAL ONE — 2019                                              │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  ATTACK: SSRF vulnerability in WAF → metadata API on EC2 node       │
         │  PATH:   App pod → cloud metadata API → IAM role credentials        │
         │          → S3 list → download 106M customer records                 │
         │  IMPACT: $80M fine, largest US financial data breach at the time    │
         │  FIX:    Ch 13 (block metadata API) + Ch 16 (restrict IAM roles)   │
         ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  🛍️  SHOPIFY — 2020                                                 │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  ATTACK: Internal rogue employee with pod access                     │
         │  PATH:   Internal access → kubectl exec → lateral movement          │
         │          → read merchant transaction data across namespaces         │
         │  IMPACT: 200 merchants' data exposed, regulatory investigation      │
         │  FIX:    Ch 6 (NetworkPolicy) + Ch 9 (RBAC) + Ch 28 (Audit logs)  │
         ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  🔗 SOLARWINDS — 2020                                               │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  ATTACK: Build system compromised → malicious code injected         │
         │  PATH:   Attacker in CI/CD → modified build → signed update pkg     │
         │          → 18,000 organizations ran trojanized software             │
         │  IMPACT: US government agencies breached via trusted software       │
         │  FIX:    Ch 25 (static analysis) + Ch 26 (cosign) + Ch 27 (SLSA)  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INCIDENT ANALYSIS — Tesla breach walkthrough]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🔍 TESLA BREACH 2018 — Step by Step                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  1. 🌐 Attacker scans internet for open Kubernetes ports    │
         │                                                              │
         │  2. 🎯 Finds Tesla's Kubernetes Dashboard (no password)     │
         │                                                              │
         │  3. 🔑 Dashboard had admin privileges → full cluster access │
         │                                                              │
         │  4. 📦 Attacker deploys crypto-mining containers           │
         │                                                              │
         │  5. 🔒 AWS credentials found in cluster → data access       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 LESSONS:                                                 │
         │     ✅ Never expose dashboard without authentication         │
         │     ✅ Use NetworkPolicy to restrict dashboard access        │
         │     ✅ Never give service accounts admin permissions         │
         │     ✅ Rotate and restrict cloud provider credentials        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.3  Kubernetes Security Threat Model (STRIDE)
         - S: Spoofing — impersonating a user or service account
         - T: Tampering — modifying pod specs or cluster configs
         - R: Repudiation — actions without audit trails
         - I: Information Disclosure — secrets leaked from etcd or env vars
         - D: Denial of Service — resource exhaustion attacks on nodes
         - E: Elevation of Privilege — breaking out of container to node
         [TABLE: STRIDE threat × K8s mitigation matrix]

         [STRIDE × CKS COVERAGE TABLE]
         | Threat                | K8s Attack Example              | CKS Mitigation                   |
         | Spoofing              | Fake service account token      | RBAC, ServiceAccount tokens       |
         | Tampering             | Modify etcd directly            | etcd encryption, RBAC             |
         | Repudiation           | No logs of API calls            | Audit logging                     |
         | Info Disclosure       | Secrets in plaintext env vars   | Encryption at rest, Vault         |
         | Denial of Service     | Unlimited CPU containers         | ResourceQuota, LimitRange         |
         | Elevation of Privilege| Root container → node escape    | Seccomp, AppArmor, PSS, non-root  |

         [ATTACK TECHNIQUE SEVERITY MATRIX — for anihpj]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                  LOW COMPLEXITY ←──────────────→ HIGH COMPLEXITY   │
         │                                                                      │
         │  HIGH     │ Exposed dashboard   │ Secrets in git    │               │
         │  IMPACT   │ (no auth)           │ (plaintext)       │               │
         │     ↑     │ ← CRITICAL RISK     │ ← CRITICAL RISK   │               │
         │     │     │─────────────────────│───────────────────│               │
         │     │     │ Root container      │ Supply chain      │               │
         │     │     │ (privilege escl.)   │ compromise        │               │
         │     │     │ ← HIGH RISK         │ ← HIGH RISK       │               │
         │     │     │─────────────────────│───────────────────│               │
         │  LOW      │ Missing resource    │ Insecure TLS      │               │
         │  IMPACT   │ limits (DoS)        │ cipher suites     │               │
         │           │ ← MEDIUM RISK       │ ← LOW RISK        │               │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [MITRE ATT&CK FOR KUBERNETES — tactics used against K8s clusters]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TACTIC              │ TECHNIQUE EXAMPLE               │ CKS FIX  │
         ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
         │  Initial Access      │ Exposed API/dashboard, SSRF     │ Ch 4,5,8 │
         │  Execution           │ kubectl exec, malicious image   │ Ch 18,29 │
         │  Persistence         │ Backdoor cron, new SA binding   │ Ch 9,28  │
         │  Privilege Escalation│ hostPath mount, root container  │ Ch 17,18 │
         │  Defense Evasion     │ Delete audit logs, clear Falco  │ Ch 28,29 │
         │  Credential Access   │ Read mounted SA token, secrets  │ Ch 10,19 │
         │  Discovery           │ kubectl get all --all-namespaces│ Ch 9,11  │
         │  Lateral Movement    │ Reach other pods via flat net   │ Ch 6     │
         │  Collection          │ Read etcd secrets directly      │ Ch 19,16 │
         │  Exfiltration        │ Copy data via pod egress        │ Ch 6,29  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.4  The anihpj/jobpost Security Threat Scenario
         - Scenario: anihpj is running in Kubernetes, serving job listings globally
         - What attackers might want:
           • User email addresses from the User model
           • Company data from the JobPost model
           • Database credentials (postgres password)
           • Django SECRET_KEY from settings.py
           • Access to cloud storage (media files)
         - What would happen without CKS knowledge:
           • Pod running as root → container escape possible
           • All secrets in environment variables → readable by anyone in namespace
           • No NetworkPolicy → database reachable from any pod
           • No image scanning → vulnerable packages deployed silently
           • No audit logging → attacker leaves no trace
         [THREAT MAP: anihpj architecture + attack vectors highlighted in red]

         [KUBERNETES SECURITY CASTLE — Defense-in-Depth Analogy]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                   │
         │  🏰 THE KUBERNETES SECURITY CASTLE — Your anihpj Fortress        │
         │                                                                   │
         │              🌐 INTERNET (the moat)                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │  🧱 WALL 1 — Edge Security (TLS, WAF, Ingress Rules)        ││
         │  │     "Only HTTPS traffic reaches the castle gate"            ││
         │  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ││
         │  │     │  🛡️ WALL 2 — API Server Security                  │ ││
         │  │     │  "Every request authenticated + authorized"       │ ││
         │  │     │  (RBAC, AuthN, Admission Control)                 │ ││
         │  │     │     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐  │ ││
         │  │     │     │  🔒 WALL 3 — Network Security            │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │  "Pods can only talk to approved others" │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │  (NetworkPolicy, mTLS, Istio)            │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     ┌────────────────────────────────┐   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │  🧊 WALL 4 — Container Security│   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │  "Containers run locked down"   │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │  (non-root, Seccomp, AppArmor,  │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │   read-only FS, drop ALL caps)  │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     ┌──────────────────────┐    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     │ 🗄️ WALL 5 — Data     │    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     │   "Secrets encrypted, │    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     │    DB isolated,       │    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     │    user data safe"    │    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     │   (etcd encrypt, PVC) │    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     │     └──────────────────────┘    │   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     │     └────────────────────────────────┘   │  │ ││
         │  │     │     └──────────────────────────────────────────┘  │ ││
         │  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ││
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                                   │
         │  🔭 WATCHTOWERS: Falco (runtime) + Audit Logs (API) + Trivy      │
         │     "Every wall is watched — any breach is detected instantly"   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ATTACKER KILL CHAIN vs CKS DEFENSES — Lateral Movement Blocked]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  🔴 ATTACKER STAGE         🛡️ CKS DEFENSE (stops them here)        │
         │                                                                      │
         │  1. RECONNAISSANCE                                                    │
         │     Port scan cluster IPs    → Firewall: no ports exposed           │
         │     Enumerate API endpoints  → --anonymous-auth=false               │
         │     Access Dashboard URL     → NetworkPolicy: only admin IPs        │
         │                                       │                               │
         │                                       ▼                               │
         │  2. INITIAL ACCESS                                                     │
         │     Exploit app CVE          → Seccomp: dangerous syscalls blocked   │
         │     Stolen kubectl cert      → RBAC: limited role, not cluster-admin │
         │     Compromised CI/CD token  → SA: automount=false, no API perms    │
         │                                       │                               │
         │                                       ▼                               │
         │  3. EXECUTION                                                          │
         │     kubectl exec into pod    → Audit log: pod/exec recorded           │
         │     Spawn shell in container → Falco: shell detected, alert fired     │
         │     Write backdoor to /etc/  → readOnlyRootFilesystem: write fails   │
         │                                       │                               │
         │                                       ▼                               │
         │  4. PERSISTENCE                                                         │
         │     Create CronJob (backdoor)→ RBAC: SA can't create CronJobs         │
         │     Add new ServiceAccount   → RBAC: SA can't create ServiceAccounts  │
         │     Modify existing pod spec → PodSecurity: restricted blocks changes │
         │                                       │                               │
         │                                       ▼                               │
         │  5. LATERAL MOVEMENT                                                    │
         │     Reach postgres (5432)    → NetworkPolicy: only anihpj allowed     │
         │     Read secrets from etcd   → Encryption at rest: secrets encrypted  │
         │     Access other namespaces  → NetworkPolicy: namespace isolation     │
         │     Impersonate SA token     → Bound token: audience mismatch         │
         │                                       │                               │
         │                                       ▼                               │
         │  6. EXFILTRATION                                                        │
         │     curl to external IP      → NetworkPolicy egress: blocked          │
         │     DNS tunneling (slow)     → CoreDNS monitoring: anomaly detected   │
         │     Copy data to /media/     → AppArmor: path write denied            │
         │                                                                      │
         │  🏁 RESULT: Attacker blocked at EVERY stage. Zero data lost.         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [anihpj SECURITY THREAT MAP]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  🌐 Internet → 🛡️ Ingress (TLS termination)                │
         │                      │                                       │
         │             ┌────────▼────────┐                             │
         │             │  📦 anihpj Pod  │ ← 🔴 runs as root!         │
         │             │  Django/jobpost │ ← 🔴 SECRET_KEY in env!    │
         │             │  port 8000      │ ← 🔴 no seccomp profile!   │
         │             └────────┬────────┘                             │
         │                      │                                       │
         │             ┌────────▼────────┐                             │
         │             │  🗄️ PostgreSQL  │ ← 🔴 reachable by all pods │
         │             │  port 5432      │ ← 🔴 root DB user!         │
         │             └─────────────────┘                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  After CKS hardening:                                        │
         │  ✅ Ingress TLS + no plaintext HTTP                         │
         │  ✅ anihpj pod: non-root, read-only fs, seccomp             │
         │  ✅ Secrets encrypted at rest in etcd                       │
         │  ✅ NetworkPolicy: only anihpj can reach postgres           │
         │  ✅ Image scanned, signed, and verified before deploy       │
         │  ✅ Falco monitoring all runtime behaviour                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 3: Security Architecture Overview — The CKS Mental Model
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    3.1  How CKS Security Maps to Kubernetes Components
         - API Server: central enforcement point for all access control
         - etcd: stores secrets — must be encrypted and access-restricted
         - kubelet: runs pods — must be hardened and have restricted API
         - Container runtime: executes containers — Seccomp/AppArmor apply here
         - kube-proxy / CNI: networking — NetworkPolicies enforced here
         [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: K8s components with security layer annotations]

         [KUBERNETES SECURITY ARCHITECTURE — components + controls]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🔐 CONTROL PLANE                                              │
         │                                                                │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🌐 kube-apiserver (port 6443)                          │  │
         │  │  Security controls:                                     │  │
         │  │    → Authentication (certs, tokens, OIDC)               │  │
         │  │    → Authorization (RBAC, Node, Webhook)                │  │
         │  │    → Admission Control (OPA, PSS, ImagePolicy)          │  │
         │  │    → Audit Logging                                      │  │
         │  └────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                           │                                    │
         │  ┌────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🗄️ etcd                                                │  │
         │  │  Security controls:                                     │  │
         │  │    → Encryption at rest (--encryption-provider-config)  │  │
         │  │    → TLS between API server and etcd                    │  │
         │  │    → Firewall: only API server can reach etcd port 2379 │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                                │
         │  🔐 WORKER NODES                                               │
         │                                                                │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  ⚙️ kubelet (port 10250)                                │  │
         │  │  Security controls:                                     │  │
         │  │    → Anonymous auth disabled                            │  │
         │  │    → Webhook authorization mode                         │  │
         │  │    → NodeRestriction admission plugin                   │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                                │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  📦 Container Runtime (containerd/CRI-O)                │  │
         │  │  Security controls:                                     │  │
         │  │    → Seccomp profiles (filter syscalls)                 │  │
         │  │    → AppArmor profiles (restrict file/network access)   │  │
         │  │    → Sandboxed runtimes (gVisor for untrusted workloads)│  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.2  Authentication vs Authorization vs Admission Control
         - Authentication: "Who are you?" → certificates, tokens, OIDC
         - Authorization: "What can you do?" → RBAC, ABAC, Webhook
         - Admission Control: "Is this request allowed?" → policy enforcement
         - All 3 must be configured correctly for a secure cluster
         [PIPELINE DIAGRAM: Request → Authn → Authz → Admission → etcd]

         [THE KUBERNETES REQUEST PIPELINE]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  kubectl apply -f anihpj-pod.yaml                               │
         │       │                                                          │
         │       ▼                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  1. AUTHENTICATION — "Who are you?"                      │  │
         │  │    → Certificate: kubectl uses client cert from kubeconfig│  │
         │  │    → Token: ServiceAccount JWT token                      │  │
         │  │    → OIDC: external identity provider (GitHub, Google)   │  │
         │  │    ❌ Not authenticated? → 401 Unauthorized               │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                 │ ✅ Authenticated              │
         │                                 ▼                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  2. AUTHORIZATION — "What can you do?"                   │  │
         │  │    → RBAC: does this user have a Role/ClusterRole        │  │
         │  │      that allows 'create pods' in this namespace?        │  │
         │  │    ❌ Not authorized? → 403 Forbidden                    │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                 │ ✅ Authorized                 │
         │                                 ▼                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  3. ADMISSION CONTROL — "Is this spec allowed?"          │  │
         │  │    → Pod Security Standards: does it violate policies?   │  │
         │  │    → OPA / Kyverno: custom business rules                │  │
         │  │    → ImagePolicyWebhook: is the image from trusted repo? │  │
         │  │    ❌ Rejected by admission? → 403 with policy reason    │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                 │ ✅ Admitted                   │
         │                                 ▼                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  4. STORED IN etcd + Scheduled to node                   │  │
         │  │    → Kubernetes creates the pod on a worker node         │  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.3  Your Security Toolkit — Tools You Will Use Throughout This Guide
         - kubectl: inspect, patch, apply security resources
         - kube-bench: check cluster against CIS benchmarks
         - Trivy: scan container images for CVEs
         - Falco: detect runtime anomalies in real-time
         - OPA / Gatekeeper: policy-as-code for admission control
         - cosign: sign and verify container images
         - AppArmor / Seccomp: OS-level container sandboxing
         [TOOL CARD GRID: one card per tool with logo, purpose, command example]

         [CKS SECURITY TOOLKIT — 8 tools you must master]
         ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
         │ 🔧 kube-bench │  │ 🔍 Trivy     │  │ 🦅 Falco     │  │ 📋 OPA/Gtkpr │
         │ CIS benchmark │  │ Image CVE    │  │ Runtime      │  │ Policy-as-   │
         │ checker       │  │ scanner      │  │ detection    │  │ code for K8s │
         │               │  │              │  │              │  │              │
         │ kube-bench    │  │ trivy image  │  │ systemctl    │  │ kubectl apply│
         │   --check all │  │   anihpj:v1  │  │ start falco  │  │   -f policy  │
         └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘
         ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
         │ 🔏 cosign    │  │ 🛡️ AppArmor  │  │ 🔒 Seccomp   │  │ 🔑 Vault     │
         │ Image signing │  │ Application  │  │ Syscall      │  │ External     │
         │ and verify   │  │ sandboxing   │  │ filtering    │  │ secret mgmt  │
         │               │  │              │  │              │  │              │
         │ cosign verify │  │ aa-status    │  │ seccomp      │  │ vault kv     │
         │   image:tag   │  │              │  │   profile    │  │ get secret   │
         └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘

         [TOOLKIT DETAILED COMPARISON — when to use which tool]
         ┌─────────────┬────────────┬──────────────────────┬───────────┬────────────┐
         │ Tool        │ When?      │ What it detects       │ Open Src? │ CKS Exam?  │
         ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────┤
         │ kube-bench  │ Setup time │ CIS benchmark fails  │ ✅ Yes    │ ✅ Know it  │
         │ Trivy       │ CI/CD +    │ CVEs in images,      │ ✅ Yes    │ ✅ Know it  │
         │             │ runtime    │ misconfigs, secrets   │           │            │
         │ Falco       │ Runtime    │ Syscall anomalies,   │ ✅ Yes    │ ✅ Must know│
         │             │ (live)     │ shell in container    │           │            │
         │ OPA/Gkpr    │ Admission  │ Policy violations    │ ✅ Yes    │ ✅ Know it  │
         │ Kyverno     │ Admission  │ Policy violations    │ ✅ Yes    │ ✅ Know it  │
         │ cosign      │ CI/CD +    │ Unsigned images      │ ✅ Yes    │ ✅ Know it  │
         │             │ admission  │                       │           │            │
         │ AppArmor    │ Runtime    │ File/net/cap abuse   │ ✅ Linux  │ ✅ Must know│
         │ Seccomp     │ Runtime    │ Dangerous syscalls   │ ✅ Kernel │ ✅ Must know│
         │ Vault       │ Runtime    │ Secret management    │ ✅ Yes    │ ⚠️ Aware   │
         │ kube-linter │ Dev time   │ YAML misconfigs      │ ✅ Yes    │ ⚠️ Aware   │
         │ kubesec     │ Dev time   │ Security score/risks │ ✅ Yes    │ ⚠️ Aware   │
         └─────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────────┴───────────┴────────────┘

         [SECURITY TOOL DECISION FLOWCHART — which tool for which task?]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  WHAT ARE YOU TRYING TO DO?                                         │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Check if my cluster passes security standards?                     │
         │    → Use: kube-bench                                                │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Find vulnerabilities in my container image?                        │
         │    → Use: trivy image <image-name>                                  │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Detect suspicious activity inside a running container?             │
         │    → Use: Falco (runtime rules)                                     │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Prevent bad configs from reaching the cluster at all?              │
         │    → Use: OPA Gatekeeper or Kyverno (admission webhooks)            │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Prove my image was built by my CI pipeline and not tampered?       │
         │    → Use: cosign sign + cosign verify                               │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Restrict what a container can do at the OS level?                  │
         │    → AppArmor (file/network) or Seccomp (syscalls) or BOTH         │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Store secrets securely outside Kubernetes?                         │
         │    → Use: HashiCorp Vault + External Secrets Operator              │
         │                                                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [COMPONENT → SECURITY RESPONSIBILITY MATRIX — who owns what]
         ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  K8s COMPONENT         │  SECURITY RESPONSIBILITY                    │
         ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  kube-apiserver        │  AuthN, AuthZ, Admission, Audit, TLS        │
         │  etcd                  │  Encryption at rest, access restriction      │
         │  kubelet               │  Anonymous auth off, webhook authz, certs    │
         │  kube-scheduler        │  TLS, minimal permissions (least privilege)  │
         │  kube-controller-mgr   │  TLS, service account key rotation          │
         │  Container runtime     │  Seccomp, AppArmor, namespace isolation      │
         │  CNI plugin            │  NetworkPolicy enforcement                   │
         │  CoreDNS               │  DNS security, restrict external queries     │
         │  Ingress controller    │  TLS termination, rate limiting, WAF         │
         │  Image registry        │  Private, authenticated, scanned images      │
         └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.4  The Complete API Request Lifecycle — The Five Security Gates
         - EVERY request to Kubernetes (every kubectl, every controller, every pod
           calling the API) passes through the SAME five gates, in this exact order.
           This is the single most important mental model in the whole CKS exam —
           when a request is blocked, knowing WHICH gate stopped it tells you
           exactly where to look.
         - This guide uses ONE canonical numbering everywhere: Gate 1 TLS →
           Gate 2 Authentication → Gate 3 Authorization → Gate 4 Admission
           (mutating then validating) → then persistence to etcd.
         - The error code is a map: TLS error = Gate 1, 401 = Gate 2, 403 (with an
           RBAC message) = Gate 3, 403/error (with a policy/webhook message) =
           Gate 4. Read the message, jump straight to the right chapter.

         [THE 5 GATES — a request's journey from kubectl to etcd]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  kubectl create -f anihpj-pod.yaml                                     │
         │        │                                                               │
         │        ▼                                                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ GATE 1 — TLS (transport)          "Is the channel encrypted?"    │ │
         │  │   client cert + server cert, mutual TLS to the API endpoint      │ │
         │  │   ❌ fail → TLS handshake error (wrong CA / expired cert)        │ │
         │  └───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │        ✅                         ▼                                     │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ GATE 2 — AUTHENTICATION           "WHO are you?"                  │ │
         │  │   client certs · bearer/SA tokens · OIDC · webhook               │ │
         │  │   ❌ fail → 401 Unauthorized   (fix: kubeconfig / token) Ch 8    │ │
         │  └───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │        ✅                         ▼                                     │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ GATE 3 — AUTHORIZATION            "Are you ALLOWED to?"           │ │
         │  │   RBAC (Node, RBAC, Webhook modes) — verb + resource + ns        │ │
         │  │   ❌ fail → 403 Forbidden      (fix: Role/Binding)  Ch 9         │ │
         │  └───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │        ✅                         ▼                                     │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ GATE 4 — ADMISSION CONTROL        "Is this SPEC acceptable?"      │ │
         │  │   4a MUTATING first  → may REWRITE the object                    │ │
         │  │      (inject sidecar, add defaults, set securityContext)         │ │
         │  │   4b VALIDATING next → may only ACCEPT or REJECT                 │ │
         │  │      (PSS, OPA/Gatekeeper, Kyverno, ImagePolicyWebhook)          │ │
         │  │   ❌ fail → 403 with policy reason   Ch 17, 24                   │ │
         │  └───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │        ✅                         ▼                                     │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ PERSISTED → etcd (encrypted at rest) → scheduler → kubelet → run │ │
         │  │   Audit logging records the whole journey (Ch 28)               │ │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [GATE REFERENCE — question, mechanism, failure signal, where to fix]
         ┌──────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────┬────────┐
         │ GATE │ QUESTION         │ MECHANISM                │ FAILURE      │ CHAPTER│
         ├──────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────┤
         │ 1 TLS│ channel secure?  │ client/server certs, mTLS│ TLS error    │ 7, 8   │
         │ 2 N  │ who are you?     │ certs, tokens, OIDC      │ 401          │ 8      │
         │ 3 Z  │ allowed to?      │ RBAC / Node / Webhook    │ 403 (rbac)   │ 9, 11  │
         │ 4a M │ rewrite spec?    │ mutating webhooks        │ (silent edit)│ 24     │
         │ 4b V │ spec acceptable? │ PSS, OPA, Kyverno, IPW   │ 403 (policy) │ 17, 24 │
         │ etcd │ store it         │ encryption at rest       │ —            │ 19     │
         └──────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────┴────────┘

         MUTATING vs VALIDATING — why the order matters:
         - MUTATING admission runs FIRST and can CHANGE the object (add a sidecar,
           set a default securityContext, inject labels). If it changed something,
           the API server re-runs the object schema check.
         - VALIDATING admission runs SECOND and can only say YES or NO — it never
           edits. This ordering is deliberate: you mutate to a compliant shape,
           THEN validate that the final shape is acceptable.
         - A controller can be BOTH (e.g., Kyverno mutate + validate rules). On the
           exam: if a pod gets extra fields you didn't write, a mutating webhook did
           it; if it's rejected with a policy message, a validating one did it.

         [WORKED EXAMPLE — an anihpj pod through all five gates]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. TLS   → kubectl's client cert is signed by the cluster CA ✅      │
         │  2. AuthN → cert CN=jane maps to user "jane" ✅                       │
         │  3. AuthZ → RoleBinding lets jane create pods in anihpj-prod ✅       │
         │  4a Mut.  → Kyverno injects runAsNonRoot + drops all caps (defaults)  │
         │  4b Val.  → PSS Restricted checks the (now-mutated) pod → compliant ✅│
         │  5. etcd  → pod object stored (Secrets it references are encrypted)   │
         │             → scheduled → kubelet pulls signed image → container runs │
         │  ► Audit log has one entry per stage: who, what, verb, decision       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

CKS Domain 1: 15% of exam
