Chapter 4: CIS Benchmarks & kube-bench — Measuring Your Security Baseline
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    4.1  What Are CIS Benchmarks?
         - CIS = Center for Internet Security
         - CIS Kubernetes Benchmark: a list of security configuration checks
         - Over 200 checks covering API server, etcd, kubelet, nodes, policies
         - Industry standard for measuring "how secure is my cluster?"
         - Every CKS exam question on hardening traces back to a CIS check
         [INFO CARD: CIS Benchmark website, download link, version history]

         [CIS BENCHMARK CATEGORIES — what gets checked]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CIS KUBERNETES BENCHMARK CHECK AREAS                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  1. Control Plane Components                                │
         │     → kube-apiserver flags (auth, admission, TLS)           │
         │     → kube-controller-manager flags                         │
         │     → kube-scheduler flags                                  │
         │                                                              │
         │  2. etcd                                                    │
         │     → TLS configuration                                     │
         │     → File permissions                                      │
         │     → Client authentication                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  3. Control Plane Configuration                             │
         │     → Audit logs enabled                                    │
         │     → Encryption at rest                                    │
         │     → Admission plugins enabled                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  4. Worker Nodes                                            │
         │     → kubelet configuration                                 │
         │     → Node OS hardening                                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  5. Kubernetes Policies                                     │
         │     → NetworkPolicy, RBAC, Secrets, Pod Security           │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.2  Running kube-bench — Your Security Audit Tool
         - kube-bench automates CIS Benchmark checks
         - Run on control plane nodes and worker nodes separately
         - Output: PASS / FAIL / WARN with remediation advice
         - FAIL = something to fix; WARN = check if applicable
         - On the CKS exam: kube-bench output tells you what to fix
         [CODE: installing and running kube-bench]
         [CODE: reading kube-bench output — how to interpret FAIL/WARN]

         [kube-bench OUTPUT EXAMPLE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ kube-bench --check 1.2                                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  [INFO] 1 Master Node Security Configuration                │
         │  [INFO] 1.2 API Server                                      │
         │  [PASS] 1.2.1 Ensure --anonymous-auth=false                 │
         │  [FAIL] 1.2.2 Ensure --token-auth-file is not set          │
         │  [WARN] 1.2.6 Ensure --authorization-mode includes RBAC    │
         │  [PASS] 1.2.9 Ensure --enable-admission-plugins includes   │
         │                NodeRestriction                              │
         │                                                              │
         │  == Remediations master ==                                  │
         │  1.2.2 Edit the API server pod specification file at        │
         │        /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml        │
         │        and remove the --token-auth-file parameter           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.3  Fixing Common CIS Failures — The API Server Manifest
         - The API server is configured via a static pod manifest
         - Location: /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml
         - Changes take effect immediately (kubelet restarts the static pod)
         - Most CKS fixes = adding/removing flags from this manifest
         [CODE: kube-apiserver.yaml — secure configuration example]
         [TABLE: CIS check → failing flag → fix to apply]

         [CRITICAL API SERVER FLAGS — CIS MUST-PASS CHECKS]
         | CIS Check | Flag                                | Secure Value               |
         | 1.2.1     | --anonymous-auth                    | false                      |
         | 1.2.6     | --authorization-mode                | Node,RBAC (not AlwaysAllow)|
         | 1.2.7     | --admission-control-config-file     | set to audit policy        |
         | 1.2.9     | --enable-admission-plugins          | NodeRestriction included   |
         | 1.2.14    | --audit-log-path                    | /var/log/kubernetes/audit  |
         | 1.2.20    | --tls-cert-file / --tls-private-key | valid cert paths           |
         | 1.2.22    | --audit-policy-file                 | path to audit policy YAML  |

    4.4  kubelet Hardening — CIS Node Checks
         - The kubelet runs on every node and manages pod lifecycle
         - Insecure kubelet = attacker can control pods on that node
         - kubelet is configured via /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml
         - Key hardening: disable anonymous auth, enable webhook auth
         [CODE: kubelet config.yaml — hardened configuration]
         [CODE: Applying changes to kubelet and restarting]

         [KUBELET HARDENING — before vs after]
         ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ INSECURE KUBELET         │   │  ✅ HARDENED KUBELET         │
         │                              │   │                              │
         │  authentication:             │   │  authentication:             │
         │    anonymous:                │   │    anonymous:                │
         │      enabled: true  ← 🔴    │   │      enabled: false  ✅      │
         │    webhook:                  │   │    webhook:                  │
         │      enabled: false ← 🔴    │   │      enabled: true   ✅      │
         │                              │   │                              │
         │  authorization:              │   │  authorization:              │
         │    mode: AlwaysAllow ← 🔴   │   │    mode: Webhook     ✅      │
         │                              │   │                              │
         │  readOnlyPort: 10255 ← 🔴   │   │  readOnlyPort: 0     ✅      │
         └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

    4.5  Applying CIS Benchmark Fixes — Hands-On with anihpj
         - Scenario: your anihpj cluster fails 12 CIS checks
         - Walk through top 5 most impactful fixes
         - Verifying each fix with kube-bench re-run
         [STEP-BY-STEP LAB: kube-bench → identify fails → fix → re-run → verify]

         [CIS FIX WORKFLOW — the cycle you repeat for every FAIL]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  1. RUN kube-bench                                                  │
         │     $ kube-bench run --targets=master,node                          │
         │     → Produces: PASS ✅ | FAIL ❌ | WARN ⚠️                         │
         │                         │                                            │
         │                         ▼                                            │
         │  2. READ the FAIL item + remediation advice                         │
         │     [FAIL] 1.2.2 Ensure --token-auth-file is not set               │
         │     Remediation: Edit kube-apiserver.yaml,                          │
         │     remove --token-auth-file parameter                              │
         │                         │                                            │
         │                         ▼                                            │
         │  3. LOCATE the file to edit                                         │
         │     $ vi /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml              │
         │     (or /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml for kubelet items)             │
         │                         │                                            │
         │                         ▼                                            │
         │  4. APPLY the fix (add/remove the flag)                             │
         │     Static pods: kubelet auto-restarts after YAML save              │
         │     kubelet: systemctl restart kubelet                              │
         │                         │                                            │
         │                         ▼                                            │
         │  5. VERIFY the fix                                                  │
         │     $ kube-bench run --check 1.2.2                                 │
         │     → Should now show: [PASS] 1.2.2 ✅                             │
         │                                                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [TOP 5 HIGHEST-IMPACT CIS FIXES FOR anihpj CLUSTER]
         ┌────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ #  │ FIX                                                            │
         ├────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 1  │ --anonymous-auth=false on API server                          │
         │    │ Impact: prevents unauthenticated API access                    │
         │    │ File: /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml           │
         ├────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 2  │ Enable audit logging (--audit-policy-file, --audit-log-path)  │
         │    │ Impact: creates forensic record of all API activity            │
         │    │ File: kube-apiserver.yaml + create audit-policy.yaml          │
         ├────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 3  │ Disable kubelet read-only port: readOnlyPort: 0              │
         │    │ Impact: prevents unauthenticated kubelet data reads           │
         │    │ File: /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml                            │
         ├────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 4  │ Enable NodeRestriction admission plugin                       │
         │    │ Impact: limits what compromised kubelets can do               │
         │    │ File: kube-apiserver.yaml --enable-admission-plugins=...      │
         ├────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 5  │ Enable encryption at rest for Secrets                         │
         │    │ Impact: secrets unreadable even if etcd is accessed directly  │
         │    │ File: create EncryptionConfiguration + add API server flag    │
         └────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.6  Top 10 CIS Failures — Exact Exam Fixes
         - The same CIS findings appear on almost every kube-bench run — memorize
           the exact fix for each; the exam task is literally "fix the FAILs"
         - Every fix is one of three kinds: an API server flag, a kubelet config
           field, or a file permission/ownership change
         - Golden habit: after each fix, re-run the single check to confirm PASS:
           kube-bench run --targets=master --check=<id>
         - API server flag changes auto-restart the static pod — expect kubectl
           to drop for 30-60s (normal, don't panic)

         [TOP 10 CIS FAILURES → EXACT FIX — the exam answer key]
         ┌────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ #  │ FAILING CHECK                       │ EXACT FIX                           │
         ├────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 1  │ API: anonymous requests enabled     │ kube-apiserver.yaml:                │
         │    │                                     │ --anonymous-auth=false              │
         │ 2  │ API: authorization-mode includes    │ --authorization-mode=Node,RBAC      │
         │    │ AlwaysAllow                         │ (never AlwaysAllow)                 │
         │ 3  │ API: profiling enabled              │ --profiling=false                   │
         │ 4  │ API: no audit logging               │ --audit-log-path=/var/log/kubernetes│
         │    │                                     │ /audit.log + --audit-policy-file    │
         │ 5  │ API: admission plugins missing      │ --enable-admission-plugins=         │
         │    │ NodeRestriction                     │ NodeRestriction,...                 │
         │ 6  │ Kubelet: anonymous auth enabled     │ /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml:       │
         │    │                                     │ authentication.anonymous.enabled:   │
         │    │                                     │ false                               │
         │ 7  │ Kubelet: authorization mode         │ authorization.mode: Webhook         │
         │    │ AlwaysAllow                         │ (kubelet asks the API server)       │
         │ 8  │ Kubelet: read-only port open        │ readOnlyPort: 0                     │
         │ 9  │ etcd: data dir / certs perms too    │ chmod 700 /var/lib/etcd             │
         │    │ open, wrong owner                   │ chown etcd:etcd /var/lib/etcd       │
         │ 10 │ Kubelet: kernel defaults not        │ protectKernelDefaults: true         │
         │    │ protected                           │ (config.yaml) then restart kubelet  │
         └────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘
         [CODE: kube-bench run --targets=master,node — before/after comparison for anihpj cluster]
         [CODE: systemctl restart kubelet + verifying with systemctl status kubelet]


Chapter 5: Securing the Kubernetes Dashboard & Web UIs
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    5.1  Why the Dashboard Is Dangerous
         - The Tesla breach started with an exposed Kubernetes Dashboard
         - Dashboard gives visual access to pods, secrets, exec shell
         - Never expose it to the public internet without authentication
         - Best practice: use kubectl proxy or VPN only
         [WARNING CARD: Dashboard security — common attack vector]

         [DASHBOARD EXPOSURE RISK MATRIX]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  HOW IS DASHBOARD EXPOSED?   → RISK LEVEL → WHAT ATTACKER CAN DO │
         ├──────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────┤
         │  Public Internet, no auth    │ 🔴 CRITICAL  │ Full cluster access │
         │  (LoadBalancer, NodePort)    │              │ Like Tesla breach   │
         ├──────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────┤
         │  Public Internet, with token │ 🟠 HIGH      │ If token stolen:    │
         │  (no extra network controls) │              │ full access too     │
         ├──────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────┤
         │  Internal only, with RBAC    │ 🟡 MEDIUM    │ Limited if RBAC is  │
         │  limited to read-only SA     │              │ correctly scoped    │
         ├──────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────┤
         │  kubectl proxy only (local)  │ 🟢 LOW       │ Only the person     │
         │  + strong RBAC + no public   │              │ running proxy can   │
         │  exposure at all             │              │ access it           │
         └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────────────┘

    5.2  Deploying Dashboard Securely
         - Install with limited RBAC (read-only by default)
         - Enable token-based authentication
         - Use kubectl port-forward instead of NodePort/LoadBalancer
         - Restrict access via NetworkPolicy to only internal admin IPs
         [CODE: Secure dashboard deployment YAML]
         [CODE: Creating a restricted ServiceAccount for dashboard]

         [SECURE DASHBOARD ACCESS — the correct workflow]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ INSECURE (never do this):                                        │
         │  kubectl expose deploy kubernetes-dashboard                           │
         │    --type=LoadBalancer --port=443                                    │
         │  → Exposes dashboard on public IP, anyone can reach it              │
         │                                                                       │
         │  ✅ SECURE (the right way):                                           │
         │  Step 1: Dashboard only has ClusterIP (internal only)               │
         │  Step 2: kubectl port-forward -n kubernetes-dashboard svc/... 8443  │
         │  Step 3: Access at https://localhost:8443 only                      │
         │  Step 4: Login with a scoped SA token (not admin token)             │
         │  Step 5: NetworkPolicy denies all external dashboard traffic        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.3  Removing Unnecessary Components
         - CIS Benchmark: remove unused components and ports
         - Dashboard itself may not be needed — assess before installing
         - Disable alpha features and insecure ports
         - Remove: unsafe capabilities, hostNetwork, hostPID usage

         [PRE-PRODUCTION CLUSTER COMPONENT AUDIT CHECKLIST]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  □ Is Kubernetes Dashboard installed?                                │
         │       If yes → is it only accessible via port-forward?              │
         │  □ Are there any services of type LoadBalancer that shouldn't be?   │
         │  □ Are there any services of type NodePort exposing internal apps?  │
         │  □ Is --insecure-port=0 set on the API server?                      │
         │  □ Is the kubelet read-only port (10255) disabled?                  │
         │  □ Are any pods using hostNetwork: true unnecessarily?              │
         │  □ Are any pods using hostPID: true unnecessarily?                  │
         │  □ Are any pods using privileged: true unnecessarily?               │
         │  □ Is there a wildcard (*) RBAC binding anywhere?                   │
         │  □ Are there any Services with externalIPs set?                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.4  Case Study — Anatomy of the Tesla Dashboard Breach
         - In 2018 Tesla's Kubernetes Dashboard was exposed to the internet with
           NO authentication. Attackers walked in, found cloud credentials, and ran
           a crypto-mining operation on Tesla's infrastructure. Every control in
           this chapter exists to break one link in that exact chain.
         - The lesson: a single exposed admin UI collapses ALL other security. The
           dashboard's ServiceAccount often has broad rights, so reaching the UI =
           reaching the API with those rights.

         [THE TESLA BREACH CHAIN — attack step vs the CKS control that stops it]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STEP 1  Recon: attacker scans for public dashboards / open ports      │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: ClusterIP only, no LoadBalancer/NodePort (5.2)   │
         │             │                                                          │
         │             ▼                                                          │
         │  STEP 2  Access: dashboard reachable, anonymous, no login required     │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: token auth + no anonymous access (5.2)          │
         │             │                                                          │
         │             ▼                                                          │
         │  STEP 3  Privilege: dashboard SA is bound to cluster-admin             │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: scoped read-only ServiceAccount, least priv (9) │
         │             │                                                          │
         │             ▼                                                          │
         │  STEP 4  Loot: read Secrets → find cloud (AWS) credentials             │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: encryption at rest + RBAC on secrets (9,19)     │
         │             │                                                          │
         │             ▼                                                          │
         │  STEP 5  Pivot: use creds to reach cloud metadata / cloud account      │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: block IMDS + least-privilege node IAM (11.4,16) │
         │             │                                                          │
         │             ▼                                                          │
         │  STEP 6  Impact: deploy crypto-miner pods on the cluster               │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: PSS + admission + Falco runtime alert (17,24,29)│
         │             │                                                          │
         │             ▼                                                          │
         │  Undetected for weeks — NO audit logging, NO runtime detection        │
         │          ─X─ CONTROL: audit logs + Falco = minutes, not weeks (28,29) │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         WHY DEFENSE-IN-DEPTH MATTERS HERE: the attackers only succeeded because
         EVERY layer was open at once. Any single control above — token auth, a
         scoped SA, encrypted secrets, blocked metadata, or Falco — would have
         stopped or exposed the attack. This is the whole CKS thesis in one story.

         [DASHBOARD ACCESS DECISION — should it even exist?]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Do you actually need a web dashboard?                               │
         │    NO  → don't install it. Smallest attack surface wins.            │
         │    YES ↓                                                            │
         │  Can your team use `kubectl` / k9s instead?                          │
         │    YES → prefer CLI; skip the dashboard entirely.                   │
         │    NO  ↓                                                            │
         │  Install with: ClusterIP only + scoped read-only SA + token auth    │
         │    + NetworkPolicy + access ONLY via `kubectl port-forward`.        │
         │  NEVER: LoadBalancer / NodePort / anonymous / cluster-admin SA.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 6: Network Policies — Zero-Trust Pod Networking
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    6.1  The Default: All Pods Can Talk to All Pods
         - Without NetworkPolicy: every pod in the cluster can reach every other
         - This is the "flat network" problem — one breach = lateral movement everywhere
         - The anihpj jobpost app: Django pods can reach postgres, redis, other namespaces
         - Zero-trust networking: default-deny everything, then allow only what's needed
         [DIAGRAM: Flat network (all arrows) vs Zero-trust (only allowed arrows)]

         [FLAT NETWORK vs ZERO TRUST — side by side]
         ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ FLAT NETWORK (default)   │   │  ✅ ZERO TRUST + NetPolicies │
         │                              │   │                              │
         │  anihpj ←──────────────────▶ │   │  anihpj ──────────────▶ pg  │
         │      ↕          ↕            │   │  (only anihpj → pg:5432)    │
         │  postgres ◀────▶ redis       │   │                              │
         │      ↕          ↕            │   │  All other traffic: BLOCKED  │
         │  monitoring ◀──▶ other-ns    │   │                              │
         │                              │   │  Attacker in pod X:          │
         │  Attacker in any pod:        │   │  cannot reach postgres ✅    │
         │  can reach EVERYTHING 🔴     │   │  cannot reach secrets ✅     │
         └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

    6.2  NetworkPolicy Fundamentals
         - NetworkPolicy is a namespaced resource
         - Uses label selectors to identify which pods it applies to
         - policyTypes: Ingress (incoming) and/or Egress (outgoing)
         - Empty ingress rules = deny all incoming; empty egress = deny all outgoing
         - CNI plugin MUST support NetworkPolicy (Calico, Cilium, Weave — yes; Flannel alone — no)
         [CODE: Basic NetworkPolicy YAML explained field by field]

         [NETWORKPOLICY ANATOMY — anihpj example]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Allow only anihpj → postgres traffic                     │
         │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                           │
         │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                         │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: postgres-netpol                                     │
         │    namespace: anihpj-prod                                   │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    podSelector:                 ← which pods this protects  │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: postgres                                         │
         │    policyTypes:                                              │
         │    - Ingress                    ← control incoming traffic   │
         │    ingress:                                                  │
         │    - from:                      ← allow traffic FROM:       │
         │      - podSelector:                                          │
         │          matchLabels:                                        │
         │            app: anihpj          ← only anihpj pods          │
         │      ports:                                                  │
         │      - protocol: TCP                                         │
         │        port: 5432               ← only postgres port        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.3  Common NetworkPolicy Patterns You Must Know
         - Default-deny all ingress in a namespace
         - Default-deny all egress in a namespace
         - Allow pod-to-pod within same namespace
         - Allow specific pod to reach a specific port
         - Allow external IP ranges (CIDR) to reach ingress
         - Allow DNS egress (port 53) — critical, else DNS breaks
         [CODE: 5 ready-to-use NetworkPolicy YAML patterns]

         [COMMON NETWORKPOLICY PATTERNS]
         | Pattern                       | Use Case                    | Exam Frequency |
         | Default deny all ingress      | Isolate namespace           | Very High      |
         | Default deny all egress       | Prevent data exfiltration   | High           |
         | Allow same-namespace only     | Team isolation              | High           |
         | Allow specific port to pod    | Database access control     | Very High      |
         | Allow DNS egress (53/UDP)     | Apps need name resolution   | Medium         |
         | Allow namespaceSelector       | Multi-namespace allowed     | High           |
         | Allow specific CIDR (ingress) | External IPs only           | Medium         |

    6.4  Testing and Verifying NetworkPolicy — Does It Actually Work?
         - Use a test pod to probe connections: kubectl run test --image=busybox
         - Verify BLOCKED traffic: timeout on nc or wget
         - Verify ALLOWED traffic: successful response
         [CODE: Test NetworkPolicy using busybox probes]
         [CODE: Using kubectl exec to verify connectivity matrix]

         [NETWORKPOLICY TESTING MATRIX — anihpj connectivity expectations]
         ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┬────────────────────┐
         │  FROM (source)   │  TO (destination) │  PORT  │  EXPECTED RESULT  │
         ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────┤
         │  ingress-nginx   │  anihpj pod       │  8000  │  ✅ ALLOWED        │
         │  anihpj pod      │  postgres pod     │  5432  │  ✅ ALLOWED        │
         │  anihpj pod      │  redis pod        │  6379  │  ✅ ALLOWED        │
         │  any pod         │  kube-dns (DNS)   │  53    │  ✅ ALLOWED        │
         │  monitoring      │  all pods         │  9090  │  ✅ ALLOWED        │
         ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────┤
         │  postgres pod    │  anihpj pod       │  8000  │  ❌ BLOCKED        │
         │  anihpj pod      │  other-namespace  │  any   │  ❌ BLOCKED        │
         │  attacker-pod    │  postgres pod     │  5432  │  ❌ BLOCKED        │
         │  anihpj pod      │  internet IPs     │  any   │  ❌ BLOCKED        │
         │  external IP     │  anihpj pod       │  8000  │  ❌ BLOCKED (direct│
         │                  │  (direct)         │        │   must go via ingr)│
         └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┴────────────────────┘

         [CNI SUPPORT FOR NETWORKPOLICY — choose the right plugin]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CNI Plugin   │ NetworkPolicy │ eBPF  │ FQDN Egress │ Production    │
         │               │  Support      │       │  Filtering  │  Readiness    │
         ├───────────────┼───────────────┼───────┼─────────────┼───────────────┤
         │  Calico       │  ✅ Full      │  ✅   │  ✅ Yes     │  ✅ Yes       │
         │  Cilium       │  ✅ Full      │  ✅   │  ✅ Yes     │  ✅ Yes       │
         │  Weave Net    │  ✅ Full      │  ❌   │  ❌ No      │  ✅ Yes       │
         │  Flannel      │  ❌ None      │  ❌   │  ❌ No      │  ⚠️ No NetPol│
         │  Kindnet      │  ❌ None      │  ❌   │  ❌ No      │  ⚠️ Testing  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ⚠️  If your cluster uses Flannel: NetworkPolicy objects are IGNORED!
             Switch to Calico or Cilium before implementing zero-trust policies.

         [NETWORKPOLICY DECISION FLOWCHART — how K8s decides to allow or block]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                          │
         │  PACKET arrives at pod's network interface                               │
         │                          │                                                │
         │                          ▼                                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  Q1: Is there ANY NetworkPolicy selecting this pod?              │   │
         │  │      ├── NO  ──▶ ✅ ALLOW (no policies = all traffic allowed)     │   │
         │  │      └── YES ──▶ Continue to Q2                                  │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                          │                                                │
         │                          ▼                                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  Q2: Is this packet EXPLICITLY allowed by at least ONE policy?   │   │
         │  │      ├── YES ──▶ ✅ ALLOW                                        │   │
         │  │      └── NO  ──▶ ❌ DENY  (implicit deny — the default behavior) │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ⚠️  CRITICAL RULE: NetworkPolicies are ADDITIVE (union of all allows). │
         │      If Policy A allows port 80 and Policy B allows port 443,           │
         │      the pod gets BOTH. No policy can override another's allow.         │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ⚠️  TRAP: Once a pod is selected by ANY NetworkPolicy, ALL traffic     │
         │      is DENIED by default except what's explicitly allowed.             │
         │      A pod with NO policies = wide open. A pod with ONE policy = locked.│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [anihpj NETWORK TRAFFIC MAP — visual representation of all allowed flows]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                          │
         │                     🌐 INTERNET                                          │
         │                          │                                               │
         │                          │ HTTPS :443 (Ingress TLS)                      │
         │                          ▼                                               │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │                   🚪 INGRESS CONTROLLER                          │    │
         │  │                   (only exposed entry point)                     │    │
         │  └────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                   │                                       │
         │                    HTTP :8000 (allowed by Policy 3)                       │
         │                                   │                                       │
         │                                   ▼                                       │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │                   📦 anihpj DEPLOYMENT                          │    │
         │  │                   app: anihpj  |  port: 8000                    │    │
         │  │                   ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐      │    │
         │  │                   │  Seccomp | AppArmor | non-root      │      │    │
         │  │                   │  readOnlyRootFS | drop ALL caps     │      │    │
         │  │                   └─────────────────────────────────────┘      │    │
         │  └────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘    │
         │       │                                     │                            │
         │       │ TCP :5432                           │ TCP :6379                 │
         │       │ (Policy 2)                          │ (Policy 4)                │
         │       ▼                                     ▼                            │
         │  ┌──────────────┐                   ┌──────────────┐                   │
         │  │ 🗄️  postgres │                   │ ⚡ Redis     │                   │
         │  │ app:postgres │                   │ app: redis   │                   │
         │  │ port: 5432   │                   │ port: 6379   │                   │
         │  └──────────────┘                   └──────────────┘                   │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  📊 MONITORING POD (Prometheus)                                   │   │
         │  │  app: monitoring  |  Policy 6: can scrape ALL pods on :9090      │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  🔍 DNS (kube-dns) — Policy 5: ALL pods can reach port 53/UDP     │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ❌ BLOCKED PATHS (thanks to default-deny Policy 1):                     │
         │     • internet → anihpj pod DIRECT (must go through ingress)            │
         │     • postgres → anihpj pod (reverse connection blocked)                │
         │     • anihpj pod → internet (egress blocked by default)                 │
         │     • any pod → any pod in other namespaces                             │
         │     • attacker-pod → ANY resource in anihpj namespace                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.5  Securing anihpj with NetworkPolicies — Full Implementation
         - Policy 1: Default deny all in anihpj-prod namespace
         - Policy 2: Allow anihpj → postgres (port 5432)
         - Policy 3: Allow ingress-controller → anihpj (port 8000)
         - Policy 4: Allow anihpj → redis (port 6379) for caching
         - Policy 5: Allow all pods → kube-dns (port 53)
         - Policy 6: Allow monitoring → all pods (port 9090 Prometheus)
         [CODE: Complete NetworkPolicy manifest set for anihpj]
         [DIAGRAM: Final network topology after all policies applied]

          [NETWORK SECURITY ZONES — anihpj post-NetworkPolicy topology]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                   │
          │   🌐 ZONE 0: INTERNET                                             │
          │   ─────────────────────────────────────                           │
          │   Only HTTPS (443) allowed through Ingress Controller             │
          │                              │                                    │
          │                              ▼                                    │
          │   ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗   │
          │   ║  🔀 ZONE 1: INGRESS (DMZ)                                ║   │
          │   ║  ──────────────────────────                               ║   │
          │   ║  ingress-nginx-controller                                ║   │
          │   ║  → Can reach: anihpj pod (port 8000 ONLY)                ║   │
          │   ║  → CANNOT reach: postgres, redis, or any other pod       ║   │
          │   ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝   │
          │                              │                                    │
          │         ┌───────────────────┴───────────────────┐                │
          │         ▼                                       ▼                │
          │   ╔═════════════════════════╗   ╔══════════════════════════╗    │
          │   ║  📦 ZONE 2: APPLICATION ║   ║  🗄️  ZONE 3: DATABASE  ║    │
          │   ║  ─────────────────────── ║   ║  ─────────────────────  ║    │
          │   ║  anihpj Django pod       ║   ║  PostgreSQL pod         ║    │
          │   ║  port: 8000              ║   ║  port: 5432             ║    │
          │   ║                          ║   ║                          ║    │
          │   ║  Can reach:              ║   ║  Can reach:              ║    │
          │   ║  → postgres (5432)       ║   ║  → nothing (egress deny)║    │
          │   ║  → redis (6379)          ║   ║                          ║    │
          │   ║  → DNS (53)              ║   ║  Can be reached by:     ║    │
          │   ║                          ║   ║  → anihpj pod only       ║    │
          │   ║  CANNOT reach:           ║   ║  → monitoring (9090)     ║    │
          │   ║  → any other namespace   ║   ║                          ║    │
          │   ║  → external internet     ║   ║                          ║    │
          │   ╚══════════════════════════╝   ╚══════════════════════════╝    │
          │                                                                   │
          │   ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗   │
          │   ║  📊 ZONE 4: MONITORING (can reach all pods for metrics) ║   │
          │   ║  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────  ║   │
          │   ║  Prometheus | Grafana | Falco                            ║   │
          │   ║  Can reach: ALL pods (port 9090, metrics only)           ║   │
          │   ║  CANNOT be reached by: ANY application pods              ║   │
          │   ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝   │
          │                                                                   │
          │   🔒 Default-deny: ALL other traffic (anihpj→redis, postgres→    │
          │                     internet, redis→anihpj) is BLOCKED            │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.6  Restricting Cluster-Level Access — Protecting the API Server
         - NetworkPolicy is namespaced: it protects PODS — it does not firewall the
           control plane itself. Cluster-level access needs its own layers.
         - Most workloads never need to call the Kubernetes API — block pod egress
           to the API server (443/6443) by default; allow it only for controllers
         - Attack path to kill: default SA token + reachable 6443 = cluster takeover
           from any compromised pod (this is exactly what the CKS curriculum means by
           "use network security policies to restrict cluster level access")
         - Combine with: --anonymous-auth=false (Ch 8), cloud security groups /
           node firewall limiting 6443 to admin CIDRs, and NodeRestriction (Ch 11)
         [CODE: Egress NetworkPolicy — deny anihpj pods → kubernetes.default (443/6443)]
         [CODE: Verify: kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- curl -m3 -k https://kubernetes.default — must TIMEOUT]

         [LAYERS THAT GUARD PORT 6443 — defense-in-depth for the API server]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Internet ──X── cloud firewall / security group (admin CIDRs only)  │
         │  Nodes    ──X── host firewall (ufw/iptables: 6443 from LB only)     │
         │  Pods     ──X── egress NetworkPolicy (no pod → 6443 by default)     │
         │  Anyone   ──X── --anonymous-auth=false + RBAC (no anonymous calls)  │
         │  Kubelets ──X── NodeRestriction (can only modify their own node)    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.7  NetworkPolicy Pattern Catalog — Exam-Ready Templates
         - Seven selector patterns cover every CKS NetworkPolicy task — drill them
           until you can write each from memory in under 3 minutes
         - Remember: policies are ADDITIVE (union of allows); empty podSelector {}
           selects ALL pods in the namespace; policyTypes decides which direction
           is enforced even when the rule list is empty

         [THE 7 PATTERNS — name → selector skeleton → when the exam wants it]
         ┌───┬──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ # │ PATTERN                  │ SKELETON / TRIGGER PHRASE                 │
         ├───┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ 1 │ Default deny ALL         │ podSelector: {} + policyTypes:            │
         │   │ (ingress + egress)       │ [Ingress, Egress], no rules               │
         │ 2 │ Deny ingress only        │ podSelector: {} + policyTypes: [Ingress]  │
         │ 3 │ Allow same-namespace     │ ingress.from: - podSelector: {}           │
         │ 4 │ Allow specific pod+port  │ from: podSelector matchLabels app=X       │
         │   │                          │ ports: [{port: N}]                        │
         │ 5 │ Allow DNS egress         │ egress to kube-system + port 53 UDP/TCP   │
         │   │                          │ (add to EVERY egress-restricted pod!)     │
         │ 6 │ Allow external CIDR      │ egress.to: - ipBlock: {cidr: x.x.x.x/nn}  │
         │ 7 │ Allow cross-namespace    │ from: - namespaceSelector matchLabels     │
         │   │                          │ kubernetes.io/metadata.name=<ns>          │
         └───┴──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [CODE: Pattern 1 + 4 + 5 combined — the anihpj production trio]

    6.8  Official Semantics — the Rules the Exam Assumes You Already Know
         - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/services-networking/network-policies:
           these five rules resolve almost every "why isn't my NetworkPolicy working"
           exam scenario. Get these wrong and a correct-looking YAML still fails.
         - RULE 1 — Isolation is per-direction, per-pod: a pod with NO NetworkPolicy
           selecting it is NON-ISOLATED (fully open) in that direction. The moment
           ANY policy's podSelector matches it AND lists that policyType, the pod
           becomes isolated in that direction — only matching rules are then allowed.
         - RULE 2 — Multiple policies are ADDITIVE, never restrictive: if pod X is
           selected by 3 policies, the allowed traffic is the UNION of all 3. You
           cannot use a second NetworkPolicy to further restrict what a first one
           already allowed — order of evaluation does not matter.
         - RULE 3 — policyTypes defaults if omitted: Ingress is implied by default;
           Egress is implied ONLY if the policy has egress rules. Always write
           policyTypes explicitly — relying on the default is a common silent bug.
         - RULE 4 — a connection needs BOTH sides to agree: source pod's EGRESS
           rules must allow it out, AND destination pod's INGRESS rules must allow
           it in. If either side lacks a matching rule, the connection is blocked.
         - RULE 5 — node traffic is always allowed: traffic to/from the node a pod
           runs on is never blocked by NetworkPolicy, regardless of IP. NetworkPolicy
           cannot fully isolate a pod from its own node (kubelet health checks, etc.
           always get through).

         [THE #1 EXAM TRAP — podSelector + namespaceSelector: AND vs OR]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SAME array element (nested under one "-") = AND (both must match)   │
         │                                                                      │
         │    ingress:                                                         │
         │    - from:                                                          │
         │      - namespaceSelector:                                           │
         │          matchLabels: {team: platform}                              │
         │        podSelector:              ← SAME "-", no new dash            │
         │          matchLabels: {role: client}                                │
         │    MEANS: pods labeled role=client, but ONLY inside namespaces      │
         │    labeled team=platform. Narrower, more restrictive.               │
         │                                                                      │
         │  SEPARATE array elements (each its own "-") = OR (either matches)   │
         │                                                                      │
         │    ingress:                                                         │
         │    - from:                                                          │
         │      - namespaceSelector:                                           │
         │          matchLabels: {team: platform}                              │
         │      - podSelector:              ← NEW "-" = separate rule          │
         │          matchLabels: {role: client}                                │
         │    MEANS: ANY pod in a team=platform namespace, OR ANY pod (in the  │
         │    policy's own namespace) labeled role=client. Much broader.       │
         │                                                                      │
         │  ⚠️  These look nearly identical in YAML — a single misplaced dash  │
         │      silently changes "AND" into "OR" and opens far more traffic    │
         │      than intended. Always verify with a connectivity test (6.4).   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [CODE: Both variants applied to anihpj + kubectl describe netpol showing the difference]


Chapter 7: Ingress Security with TLS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    7.1  TLS Certificates in Kubernetes
         [DIAGRAM: User → HTTPS → Ingress (TLS termination) → HTTP → pod]

         [TLS TERMINATION — what it means for anihpj]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  WITHOUT TLS:                                                        │
         │  👤 User ──── HTTP (plaintext) ───────────────── anihpj pod        │
         │  "password=secret" visible on any network hop                      │
         │                                                                      │
         │  WITH TLS (termination at Ingress):                                 │
         │                        Ingress                                      │
         │  👤 User ──── HTTPS ──── [decrypt] ──── HTTP ─── anihpj pod       │
         │     encrypted          TLS cert         internal plain               │
         │     on internet        stored here       (inside cluster)           │
         │                                                                      │
         │  WITH END-TO-END TLS (mTLS — Chapter 22):                          │
         │  👤 User ──── HTTPS ──── Ingress ──── HTTPS ─── anihpj pod        │
         │     encrypted          re-encrypts      pod cert   pod cert        │
         │     both hops          to pod           verified   verified        │
         │                                                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [TLS CERTIFICATE TYPES — which to use when]
         ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Certificate Type      │  Use Case                                   │
         ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Self-signed           │  Local dev, testing only                    │
         │  (openssl req -x509)   │  ⚠️  Browser shows "not secure" warning     │
         ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Let's Encrypt (free)  │  Production public domains                  │
         │  via cert-manager      │  ✅ Auto-renewed, trusted by all browsers   │
         ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Private CA (internal) │  Internal services, air-gapped environments │
         │                        │  ✅ Your org controls the CA                │
         ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Wildcard cert         │  *.anihpj.com covers all subdomains         │
         │                        │  ⚠️  One cert compromise = all subdomains   │
         └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Certificates stored as Kubernetes Secrets (type: kubernetes.io/tls)
         - Ingress controller reads the Secret and presents cert to users
         - Two options: self-signed (for testing), CA-signed (for production)
         - cert-manager: automates certificate issuance from Let's Encrypt
         [CODE: Creating TLS secret from existing cert+key files]
         [CODE: Ingress YAML with TLS configuration for anihpj.com]

    7.2  cert-manager — Automated Certificate Management
         - cert-manager watches Ingress resources and issues certs automatically
         - Uses Let's Encrypt (free CA) or private CA
         - Automatically renews certificates before expiry
         - ClusterIssuer vs Issuer: cluster-wide vs namespace-scoped
         [CODE: cert-manager ClusterIssuer YAML — Let's Encrypt configuration]
         [CODE: Annotated Ingress YAML for automatic cert issuance]

         [cert-manager CERTIFICATE LIFECYCLE — automated flow for anihpj]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  1. You create Ingress with annotation:                             │
         │     cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod               │
         │                   │                                                  │
         │                   ▼                                                  │
         │  2. cert-manager detects the Ingress → creates Certificate resource │
         │                   │                                                  │
         │                   ▼                                                  │
         │  3. cert-manager creates a CertificateRequest                       │
         │     → challenges Let's Encrypt via HTTP-01 or DNS-01 challenge     │
         │                   │                                                  │
         │                   ▼                                                  │
         │  4. Let's Encrypt verifies you own anihpj.com                       │
         │     → issues signed certificate                                     │
         │                   │                                                  │
         │                   ▼                                                  │
         │  5. cert-manager stores cert in K8s Secret: anihpj-tls             │
         │                   │                                                  │
         │                   ▼                                                  │
         │  6. Ingress controller reads Secret → serves HTTPS to users        │
         │                   │                                                  │
         │                   ▼                                                  │
         │  7. 30 days before expiry → cert-manager auto-renews! 🔄           │
         │     Zero manual intervention needed.                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.3  Enforcing HTTPS — Redirecting HTTP to HTTPS
         - Never serve your app over plain HTTP in production
         - Ingress annotation: nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/ssl-redirect: "true"
         - HTTP Strict Transport Security (HSTS): tell browsers to always use HTTPS
         [CODE: Ingress YAML with SSL redirect and HSTS headers]

         [INGRESS SECURITY ANNOTATIONS — reference table]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ANNOTATION                                    │ EFFECT              │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
         │  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/ssl-redirect:     │ Forces HTTP→HTTPS   │
         │  "true"                                        │ redirect           │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
         │  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/                  │ Adds HSTS header:   │
         │  configuration-snippet: |                      │ browsers always use │
         │    add_header Strict-Transport-Security        │ HTTPS for 1 year   │
         │    "max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains"       │                     │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
         │  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/                  │ Blocks clickjacking │
         │  configuration-snippet: |                      │ attacks             │
         │    add_header X-Frame-Options "DENY"           │                     │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
         │  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/                  │ Only allow TLS 1.2+ │
         │  ssl-protocols: "TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3"             │ no old TLS versions │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
         │  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/                  │ Rate limiting       │
         │  limit-rps: "10"                               │ per IP per second   │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

Chapter 8: Restricting API Server Access
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    8.1  The API Server is the Crown Jewel
         - Every kubectl command, every controller, every pod — goes through API server
         - Compromise the API server = own the entire cluster
         - CIS Benchmark: API server has the most checks of any component
         - Goal: restrict WHO can reach it, HOW they authenticate, WHAT they can do
         [DIAGRAM: Everything flows through the API server — it is the single entry point]

    8.2  Disabling Anonymous Authentication
         - By default, unauthenticated requests get the "system:anonymous" user
         - Anonymous user can do very little, but some misconfigured clusters give it access
         - CIS 1.2.1: Set --anonymous-auth=false on the API server
         - After disabling: ALL requests must carry a valid credential
         [CODE: kube-apiserver.yaml — adding --anonymous-auth=false]
         [CODE: Testing anonymous auth is disabled with curl]

    8.3  Enabling Audit Logging on the API Server
         - Audit logging records every request to the API server
         - Who made the request, what resource, what action, when
         - Required for compliance (PCI-DSS, SOC2, HIPAA)
         - CKS exam: you will be asked to configure an audit policy
         [CODE: audit-policy.yaml — levels and rules explained]
         [CODE: kube-apiserver flags to enable audit logging]

         [AUDIT POLICY LEVELS — what gets logged]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  AUDIT LEVELS (ordered from least to most verbose)          │
         │                                                              │
         │  None     → Do not log this event at all                    │
         │  Metadata → Log request metadata only (who, what, when)     │
         │             not the actual request or response body          │
         │  Request  → Log metadata + request body                     │
         │  RequestResponse → Log metadata + request + response body   │
         │             (most verbose — use carefully, fills disk fast)  │
         │                                                              │
         │  [EXAMPLE POLICY]                                            │
         │  rules:                                                      │
         │    - level: None                                             │
         │      resources: [{group: "", resources: ["events"]}]         │
         │      # don't log noisy event updates                         │
         │    - level: Metadata                                         │
         │      resources: [{group: "", resources: ["secrets"]}]        │
         │      # log who accessed secrets, but not the values         │
         │    - level: RequestResponse                                  │
         │      resources: [{group: "", resources: ["pods"]}]           │
         │      # log full pod create/delete operations                │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.4  Restricting API Server to Specific IP Ranges
         - Use --service-cluster-ip-range to limit service IPs
         - Use cloud firewall rules to restrict port 6443 to known IPs
         - OPA/Gatekeeper can enforce IP-based admission policies

         [API SERVER AUTHENTICATION METHODS — comparison card]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  METHOD              │ HOW IT WORKS              │ USE CASE        │
         ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
         │  Client Certificate  │ kubectl uses cert from    │ Human admins    │
         │  (x509)              │ kubeconfig; API verifies  │ kubeadm default │
         │                      │ against cluster CA        │                 │
         ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
         │  Bearer Token        │ SA token in Authorization │ Pods → API      │
         │  (ServiceAccount)    │ header; signed by K8s     │ In-cluster apps │
         ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
         │  OIDC (OpenID Conn.) │ External IdP (GitHub,     │ SSO for humans  │
         │                      │ Google, Dex) issues JWT   │ Enterprise auth │
         ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
         │  Webhook Token Auth  │ External server validates │ Custom auth     │
         │                      │ token via HTTP callback   │ systems         │
         ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
         │  Bootstrap Tokens    │ Short-lived tokens for    │ Node joining    │
         │                      │ new nodes joining cluster │ kubeadm join    │
         ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
         │  Static Token File   │ Tokens in CSV file on     │ ❌ NEVER use in │
         │  (--token-auth-file) │ control plane disk        │ production!     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [API SERVER IP RESTRICTION — layered approach]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LAYER 1: Cloud Firewall / Security Group                           │
         │  Rule: Allow TCP 6443 FROM admin_cidr (e.g. 10.0.0.0/8)           │
         │  Rule: DENY TCP 6443 from 0.0.0.0/0                                │
         │  → Attacker from internet cannot even reach API server port         │
         │                                                                      │
         │  LAYER 2: Kubernetes RBAC (what authenticated callers can do)       │
         │  → Even if attacker gets past firewall, RBAC denies their actions   │
         │                                                                      │
         │  LAYER 3: Admission Control                                          │
         │  → Even if RBAC allows, OPA/PSS can still reject the request       │
         │                                                                      │
         │  LAYER 4: Audit Logging                                              │
         │  → Every access recorded, even if it succeeded                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.5  Insecure Port — Removing the Back Door
         - Old Kubernetes versions had --insecure-port=8080 (no auth, no TLS)
         - Removed in Kubernetes 1.20+, but some clusters have it lingering
         - CIS 1.2.5: ensure insecure port is not set
         - Verify: netstat -tlnp | grep 8080 → nothing should appear
         [CODE: Checking for and removing insecure port configuration]

         [API SERVER PORTS — which are safe, which are dangerous]
         ┌──────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PORT    │  PURPOSE AND SECURITY STATUS                            │
         ├──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  6443    │  ✅ Secure API port (TLS + auth required)               │
         │          │  → This is the ONLY port you should expose             │
         ├──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  8080    │  ❌ Insecure port (NO TLS, NO auth — removed in 1.20)   │
         │          │  → CIS check: must be 0 (disabled) or absent           │
         ├──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  10250   │  ⚠️  kubelet API (should be locked down with auth)      │
         │          │  → Should NOT be reachable from outside cluster        │
         ├──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  10255   │  ❌ kubelet read-only port (unauthenticated!)           │
         │          │  → CIS check: readOnlyPort must be 0                  │
         ├──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  2379    │  ⚠️  etcd client port (only API server should reach it) │
         │          │  → Firewall: allow ONLY from control plane IPs        │
         ├──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  2380    │  ⚠️  etcd peer port (only etcd members should reach it) │
         └──────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.6  API Server Security Flag Quick Reference
         - One table with every security-relevant kube-apiserver flag the CKS can
           ask about — edit them all in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml
         - Verify any flag in seconds: ps aux | grep kube-apiserver | tr ' ' '\n' | grep <flag>
         - Remember: the kubelet watches the manifest file — save = auto-restart

         [API SERVER SECURITY FLAGS — flag → secure value → what it does]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
         │ FLAG                             │ SECURE VALUE        │ WHAT IT DOES                 │
         ├──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
         │ --anonymous-auth                 │ false               │ no unauthenticated requests  │
         │ --authorization-mode             │ Node,RBAC           │ authz chain (never           │
         │                                  │                     │ AlwaysAllow)                 │
         │ --enable-admission-plugins       │ NodeRestriction,... │ kubelet scope-limiting +     │
         │                                  │                     │ policy admission             │
         │ --audit-policy-file              │ /etc/kubernetes/    │ which events get recorded    │
         │                                  │ audit-policy.yaml   │                              │
         │ --audit-log-path                 │ /var/log/kubernetes │ where audit events land      │
         │                                  │ /audit.log          │                              │
         │ --audit-log-maxage/-maxbackup/   │ 30 / 10 / 100       │ audit log retention          │
         │ -maxsize                         │                     │                              │
         │ --encryption-provider-config     │ /etc/kubernetes/    │ Secrets encrypted at rest    │
         │                                  │ enc/enc.yaml        │ in etcd                      │
         │ --client-ca-file                 │ ca.crt              │ verifies client certificates │
         │ --etcd-cafile/-certfile/-keyfile │ etcd certs          │ mTLS between API and etcd    │
         │ --kubelet-certificate-authority  │ ca.crt              │ verify kubelet serving certs │
         │ --profiling                      │ false               │ disable /debug/pprof         │
         │ --service-account-lookup         │ true                │ reject deleted-SA tokens     │
         │ --tls-cert-file/--tls-private-   │ apiserver certs     │ TLS for the API endpoint     │
         │ key-file                         │                     │                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘
         [CODE: one-liner audit of all current flags: ps aux | grep kube-apiserver]


Chapter 9: RBAC — Role-Based Access Control for Security
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    9.1  RBAC Refresher — The Security Lens
         - RBAC controls what authenticated users/pods CAN DO in the cluster
         - Principle of Least Privilege: minimum permissions needed, nothing more
         - 4 key resources: Role, ClusterRole, RoleBinding, ClusterRoleBinding
         - Common mistake: using ClusterRole when a namespaced Role would do

         [RBAC 4-RESOURCE ANATOMY — how they connect]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                      │
         │  WHO                    BINDING               WHAT CAN DO           │
         │  (Subject)              (Connects who         (Role/ClusterRole)    │
         │                          to what)                                   │
         │                                                                      │
         │  👤 User: alice  ──────▶ RoleBinding       ──▶ Role               │
         │  🤖 SA: anihpj-sa        (namespace-scoped)     (namespace-scoped) │
         │  👥 Group: devs  ──────▶                        verbs + resources  │
         │                                                                      │
         │  👤 User: bob    ──────▶ ClusterRoleBinding ──▶ ClusterRole        │
         │  🤖 SA: monitoring       (cluster-wide)          (cluster-wide)    │
         │                                                   verbs + resources │
         │                                                                      │
         │  SCOPE RULE:                                                         │
         │  Role         = permission within ONE namespace                     │
         │  ClusterRole  = permission across ALL namespaces OR cluster objects │
         │  RoleBinding  = binds Role OR ClusterRole to subject in namespace  │
         │  ClusterRoleBinding = binds ClusterRole to subject cluster-wide    │
         │                                                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [RBAC VERB × RESOURCE PERMISSION MATRIX — for anihpj team]
         ┌──────────────────┬────────┬──────┬────────┬──────────┬─────────────┐
         │  Role            │ create │ get  │ update │  delete  │  exec/logs  │
         ├──────────────────┼────────┼──────┼────────┼──────────┼─────────────┤
         │  anihpj-dev      │  pods  │ pods │   —    │    —     │  pods/log   │
         │  (Developer)     │        │ logs │        │          │  pods/exec  │
         ├──────────────────┼────────┼──────┼────────┼──────────┼─────────────┤
         │  anihpj-deployer │   —    │ dep. │  dep.  │    —     │     —       │
         │  (CI/CD)         │        │ pods │        │          │             │
         ├──────────────────┼────────┼──────┼────────┼──────────┼─────────────┤
         │  cluster-viewer  │   —    │ all  │   —    │    —     │     —       │
         │  (Ops read-only) │        │      │        │          │             │
         ├──────────────────┼────────┼──────┼────────┼──────────┼─────────────┤
         │  security-audit  │   —    │roles │   —    │    —     │     —       │
         │  (Security)      │        │ sec. │        │          │             │
         ├──────────────────┼────────┼──────┼────────┼──────────┼─────────────┤
         │  cluster-admin   │  ALL   │  ALL │  ALL   │   ALL    │    ALL      │
         │  ← NEVER grant   │        │      │        │          │ ← 🔴 NEVER │
         └──────────────────┴────────┴──────┴────────┴──────────┴─────────────┘

         [DIAGRAM: User → ClusterRoleBinding → ClusterRole → verb + resource pairs]

         [RBAC COMPONENTS — anihpj deployment context]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  👨‍💻 Developer (Alice)          🤖 CI/CD Pipeline            │
         │  Role: anihpj-developer        Role: anihpj-deployer        │
         │  Namespace: anihpj-dev         Namespace: anihpj-prod       │
         │  Verbs: get, list, create      Verbs: get, list, update     │
         │  Resources: pods, logs         Resources: deployments        │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔧 Ops (Bob)                  🛡️ Security (Carol)          │
         │  ClusterRole: cluster-viewer   ClusterRole: security-auditor│
         │  Verbs: get, list, watch       Verbs: get, list             │
         │  Resources: nodes, namespaces  Resources: roles, secrets    │
         │                                (read-only audit)            │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.2  RBAC Misconfigurations — The Top Security Risks
         - Wildcard (*) verbs: grants everything — never use in production
         - Wildcard (*) resources: same issue
         - Binding to system:masters: gives cluster-admin permanently
         - Wildcard API groups: exposes all API groups
         - escalate/bind/impersonate verbs: allow privilege escalation
         [CODE: Example of dangerous RBAC bindings and how to fix them]

         [DANGEROUS RBAC PATTERNS — red flags]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ DANGEROUS                   ✅ SECURE                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  rules:                         rules:                       │
         │  - apiGroups: ["*"]  ← 🔴       - apiGroups: [""]  ✅       │
         │    resources: ["*"]  ← 🔴         resources:                 │
         │    verbs: ["*"]      ← 🔴           ["pods", "services"] ✅ │
         │                                   verbs: ["get","list"] ✅  │
         │                                                              │
         │  # This is cluster-admin!        # Minimal, specific        │
         │  # Never use in production       # permissions only         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [RBAC AUTHORIZATION FLOW — the complete decision path]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                          │
         │  👤 USER or 🤖 SERVICEACCOUNT makes an API request                       │
         │                          │                                                │
         │                          ▼                                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  STEP 1: AUTHENTICATION — "Who are you?"                         │   │
         │  │  • Validates certificate / token / OIDC claim                    │   │
         │  │  • Extracts: username, groups, extra attributes                  │   │
         │  │  • FAIL → 401 Unauthorized (stops here)                          │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                          │ ✅ Authenticated                               │
         │                          ▼                                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  STEP 2: AUTHORIZATION — "What can you do?"                      │   │
         │  │  • Combines: ALL Roles + ClusterRoles bound to this user/group   │   │
         │  │  • Checks: does ANY bound role allow {verb, resource, namespace}?│   │
         │  │  • ALLOW if any rule matches → 200 OK                            │   │
         │  │  • DENY if no rule matches → 403 Forbidden                        │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                          │ ✅ Authorized                                  │
         │                          ▼                                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  STEP 3: ADMISSION CONTROL — "Should we allow this change?"     │   │
         │  │  • Mutating webhooks modify the request                          │   │
         │  │  • Validating webhooks accept/reject                             │   │
         │  │  • Built-in plugins: NodeRestriction, PodSecurity, etc.          │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                          │ ✅ Admitted                                   │
         │                          ▼                                                │
         │                    ✅ REQUEST PROCESSED                                  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  COMMON RBAC INTERVIEW/EXAM QUESTION:                             │   │
         │  │  Q: "User Alice can get pods in ns1 but not ns2. Why?"          │   │
         │  │  A: Alice has a Role (not ClusterRole) binding in ns1 only.      │   │
         │  │     Roles are namespace-scoped. ClusterRoles are cluster-wide.   │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.3  RBAC for the anihpj Application
         - Developer role: view pods, logs, configmaps in anihpj-dev
         - CI/CD role: update deployments in anihpj-prod
         - Ops role: view nodes, namespaces cluster-wide
         - Security role: view roles, rolebindings, secrets (read-only)
         - Monitoring role: get pods, services across all namespaces
         [CODE: Complete RBAC manifest set for anihpj team roles]

    9.4  Verifying RBAC — kubectl auth can-i
         - Test permissions before applying them to real users
         - Impersonate users: --as=username --as-group=group
         - Useful for validating least-privilege configuration
         [CODE: Complete suite of kubectl auth can-i test commands]

         [kubectl auth can-i VERIFICATION TABLE — anihpj team expected results]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ kubectl auth can-i <verb> <resource> -n <ns> --as=<user>        │
