         │                                                                      │
         │  USER: alice (developer)                                             │
         │  kubectl auth can-i get pods -n anihpj-dev --as=alice       → yes  │
         │  kubectl auth can-i delete pods -n anihpj-dev --as=alice    → no   │
         │  kubectl auth can-i get pods -n anihpj-prod --as=alice      → no   │
         │  kubectl auth can-i get secrets -n anihpj-dev --as=alice    → no   │
         │                                                                      │
         │  USER: ci-pipeline (deployer)                                       │
         │  kubectl auth can-i update deployments -n anihpj-prod --as=ci → yes│
         │  kubectl auth can-i delete deployments -n anihpj-prod --as=ci → no │
         │  kubectl auth can-i get secrets -n anihpj-prod --as=ci      → no   │
         │                                                                      │
         │  SA: anihpj-sa (the pod's own identity)                            │
         │  kubectl auth can-i get pods --as=system:serviceaccount:           │
         │           anihpj-prod:anihpj-sa                              → no  │
         │  kubectl auth can-i get secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:        │
         │           anihpj-prod:anihpj-sa                              → no  │
         │  # anihpj pod should have NO API access at all ✅                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.5  Automatic RBAC Audit — Finding Overpermitted Subjects
         - Tools: kubectl-who-can, rakkess, rbac-police
         - Find all subjects that can access secrets in a namespace
         - Find all subjects with cluster-admin rights
         - Run as part of security audit before production deployment
         [CODE: Scanning for overpermitted RBAC bindings]

    9.6  Official RBAC Facts — Patterns the Exam Assumes You Already Know
         - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/reference/access-authn-authz/rbac: the
           details below resolve the RBAC scenarios that trip up otherwise-correct
           answers on the CKS exam.
         - THE #1 UNDER-TAUGHT PATTERN — ClusterRole bound via RoleBinding: you can
           reference a ClusterRole from a RoleBinding. The result is NAMESPACE-
           SCOPED (limited to the RoleBinding's namespace), even though a
           ClusterRole is normally cluster-wide. This is the standard way to reuse
           one ClusterRole definition (e.g. "secret-reader") across many namespaces
           without writing a near-identical Role in each one.
         - Rule anatomy has TWO more fields worth knowing: resourceNames (restrict
           a rule to specific named objects, e.g. only ConfigMap "anihpj-config")
           and nonResourceURLs (ClusterRole-only, for endpoints like /healthz,
           /metrics — no apiGroups/resources needed for these).
         - Kubernetes ships 4 built-in user-facing ClusterRoles — know their exact
           scope, especially that "view" deliberately EXCLUDES Secrets:
         - Aggregated ClusterRoles: a ClusterRole can define an aggregationRule
           with a label selector; any OTHER ClusterRole carrying a matching label
           gets its rules automatically merged in. This is how "edit"/"view" stay
           extensible as CRDs are installed — a CRD's ClusterRole just carries the
           right aggregation label and it's included automatically.
         - Binding immutability: once created, a RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding's
           roleRef CANNOT be changed (it's immutable by design, to prevent silent
           privilege changes). To point a binding at a different Role, you must
           delete and recreate it — kubectl apply on a changed roleRef will error.

         [BUILT-IN CLUSTERROLES — exact scope, memorize this table]
         ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CLUSTERROLE     │ EXACT SCOPE                                          │
         ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ cluster-admin   │ superuser — all verbs, all resources, all namespaces │
         │ admin           │ full namespace admin (rw + manage Roles/RoleBindings │
         │                 │ in that namespace) — via RoleBinding only            │
         │ edit            │ rw on most namespaced objects — does NOT allow       │
         │                 │ viewing/editing Roles or RoleBindings                │
         │ view            │ read-only on most namespaced objects — DOES NOT      │
         │                 │ include Secrets (by design, to avoid leaking creds)  │
         └─────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CLUSTERROLE + ROLEBINDING — the reuse pattern, official example]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ClusterRole "secret-reader" (defined ONCE, cluster-wide object)     │
         │        │                                                             │
         │        │  bound via RoleBinding in namespace "anihpj-dev"           │
         │        ▼                                                             │
         │  RoleBinding (namespace: anihpj-dev) → roleRef: secret-reader       │
         │        │                                                             │
         │  RESULT: subject can read secrets ONLY in anihpj-dev —              │
         │  the SAME ClusterRole bound again via a RoleBinding in               │
         │  anihpj-prod would grant access ONLY in anihpj-prod.                │
         │  ⚠️  It is the RoleBinding's namespace that scopes access, not the  │
         │      ClusterRole itself — this is the opposite of what the names    │
         │      "Cluster"Role vs "Role"Binding might suggest.                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [CODE: One ClusterRole "secret-reader" reused via RoleBinding in anihpj-dev AND anihpj-prod]
         [CODE: Rule with resourceNames restricting access to one named ConfigMap]


Chapter 10: Service Accounts — Securing Pod Identity
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    10.1  How Pods Authenticate to the API Server
          - Every pod gets a Service Account (default if not specified)
          - The ServiceAccount token is mounted at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io
          - Pods can use this token to call the Kubernetes API
          - The default ServiceAccount in most namespaces has no permissions
          - But: a compromised pod CAN use its token to explore the API
          [DIAGRAM: Pod → reads mounted token → calls API server → RBAC check]

    10.2  The Dangers of the Default Service Account
          - The default ServiceAccount is shared by all pods in a namespace
          - If you grant the default SA any permissions, ALL pods inherit them
          - Best practice: create dedicated ServiceAccounts per workload
          - Disable token auto-mount for pods that don't need API access
          [CODE: Disabling automount on ServiceAccount and individual pods]

          [DEFAULT vs DEDICATED SERVICE ACCOUNT — security comparison]
          ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
          │  ❌ DEFAULT SERVICEACCOUNT   │   │  ✅ DEDICATED SERVICEACCOUNT │
          │                              │   │                              │
          │  All pods → "default" SA    │   │  anihpj pod → anihpj-sa     │
          │  Grant permissions to default│   │  postgres pod → postgres-sa │
          │  → ALL pods get permissions  │   │  Each SA → minimal permissions│
          │                              │   │                              │
          │  Compromised pod:            │   │  Compromised anihpj pod:     │
          │  → can access API as default │   │  → can only do what         │
          │  → may reach secrets across  │   │    anihpj-sa is allowed     │
          │    the namespace             │   │  → nothing else              │
          └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

    10.3  Bound Service Account Tokens (Projected Tokens)
          - Old tokens: never expire, static, stored as Secrets
          - New tokens (K8s 1.21+): time-limited, audience-bound, auto-rotated
          - Use TokenRequest API for short-lived tokens
          - Projected volumes: mount token into pod with expiry
          [CODE: Pod spec with projected ServiceAccount token]

          [OLD vs NEW SERVICE ACCOUNT TOKENS — security comparison]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  FEATURE             │ OLD (Static) TOKEN      │ NEW (Bound) TOKEN  │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
          │  Stored as           │ K8s Secret (auto-created)│ Projected volume  │
          │  Expiry              │ Never expires! ← 🔴     │ 1 hour (default)  │
          │  Audience            │ Any K8s API server       │ Specific audience │
          │  Rotation            │ Manual only              │ Automatic ✅      │
          │  Revocable           │ Only by deleting Secret  │ Time-limited      │
          │  If stolen impact    │ Persistent access! 🔴   │ Expires soon ✅   │
          │  Available since     │ Always                   │ K8s 1.21+         │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [SERVICE ACCOUNT TOKEN FLOW — how a pod uses its identity]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  1. Pod starts → kubelet creates projected volume                   │
          │     Mount path: /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/      │
          │     Files: token (JWT), ca.crt (cluster CA), namespace             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  2. App inside pod reads the token file                             │
          │     $ cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token       │
          │     → eyJhbGciOiJSUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9...                     │
          │                                                                      │
          │  3. App calls K8s API with Authorization: Bearer <token>            │
          │     $ curl https://kubernetes.default.svc/api/v1/pods               │
          │       -H "Authorization: Bearer $(cat /var/run/.../token)"          │
          │                                                                      │
          │  4. API server validates token → RBAC check → respond              │
          │                                                                      │
          │  IF automountServiceAccountToken: false:                            │
          │     Step 1 never happens → /var/run/secrets/ is EMPTY              │
          │     → Pod cannot call K8s API at all ✅                            │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.4  Securing anihpj Service Accounts
          - anihpj-sa: no API access needed (pure web app), disable automount
          - postgres-sa: no API access needed, disable automount
          - anihpj-operator-sa: needs to read ConfigMaps in same namespace only
          - ci-deployer-sa: needs to update Deployments in anihpj-prod only
          [CODE: ServiceAccount YAML with automountServiceAccountToken: false]
          [CODE: RBAC Role + RoleBinding for anihpj-operator-sa]

    10.5  Official ServiceAccount Facts — Precedence and Lifecycle Details
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/configure-pod-container/
            configure-service-account: the precise rules the exam expects you to
            already know cold.
          - AUTOMOUNT PRECEDENCE (frequently tested): automountServiceAccountToken
            can be set on the ServiceAccount object AND/OR the Pod spec. If BOTH
            are set, the POD SPEC WINS. This means a Pod can opt IN to a token even
            if its ServiceAccount says false, and opt OUT even if the SA says true
            (or omits the field, which defaults to mounting).
          - REVOCATION TIMING: a bound/projected token is automatically invalidated
            when its Pod is deleted — specifically around 60 seconds after
            .metadata.deletionTimestamp is set (not instantly). Don't assume a
            "kubectl delete pod" instantly kills the token's usefulness.
          - Every namespace ALWAYS has a "default" ServiceAccount — you cannot
            remove this behavior at the namespace level. If a Pod spec omits
            serviceAccountName entirely, it silently gets "default" — this is why
            "never bind RBAC to default" (10.2) matters even for pods you didn't
            explicitly configure.
          - serviceAccountName is IMMUTABLE after Pod creation — you cannot patch
            a running Pod onto a different ServiceAccount; you must recreate it
            (this mirrors the RBAC binding immutability in 9.6).
          - kubectl create token is the modern, correct way to mint a short-lived
            token for testing (--duration flag); manually creating a long-lived
            Secret of type kubernetes.io/service-account-token still works but
            should be treated as the deprecated legacy path.

          [AUTOMOUNT PRECEDENCE — who wins when both are set]
          ┌────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
          │ SERVICEACCOUNT SETTING     │ POD SPEC SETTING      │ RESULT           │
          ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
          │ automount: false           │ (not set)              │ NOT mounted      │
          │ automount: false           │ automount: true        │ MOUNTED (Pod wins)│
          │ automount: true / not set  │ automount: false       │ NOT mounted (Pod)│
          │ (not set → defaults true)  │ (not set)              │ MOUNTED (default)│
          └────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────────┘
          [CODE: kubectl create token anihpj-sa --duration=1h -- verify short-lived token behavior]


Chapter 11: Node Restriction & Hardening Authorization
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    11.1  The NodeRestriction Admission Plugin
          - NodeRestriction limits what kubelets can modify via the API
          - Without it: a compromised kubelet can modify ANY node/pod labels
          - With it: kubelets can only modify their OWN node and pods they run
          - CIS Benchmark 1.2.17: NodeRestriction must be enabled
          [CODE: API server flag to enable NodeRestriction]

          [NodeRestriction — WHAT EACH KUBELET CAN AND CANNOT TOUCH]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  SCENARIO: node-worker-2's kubelet is compromised                   │
          │                                                                      │
          │  WITHOUT NodeRestriction:                                            │
          │  Compromised kubelet on node-worker-2 CAN:                          │
          │  ❌ Modify labels on node-worker-1 (impersonate another node)       │
          │  ❌ Evict pods from node-worker-3 (disrupt other nodes)             │
          │  ❌ Modify labels on pods it doesn't run (bypass scheduling)        │
          │  ❌ This enables privilege escalation across the entire cluster     │
          │                                                                      │
          │  WITH NodeRestriction enabled:                                       │
          │  Compromised kubelet on node-worker-2 CAN ONLY:                     │
          │  ✅ Read its own node object (node-worker-2)                        │
          │  ✅ Modify its own node status and labels                           │
          │  ✅ Read/write pods scheduled ON node-worker-2 only                │
          │  ✅ Cannot touch anything on node-worker-1 or node-worker-3        │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.2  Authorization Modes — Understanding the Chain
          - Kubernetes supports multiple authorization modes simultaneously
          - Modes checked in order; first ALLOW stops the chain
          - Recommended modes: Node,RBAC
          - Never use: AlwaysAllow (disables all authorization — catastrophic)
          [CODE: API server --authorization-mode flag — secure configuration]

          [AUTHORIZATION MODE COMPARISON]
          | Mode           | How it works                     | Security Level |
          | AlwaysAllow    | Allow everything, no checks       | 🔴 NEVER use   |
          | AlwaysDeny     | Deny everything (testing only)    | ⚠️  Testing    |
          | RBAC           | Roles + Bindings                  | ✅ Use this    |
          | Node           | Kubelet access control            | ✅ Required    |
          | Webhook        | External authorization service    | ✅ Advanced    |
          | ABAC           | Attribute-based, file-based       | ⚠️  Legacy    |

    11.3  Protecting Node-Level Access
          - SSH access to nodes: use SSO, audit keys, remove stale users
          - Disable root SSH login on all nodes
          - Use node groups and IAM roles for cloud-managed nodes
          - Never expose kubelet API (port 10250) to the internet
          [CHECKLIST: Node access hardening checklist]

    11.4  Protecting Node Metadata & Endpoints
          - CKS curriculum (Cluster Setup): "protect node metadata and endpoints"
          - THE cloud metadata service (IMDS) lives at 169.254.169.254 — a link-
            local address reachable from EVERY pod by default. It hands out the
            node's IAM role credentials to anyone who can send it an HTTP GET.
          - This is exactly the Capital One breach: SSRF in an app → GET to the
            metadata API → steal the node's IAM credentials → read S3 buckets
          - THREE layers protect it:
            1. NetworkPolicy egress: deny all pods → 169.254.169.254/32 (pods that
               truly need cloud identity should use IRSA/Workload Identity instead)
            2. IMDSv2 (AWS): require a session token + set hop limit = 1 so a
               proxied SSRF request can't reach it; GCP/Azure require a special
               header (Metadata-Flavor: Google / Metadata: true)
            3. Least-privilege node IAM role (Ch 16) so a stolen credential is worth
               little even if reached
          - ENDPOINTS to lock down on every node (the "endpoints" half of the
            objective): kubelet 10250 (authenticated API — never public), kubelet
            read-only 10255 (must be OFF: readOnlyPort: 0), kube-proxy 10256,
            control-plane 6443/2379-2380/10257/10259 — restrict all to the
            components/CIDRs that need them (see Ch 13.5 / 16)
          - anihpj: jobpost pods have NO reason to call the metadata API — a
            default-deny egress + explicit deny of 169.254.169.254/32 closes the
            single most damaging cloud-pivot path

          [THE METADATA SSRF PIVOT — and the three cuts that stop it]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  attacker → SSRF in jobpost → GET http://169.254.169.254/            │
          │             latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/               │
          │                         │                                            │
          │      CUT 1 ─X─ NetworkPolicy egress deny 169.254.169.254/32          │
          │      CUT 2 ─X─ IMDSv2 token required + hop-limit 1 (SSRF can't proxy)│
          │      CUT 3 ─X─ node IAM role = minimal (stolen creds nearly useless) │
          │                         │                                            │
          │             (if all cuts fail) → cloud IAM credentials stolen        │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [NODE ENDPOINTS — port → who may reach it → hardening]
          ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
          │ PORT     │ SERVICE                  │ HARDENING                        │
          ├──────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
          │ 10250    │ kubelet API (authn)      │ authn=Webhook, authz=Webhook;    │
          │          │                          │ never public                     │
          │ 10255    │ kubelet read-only        │ readOnlyPort: 0 (turn OFF)       │
          │ 10256    │ kube-proxy health        │ localhost / node CIDR only       │
          │ 6443     │ API server               │ admin CIDR / VPN only            │
          │ 2379-80  │ etcd client/peer         │ control plane only, mTLS         │
          │169.254.  │ cloud metadata (IMDS)    │ NetworkPolicy deny + IMDSv2      │
          │169.254   │                          │                                  │
          └──────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Egress NetworkPolicy denying pods → 169.254.169.254/32 for anihpj-prod]
          [CODE: Enforce IMDSv2 (AWS) with hop limit 1 + verify from inside a pod (must fail)]


Chapter 12: Kubernetes Version Management for Security
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    12.1  Why Keeping Kubernetes Updated Is a Security Requirement
          - CVEs (Common Vulnerabilities & Exposures) are disclosed regularly
          - Old Kubernetes versions = known vulnerabilities = easy targets
          - K8s supports only 3 minor versions: n, n-1, n-2
          - CKS exam: understand upgrade path and security implications

          [NOTABLE KUBERNETES CVEs — real vulnerabilities, real consequences]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  CVE             │ Severity │ Affected Vers.  │ Impact               │
          ├──────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
          │  CVE-2018-1002105│ CRITICAL │ < 1.10.11,      │ Privilege escalation │
          │  "The Big Bird"  │  9.8     │ < 1.11.5,       │ to cluster-admin via │
          │                  │          │ < 1.12.3        │ API server proxy     │
          ├──────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
          │  CVE-2019-11247  │ HIGH     │ < 1.13.9,       │ RBAC bypass: access  │
          │                  │  8.1     │ < 1.14.5        │ cluster-scoped res   │
          │                  │          │                 │ as namespace-scoped  │
          ├──────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
          │  CVE-2021-25741  │ HIGH     │ < 1.19.15,      │ Symlink attack:      │
          │                  │  8.1     │ < 1.20.11,      │ hostPath volume      │
          │                  │          │ < 1.21.5        │ escapes restriction  │
          ├──────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
          │  CVE-2022-3294   │ HIGH     │ < 1.25.4,       │ Node address type    │
          │                  │  7.6     │ < 1.24.8        │ confusion → MITM     │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.2  Upgrade Strategy for a Running Cluster
          - Always upgrade control plane before worker nodes
          - Use kubeadm upgrade plan to see available versions
          - Drain nodes before upgrading worker node components
          - Test in staging before upgrading production

          [SAFE CLUSTER UPGRADE SEQUENCE — step by step]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  Phase 1: PREPARE                                                   │
          │  □ kubeadm upgrade plan → shows available versions                 │
          │  □ Backup etcd: etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd-pre-upgrade.db  │
          │  □ Check addon compatibility (Calico, Ingress, cert-manager)        │
          │  □ Read release notes for breaking changes                          │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Phase 2: UPGRADE CONTROL PLANE (one node at a time)               │
          │  □ apt-get update && apt-get install kubeadm=1.29.x-00            │
          │  □ kubeadm upgrade apply v1.29.x                                   │
          │  □ apt-get install kubelet=1.29.x-00 kubectl=1.29.x-00            │
          │  □ systemctl restart kubelet                                        │
          │  □ kubectl get nodes → control plane shows new version             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Phase 3: UPGRADE WORKER NODES (one at a time)                     │
          │  □ kubectl drain <worker> --ignore-daemonsets                       │
          │  □ SSH to worker: apt-get install kubeadm kubelet kubectl          │
          │  □ kubeadm upgrade node                                             │
          │  □ systemctl restart kubelet                                        │
          │  □ kubectl uncordon <worker>                                        │
          │  □ Verify: kubectl get nodes → all nodes show new version          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.3  Security Patches vs Minor Upgrades
          - Patch release (1.28.1 → 1.28.5): security fixes, bug fixes
          - Minor release (1.28 → 1.29): new features + deprecations
          - Always apply patch releases immediately (they fix CVEs)
          - Plan minor releases with 1-2 version lag from latest

          [K8s RELEASE CYCLE — when to act]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  PATCH RELEASE (1.29.0 → 1.29.5)                                   │
          │  ├─ Security fixes, bug fixes only                                  │
          │  ├─ Usually released every 4-6 weeks                                │
          │  └─ ACTION: Apply IMMEDIATELY when CVE is in the release notes     │
          │                                                                      │
          │  MINOR RELEASE (1.28 → 1.29 → 1.30)                               │
          │  ├─ New features, API changes, some deprecations                    │
          │  ├─ Released approximately every 4 months                           │
          │  └─ ACTION: Test in staging → upgrade within 6-8 months            │
          │                                                                      │
          │  EOL (End of Life: running version N-3 or older)                   │
          │  ├─ No more patches, including CRITICAL CVE fixes                  │
          │  └─ ACTION: URGENT upgrade — you are running known vulnerabilities │
          │                                                                      │
          │  SUPPORT MATRIX (as of 2025):                                       │
          │  ✅ 1.31 — fully supported                                          │
          │  ✅ 1.30 — supported                                                │
          │  ✅ 1.29 — supported                                                │
          │  ❌ 1.28 — end of life → upgrade required                          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.4  Version Skew — Why Upgrade Order Is a Security Rule, Not a Suggestion
         - Kubernetes enforces a strict VERSION SKEW POLICY: components may only be
           a few minor versions apart from kube-apiserver. Violate it and you get
           undefined behavior, silent RBAC/admission gaps, or a cluster that
           refuses to start — all of which are security problems, not just bugs.
         - The rule of thumb: the API server is always the NEWEST component.
           Everything else is the same version or OLDER, never newer.
         - This is why Phase 2 (control plane) always comes before Phase 3 (worker
           nodes) in the upgrade sequence above — upgrading a kubelet past the API
           server version breaks the skew policy immediately.

         [VERSION SKEW POLICY — how far each component may lag the API server]
         ┌────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ COMPONENT              │ ALLOWED VERSION RELATIVE TO kube-apiserver    │
         ├────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ kube-apiserver (HA)    │ within 1 minor version of each other          │
         │ kube-controller-mgr /  │ same minor OR up to 1 version OLDER           │
         │ kube-scheduler         │ (never newer than the API server)             │
         │ kubelet                │ same minor OR up to 3 versions OLDER          │
         │                        │ (never newer than the API server)             │
         │ kube-proxy             │ same as kubelet's rule                        │
         │ kubectl                │ within 1 minor version, older OR newer        │
         └────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [UPGRADE ORDER — why it must flow in exactly this direction]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. etcd            (back up first, upgrade if needed)               │
         │        │                                                             │
         │        ▼                                                             │
         │  2. kube-apiserver  ← becomes the NEW ceiling every other            │
         │        │              component is measured against                 │
         │        ▼                                                             │
         │  3. controller-manager, scheduler   (same/older than apiserver)      │
         │        │                                                             │
         │        ▼                                                             │
         │  4. kubelet, kube-proxy on EACH worker node, one at a time,          │
         │     draining before and uncordoning after                            │
         │        │                                                             │
         │        ▼                                                             │
         │  5. kubectl / CI tooling                                             │
         │                                                                      │
         │  ❌ Upgrading a kubelet BEFORE the API server = violates skew policy │
         │     → node may be marked NotReady, or worse, silently miss new       │
         │       admission/PSS behavior the newer API server expects            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         SECURITY ANGLE: an out-of-skew kubelet can silently fail to enforce a
         SecurityContext field the API server assumes it understands, or reject
         valid pods it doesn't recognize yet — either way you lose the guarantee
         that "what you configured is what's enforced." Checking skew is a quick,
         high-value exam habit: kubectl version --short (control plane) and
         kubectl get nodes -o wide (kubelet versions) before touching anything.
         [CODE: kubectl version --short + kubectl get nodes -o wide skew check]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 4: SYSTEM HARDENING 🎯🔐                                            🟡
CKS Domain 3: 10% of exam
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 13: Reducing the OS Attack Surface — Node Hardening
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    13.1  The Node OS Is Your Last Line of Defense
          - If a container escapes its sandbox, it lands on the node OS
          - A hardened node OS makes it much harder for attackers to persist
          - CIS Benchmarks also cover the OS level (Linux Security Benchmark)
          - Focus areas: unused packages, open ports, IAM, filesystem permissions
          [DIAGRAM: Container escape → hits hardened OS → limited blast radius]

    13.2  Removing Unused Services and Packages
          - Every service/package = a potential attack surface
          - List all running services: systemctl list-units --type=service
          - Stop and disable services you don't need
          - Uninstall unnecessary packages: apt remove / yum remove
          [CODE: Auditing and removing unnecessary services on a Kubernetes node]

          [COMMON UNNECESSARY SERVICES ON K8s NODES — to check and remove]
          | Service         | Why it might be installed          | Action on K8s node |
          | apache2/nginx   | Web server — not needed on node    | Remove             |
          | vsftpd          | FTP server                         | Remove             |
          | avahi-daemon    | mDNS discovery — not needed        | Remove             |
          | cups            | Printing service                   | Remove             |
          | snapd           | Package manager — extra surface    | Evaluate           |
          | docker (old)    | K8s 1.24+ uses containerd          | Remove docker only |

    13.3  Limiting Open Ports and Network Interfaces
          - Check open ports: ss -tlnp or netstat -tlnp
          - Only required ports should be listening
          - Use iptables/ufw/firewalld to block unnecessary ports
          [TABLE: Required ports for Kubernetes control plane and workers]

          [REQUIRED KUBERNETES PORTS — what should and should not be open]
          | Component         | Port(s)    | Who needs access          | Allow from      |
          | kube-apiserver    | 6443       | All cluster components    | Admin IPs only  |
          | etcd              | 2379-2380  | API server, etcd peers    | Control plane   |
          | kubelet           | 10250      | API server, monitoring    | Control plane   |
          | kube-proxy        | 10256      | Health checks             | Internal only   |
          | NodePort services | 30000-32767| External users (optional) | Restricted      |
          | CNI (Calico)      | 179 (BGP)  | Other nodes               | Cluster nodes   |

    13.4  User and IAM Hardening on Nodes
          - Kubernetes service accounts ≠ Linux user accounts
          - Create dedicated Linux users for node admins (no shared root)
          - Disable direct root login: PermitRootLogin no in sshd_config
          - Use SSH key-based auth only — disable password login
          - Rotate SSH keys regularly; remove keys for departed team members
          [CODE: Hardening sshd_config for Kubernetes nodes]
          [CODE: Creating an admin Linux user with sudo and SSH key]

    13.5  Minimizing External Access to the Network
          - CKS curriculum wording: "minimize external access to the network" —
            nodes should never expose more than the cluster actually needs
          - Nodes get NO public IPs: all inbound traffic enters through a cloud
            load balancer → ingress controller; admins enter via bastion/VPN only
          - Host firewall (ufw/firewalld) on every node: default deny inbound,
            allow only the K8s port matrix (6443/2379-80/10250/NodePort range)
            from the specific sources that need them
          - Watch for accidental exposure: hostNetwork: true pods, hostPort
            mappings, NodePort services open to 0.0.0.0/0, kubelet 10250 public
          - Audit from outside: nmap the node's public address — anything open
            beyond 80/443 on the LB is a finding

          [EXTERNAL EXPOSURE — what should face the internet (and what must not)]
          ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ ALLOWED TO FACE INTERNET   │ MUST NEVER FACE INTERNET                 │
          ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Cloud LB :443 (TLS)        │ 6443 API server (admin CIDR/VPN only)    │
          │ (→ ingress-nginx)          │ 10250 kubelet API (node/CP only)         │
          │ Bastion :22 (admin IPs,    │ 2379/2380 etcd (control plane only)      │
          │ key-auth, MFA)             │ 30000-32767 NodePorts (use LB instead)   │
          │                            │ 10256/10257/10259 kube components        │
          └────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: ufw default-deny ruleset for an anihpj worker node]
          [CODE: external nmap audit + interpreting the results]


Chapter 14: AppArmor — Application-Level Sandboxing
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    14.1  What Is AppArmor and Why Does It Matter for Containers?
          - AppArmor = Linux security module that restricts program capabilities
          - Creates per-process security profiles: what files, networks, capabilities it can access
          - Without AppArmor: a container with a vulnerability can do anything on the node
          - With AppArmor: even a compromised container can only do what its profile allows
          [ANALOGY: AppArmor = a security clearance badge — you can only enter rooms on your badge]

          [APPARMOR CONCEPT — before and after]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  📦 Container WITHOUT AppArmor                           │
          │                                                          │
          │  Django process (anihpj) can:                           │
          │  ❌ read /etc/shadow (password file)                    │
          │  ❌ write to /proc (kernel interfaces)                  │
          │  ❌ open raw network sockets                            │
          │  ❌ access /dev devices                                 │
          │                                                          │
          │  📦 Container WITH AppArmor profile                      │
          │                                                          │
          │  Django process (anihpj) can ONLY:                      │
          │  ✅ read /app/ directory (its own code)                 │
          │  ✅ write to /tmp/ and /app/media/                      │
          │  ✅ connect TCP to postgres:5432                        │
          │  ✅ connect TCP to redis:6379                           │
          │  🚫 everything else → DENIED                            │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.2  AppArmor Modes — Disabled, Complain, Enforce
          - Disabled: AppArmor off for this process
          - Complain (learning): logs violations but does NOT block them
          - Enforce: blocks all actions not allowed by the profile
          - Start with Complain to learn what your app actually needs
          [CODE: Checking AppArmor status — aa-status]
          [CODE: Loading a profile and switching between modes]

          [APPARMOR PROFILE WORKFLOW — from zero to enforce]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  STEP 1: GENERATE (auto-profile in complain mode)                   │
          │  $ aa-genprof /usr/bin/python3                                      │
          │  → Runs app, records all file/network/cap accesses                  │
          │  → Writes draft profile to /etc/apparmor.d/                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  STEP 2: TEST in COMPLAIN mode (log violations, don't block)        │
          │  $ aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/anihpj-django                        │
          │  → Run anihpj normally, check syslog for denied events             │
          │  $ journalctl -k | grep apparmor | grep ALLOWED                     │
          │  → Add needed paths/permissions to the profile                      │
          │                                                                      │
          │  STEP 3: ENFORCE (block violations)                                 │
          │  $ aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/anihpj-django                         │
          │  → Profile now active: violations → blocked + logged               │
          │                                                                      │
          │  STEP 4: VERIFY profile loaded on all K8s nodes                    │
          │  $ ssh node01 aa-status | grep anihpj-django                        │
          │  → Should show: anihpj-django (enforce)                            │
          │                                                                      │
          │  STEP 5: APPLY to Kubernetes pod spec                               │
          │  securityContext:                                                    │
          │    appArmorProfile:                                                  │
          │      type: Localhost                                                 │
          │      localhostProfile: anihpj-django                               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [APPARMOR MODES COMPARED]
          ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  MODE          │  BEHAVIOUR                                        │
          ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  disabled      │  No AppArmor for this process. No restrictions. │
          │  complain      │  Logs violations to syslog. Does NOT block them. │
          │                │  Use during: profile development / testing       │
          │  enforce       │  Blocks ALL actions not in the profile.          │
          │                │  Use in: production always                       │
          └────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.3  Writing AppArmor Profiles for anihpj
          - Profile for Django/Gunicorn: allow app files, deny system files
          - Profile for postgres: allow data directory, deny everything else
          - Use aa-genprof to auto-generate a profile in complain mode
          [CODE: AppArmor profile for anihpj Django container]
          [CODE: Loading profile to a node and applying to a pod]

          [APPARMOR PROFILE — anihpj Django example]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  #include <tunables/global>                               │
          │                                                          │
          │  profile anihpj-django flags=(attach_disconnected) {     │
          │    #include <abstractions/base>                          │
          │    #include <abstractions/python>                        │
          │                                                          │
          │    # Allow app code files (read only)                    │
          │    /app/** r,                                            │
          │                                                          │
          │    # Allow media/static writes                           │
          │    /app/media/** rw,                                     │
          │    /tmp/** rw,                                           │
          │                                                          │
          │    # Allow network connections                           │
          │    network tcp,                                          │
          │                                                          │
          │    # Deny everything else                                │
          │    deny /etc/shadow r,                                   │
          │    deny /proc/** rw,                                     │
          │  }                                                       │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.4  Applying AppArmor in Kubernetes
          - AppArmor profile MUST be loaded on EVERY node where the pod can run
          - Applied via pod annotation (pre-1.30) or securityContext (1.30+)
          - CKS exam: know both annotation method and securityContext method
          [CODE: Pod spec with AppArmor annotation (pre-1.30)]
          [CODE: Pod spec with securityContext.appArmorProfile (1.30+)]
          [CODE: Verifying AppArmor is active inside a running pod]

          [APPARMOR IN KUBERNETES — TWO WAYS TO APPLY]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  METHOD 1: Annotation (pre-1.30, still widely seen in CKS exam)     │
          │                                                                      │
          │  metadata:                                                           │
          │    annotations:                                                      │
          │      container.apparmor.security.beta.kubernetes.io/anihpj:         │
          │        localhost/anihpj-django                                      │
          │  # format: container.<name>: runtime-default | localhost/<profile>  │
          │                                                                      │
          │  METHOD 2: securityContext (1.30+ native field)                     │
          │                                                                      │
          │  spec:                                                               │
          │    containers:                                                       │
          │    - name: anihpj                                                   │
          │      securityContext:                                                │
          │        appArmorProfile:                                              │
          │          type: Localhost          # or: RuntimeDefault, Unconfined  │
          │          localhostProfile: anihpj-django                            │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ⚠️  EXAM TIP: Profile must be loaded on the NODE before use        │
          │     kubectl get pod -o jsonpath='{.metadata.annotations}'           │
          │     → verify apparmor annotation is set                             │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.5  Official Node-Loading Procedure — the Steps Between "Write" and "Use"
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/tutorials/security/apparmor: the
            Kubernetes scheduler is NOT AppArmor-aware. Writing a profile and
            referencing it in a pod spec is only half the job — the profile must
            ALSO be loaded into the kernel on EVERY node the pod might land on,
            BEFORE the pod is scheduled there, or the kubelet rejects the pod with
            "apparmor profile not found".
          - Load method: apparmor_parser (not kubectl, not any K8s API call — this
            is a node-level Linux operation, typically done via a DaemonSet or
            config management, since it must run on every node).
          - Persistent storage convention: /etc/apparmor.d/ on the node filesystem
            (so the profile survives reboots and is picked up by AppArmor tooling).
          - Prerequisite check: the AppArmor kernel module must be enabled — most
            CKS exam clusters run Ubuntu where it's on by default, but always verify.

          [THE FULL NODE-LOADING SEQUENCE]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  0. Prerequisite — is AppArmor even enabled on this node?            │
          │     cat /sys/module/apparmor/parameters/enabled                     │
          │     → must print: Y                                                  │
          │                                                                      │
          │  1. Write the profile to /etc/apparmor.d/ (persists across reboots)  │
          │     /etc/apparmor.d/anihpj-django:                                  │
          │       #include <tunables/global>                                    │
          │       profile anihpj-django flags=(attach_disconnected) {           │
          │         #include <abstractions/base>                                │
          │         file,                                                       │
          │         deny /etc/**       w,     ← block writes to /etc            │
          │         deny /bin/**       w,     ← block writes to /bin            │
          │       }                                                             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  2. Load it into the kernel (run on EVERY node — DaemonSet/Ansible)  │
          │     sudo apparmor_parser -q /etc/apparmor.d/anihpj-django           │
          │                                                                      │
          │  3. Verify it's loaded and active                                   │
          │     cat /sys/kernel/security/apparmor/profiles | sort               │
          │     → should list: anihpj-django (enforce)                          │
          │                                                                      │
          │  4. NOW reference it in the pod spec (14.4) and schedule the pod    │
          │                                                                      │
          │  5. Verify the RUNNING container actually has it applied            │
          │     kubectl exec anihpj -- cat /proc/1/attr/current                 │
          │     → should print: anihpj-django (enforce)                         │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          ⚠️  Skipping step 0-2 on even ONE node is the #1 real-world AppArmor
          failure: the pod schedules fine on nodes WITH the profile, then gets
          stuck Pending/CreateContainerError the moment the scheduler places it on
          a node that never had apparmor_parser run. Always load on ALL nodes.
          [CODE: DaemonSet that loads the anihpj-django profile on every node at boot]


Chapter 15: Seccomp — Filtering System Calls
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    15.1  What Is Seccomp and Why Does It Protect Containers?
          - Seccomp = Secure Computing Mode (Linux kernel feature)
          - Every program communicates with the kernel via system calls (syscalls)
          - Seccomp filters which syscalls a process is ALLOWED to make
          - A container should only need ~50 syscalls — Linux has 300+ available
          - Block the other 250+ → massive reduction in kernel attack surface
          [DIAGRAM: App → makes syscall → Seccomp filter → allow/deny → kernel]

          [SYSCALL ATTACK REDUCTION]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🐧 LINUX KERNEL HAS ~300+ SYSCALLS                     │
          │                                                          │
          │  A typical Django app uses ≈ 40-60 syscalls:            │
          │  read, write, open, close, socket, connect, accept...   │
          │                                                          │
          │  WITHOUT Seccomp:                                        │
          │  Attacker can use ANY of 300+ syscalls to:              │
          │    → ptrace another process                              │
          │    → mount filesystems                                   │
          │    → load kernel modules                                 │
          │    → create raw sockets                                  │
          │                                                          │
          │  WITH Seccomp (RuntimeDefault profile):                 │
          │  Only the ~60 needed syscalls are allowed               │
          │  240+ dangerous syscalls: blocked at kernel level        │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DANGEROUS SYSCALL EXAMPLES — what attackers use, what Seccomp blocks]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DANGEROUS SYSCALL  │ WHAT IT ENABLES                │ Blocked by   │
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  ptrace             │ Attach to another process,     │ RuntimeDefault│
         │                     │ read its memory                │              │
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  mount              │ Mount filesystems              │ RuntimeDefault│
         │                     │ (escape container isolation)   │              │
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  kexec_load         │ Replace running kernel         │ RuntimeDefault│
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  create_module      │ Load kernel module             │ RuntimeDefault│
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  setns              │ Join another namespace          │ RuntimeDefault│
         │                     │ (namespace escape)             │              │
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  unshare            │ Create new namespaces          │ RuntimeDefault│
         ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
         │  socket (raw)       │ Create raw network sockets     │ Custom profile│
         │                     │ (craft malicious packets)      │              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.2  Seccomp Profiles — RuntimeDefault and Custom
          - RuntimeDefault: provided by the container runtime (containerd/CRI-O)
          - Covers syscalls needed by most applications — safe default
          - Custom (Localhost): JSON file defining exact allowed/denied syscalls
          - Unconfined: no seccomp filter (insecure default for older clusters)
          [CODE: Seccomp profiles JSON format explained]
          [TABLE: RuntimeDefault vs Custom vs Unconfined comparison]

    15.3  Applying Seccomp in Kubernetes Pod Specs
          - Applied in pod's securityContext.seccompProfile
          - Types: RuntimeDefault, Localhost (custom profile), Unconfined
          - Localhost profiles must exist at /var/lib/kubelet/seccomp/ on nodes
          [CODE: Pod spec with RuntimeDefault seccomp profile]
          [CODE: Pod spec with custom localhost seccomp profile]
          [CODE: Deploying a custom seccomp profile for anihpj]

    15.4  AppArmor vs Seccomp — Know the Difference (CKS Exam Critical)
          [SIDE-BY-SIDE COMPARISON TABLE]
          | Feature         | AppArmor                       | Seccomp                        |
          | Controls        | File/network/capability access | Linux system calls (syscalls)  |
          | Level           | Application behaviour          | Kernel interface               |
          | Config location | Node filesystem + pod annotation| Pod securityContext            |
          | Profile format  | AppArmor profile syntax        | JSON file                      |
          | Distribution    | Ubuntu/Debian native           | All distros (kernel feature)   |
          | Complain mode   | Yes (great for learning)       | Limited                        |
          | CKS exam weight | Medium                         | Medium                         |

          [SECCOMP SYSCALL FILTERING — visual representation of how it works]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                          │
          │  Container process (e.g., Django/anihpj)                                 │
          │       │                                                                   │
          │       │ Makes a system call (e.g., open("/etc/shadow", O_RDONLY))        │
          │       ▼                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
          │  │  🛡️ SECCOMP FILTER (BPF program in kernel)                       │    │
          │  │                                                                   │    │
          │  │  Reads the Seccomp profile JSON:                                 │    │
          │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
          │  │  │  {                                                         │   │    │
          │  │  │    "defaultAction": "SCMP_ACT_ERRNO",                      │   │    │
          │  │  │    "syscalls": [                                            │   │    │
          │  │  │      {"names": ["read","write"], "action": "ALLOW"},      │   │    │
          │  │  │      {"names": ["mount","chmod"], "action": "ERRNO"}      │   │    │
          │  │  │    ]                                                        │   │    │
          │  │  │  }                                                          │   │    │
          │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
          │  │                              │                                    │    │
          │  │              ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐                   │    │
          │  │              │               │               │                    │    │
          │  │              ▼               ▼               ▼                    │    │
          │  │  ┌─────────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────────┐        │    │
          │  │  │  read(), write()│ │ mount(),     │ │  ANY OTHER      │        │    │
          │  │  │  open(), close()│ │ chmod(),     │ │  SYSCALL        │        │    │
          │  │  │  socket(), etc. │ │ ptrace()     │ │  (not in list)  │        │    │
          │  │  │                  │ │              │ │                  │        │    │
          │  │  │  ✅ ALLOWED      │ │  ❌ ERRNO    │ │  ❌ DEFAULT      │        │    │
          │  │  │  (returns 0)     │ │  (Permission │ │  (Errno)        │        │    │
          │  │  │                  │ │   denied)    │ │                  │        │    │
          │  │  └─────────────────┘ └─────────────┘ └─────────────────┘        │    │
          │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
          │                                                                          │
          │  💡 CKS EXAM TIP: RuntimeDefault uses Docker's default seccomp profile   │
          │     which blocks ~44 syscalls out of ~300+. This is the safest starting   │
          │     point and what you'll most likely use on the exam.                   │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.5  Official Seccomp Facts — Exact Paths, Exact JSON, One Hard Constraint
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/tutorials/security/seccomp: two
            precision corrections to 15.2-15.4 above, plus a hard constraint the
            exam loves to test.
          - PATH CORRECTION: the kubelet's seccomp root directory is
            /var/lib/kubelet/seccomp/ — but localhostProfile paths in the pod spec
            are RELATIVE to a profiles/ subdirectory beneath it. So a pod spec
            saying localhostProfile: anihpj-audit.json actually resolves on disk to
            /var/lib/kubelet/seccomp/profiles/anihpj-audit.json — always create
            that profiles/ subdirectory; a file placed directly in .../seccomp/
            without it will not be found.
          - JSON ACTION CORRECTION: raw seccomp profile JSON (the actual file on
            disk) requires the FULL libseccomp action names — "SCMP_ACT_ALLOW",
            "SCMP_ACT_ERRNO", "SCMP_ACT_LOG" — not the short forms "ALLOW"/"ERRNO"
            shown in the simplified diagram above (that diagram illustrates the
            CONCEPT; real profile files must use the SCMP_ACT_ prefix or the
            container runtime will reject the profile as invalid).
          - HARD CONSTRAINT: a privileged: true container CANNOT use a seccomp
            profile at all — it always runs Unconfined regardless of what
            seccompProfile.type says. This is exactly why "privileged: true" and
            "seccomp/AppArmor sandboxing" are mutually exclusive on the exam: if a
            question requires BOTH privileged access AND seccomp filtering, that's
            a trick — you cannot have both; the real fix is usually to avoid
            privileged: true entirely and grant specific capabilities instead (18.3).
          - Debugging workflow: start with defaultAction SCMP_ACT_LOG (audit mode,
            logs every syscall without blocking anything) to discover exactly which
            syscalls your app needs, THEN switch to SCMP_ACT_ERRNO as the default
            with an explicit ALLOW list built from what you observed.

          [CORRECTED PATHS AND JSON — copy these exactly]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ON-DISK LOCATION (note the profiles/ subdirectory):                 │
          │    /var/lib/kubelet/seccomp/profiles/anihpj-audit.json               │
          │                                                                      │
          │  POD SPEC (localhostProfile is relative to profiles/):               │
          │    securityContext:                                                  │
          │      seccompProfile:                                                 │
          │        type: Localhost                                              │
          │        localhostProfile: anihpj-audit.json  ← NOT the full path      │
          │                                                                      │
          │  VALID JSON (full SCMP_ACT_ prefix required):                        │
          │    {                                                                 │
          │      "defaultAction": "SCMP_ACT_ERRNO",                             │
          │      "architectures": ["SCMP_ARCH_X86_64"],                          │
          │      "syscalls": [                                                   │
          │        {"names": ["read","write","close","exit_group"],            │
          │         "action": "SCMP_ACT_ALLOW"}                                 │
          │      ]                                                              │
          │    }                                                                │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Audit-mode profile (SCMP_ACT_LOG) → observe syscalls → build the real ALLOW list]


Chapter 16: Open Ports, Packages & IAM Minimization
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    16.1  Minimizing the Node Attack Surface — The Full Checklist
          - Remove all packages not needed for Kubernetes operation
          - Disable all services not required (networking, printing, audio)
          - Close all ports not required by Kubernetes components
          - Remove all user accounts not needed for cluster operation
          - Restrict sudo access to only necessary users
          [AUDIT CHECKLIST: Node security audit before production]

          [NODE HARDENING CHECKLIST — 15-point audit]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ☐  1. Remove unnecessary packages:                                  │
          │       apt remove --purge git curl wget vim (if not needed)          │
          │  ☐  2. Disable unnecessary services:                                 │
          │       systemctl disable --now cups bluetooth avahi-daemon           │
          │  ☐  3. Close unnecessary ports:                                      │
          │       ss -tlnp → identify → firewall rule to block                   │
          │  ☐  4. Kernel hardening:                                             │
          │       sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=0 (if not routing)              │
          │  ☐  5. Restrict SSH:                                                 │
          │       PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, Port != 22     │
          │  ☐  6. File integrity monitoring:                                    │
          │       apt install aide → aide --init (CKS exam topic!)              │
          │  ☐  7. Remove compilers/build tools from production nodes:          │
          │       apt remove --purge build-essential gcc g++ make               │
          │  ☐  8. Kernel module restrictions:                                   │
          │       blacklist unnecessary kernel modules                           │
          │  ☐  9. Set up automatic security updates:                            │
          │       apt install unattended-upgrades                               │
          │  ☐ 10. Restrict cron/at to authorized users only                     │
          │  ☐ 11. Disable USB storage if not needed                             │
          │  ☐ 12. Set up log forwarding:                                        │
          │       journalctl → remote syslog server                              │
          │  ☐ 13. Configure auditd for system-level auditing (separate from K8s)│
          │  ☐ 14. Remove unnecessary users from passwd:                         │
          │       cat /etc/passwd | grep /home → review and remove               │
          │  ☐ 15. sudoers hardening:                                            │
          │       Defaults use_pty, Defaults logfile=/var/log/sudo.log          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.2  Essential Ports for Kubernetes — Know What Must Stay Open
          - Control Plane: 6443 (API), 2379-2380 (etcd), 10250 (kubelet), 10259 (scheduler)
          - Worker: 10250 (kubelet), 30000-32767 (NodePort), 10256 (kube-proxy)
          - CNI-specific: Calico 179 (BGP), Cilium 4240 (health), Flannel 8472 (VXLAN)
          [PORT DIAGRAM: Visual map of all required K8s ports per component]
          [TABLE: Port → Component → Required by → Allow from]

          [KUBERNETES PORT MAP — what needs to be open and why]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  CONTROL PLANE NODE                                              │
          │  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐ │
          │  │ Port         │ Component            │ Who Needs Access      │ │
          │  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤ │
          │  │ 6443/TCP     │ kube-apiserver       │ Everyone (kubectl,     │ │
          │  │              │                      │ kubelet, controllers)│ │
          │  │ 2379/TCP     │ etcd (client)        │ API server ONLY        │ │
          │  │ 2380/TCP     │ etcd (peer)          │ Other etcd nodes       │ │
          │  │ 10250/TCP    │ kubelet (API)        │ API server (for exec,  │ │
          │  │              │                      │ logs, metrics)        │ │
          │  │ 10259/TCP    │ kube-scheduler       │ Yourself (debug only)  │ │
          │  │ 10257/TCP    │ kube-controller-mgr  │ Yourself (debug only)  │ │
          │  └──────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘ │
          │                                                                   │
          │  WORKER NODE                                                     │
          │  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐ │
          │  │ Port         │ Component            │ Who Needs Access      │ │
          │  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤ │
          │  │ 10250/TCP    │ kubelet (API)       │ API server             │ │
          │  │ 30000-32767  │ NodePort services    │ External users         │ │
          │  │ 10256/TCP    │ kube-proxy health   │ Localhost only         │ │
          │  └──────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘ │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.3  Limiting Kernel Modules — Blacklisting Dangerous Ones
          - Kernel modules extend the kernel, adding more attack surface
          - Blacklist modules not needed: cramfs, freevxfs, usb-storage
          - CIS Benchmark requirement: certain modules MUST be blacklisted
          - On the CKS exam: know how to blacklist modules via modprobe.d
          [CODE: Blacklisting dangerous kernel modules]
          [CODE: Verifying modules are blocked]

          [DANGEROUS KERNEL MODULES — CIS-MANDATED BLACKLISTING]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # Create blacklist file: /etc/modprobe.d/cis-blacklist.conf         │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Prevent loading of unnecessary filesystems:                       │
          │  install cramfs /bin/true                                             │
          │  install freevxfs /bin/true                                           │
          │  install jffs2 /bin/true                                              │
          │  install hfs /bin/true                                                │
          │  install hfsplus /bin/true                                            │
          │  install squashfs /bin/true                                           │
          │  install udf /bin/true                                                │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Prevent loading of dangerous modules:                              │
          │  install usb-storage /bin/true                                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Verify:                                                            │
          │  $ modprobe --show-depends cramfs                                    │
          │  $ lsmod | grep -E "cramfs|freevxfs|usb-storage"                    │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.4  Cloud IAM for Kubernetes Nodes
          - AWS: EC2 instance profiles → minimal IAM roles for nodes
          - GCP: Service Account for GKE nodes with minimal scopes
          - Azure: Managed Identity for AKS nodes with minimal permissions
          - Never use admin-level cloud credentials on Kubernetes nodes
          [TABLE: Minimum cloud IAM permissions per provider for worker nodes]

          [CLOUD IAM LEAST PRIVILEGE — what each node REALLY needs]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  AWS EKS Node IAM (minimum):                                          │
          │  {                                                                   │
          │    "Action": [                                                        │
          │      "ecr:GetAuthorizationToken",        ← pull images from ECR      │
          │      "ecr:BatchCheckLayerAvailability",                               │
          │      "ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer",       ← download image layers      │
          │      "ec2:DescribeInstances",            ← node discovery             │
          │      "logs:CreateLogStream",             ← optional: CloudWatch logs  │
          │      "logs:PutLogEvents"                                              │
          │    ],                                                                 │
          │    "Resource": "*"                                                    │
          │  }                                                                   │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ❌ NEVER give nodes: s3:*, iam:*, ec2:*, rds:* (massive blast radius)│
          │                                                                      │
          │  GCP GKE Node Service Account (minimum):                              │
          │  roles/monitoring.metricWriter       ← metrics to Cloud Monitoring    │
          │  roles/logging.logWriter             ← logs to Cloud Logging          │
          │  roles/artifactregistry.reader       ← pull images from Artifact Reg.│
          │                                                                      │
          │  Azure AKS Node (minimum):                                            │
          │  AcrPull role assignment for container registry                       │
          │  Monitoring Metrics Publisher (for Azure Monitor)                     │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.5  etcd Security — Restricting Who Can Read the Cluster Brain
          - etcd stores ALL cluster state, including Secrets (if not encrypted)
          - Only the API server should ever connect to etcd
          - Use TLS between API server and etcd
          - Restrict etcd port (2379) with firewall to allow only API server IP
          - Enable etcd authentication for additional protection
          [CODE: etcd TLS flags and firewall rule example]

          [etcd SECURITY HARDENING — the cluster's most sensitive component]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  etcd HARDENING CHECKLIST:                                           │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ✅ TLS everywhere:                                                   │
          │     --cert-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt                  │
          │     --key-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key                   │
          │     --peer-cert-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/peer.crt              │
          │     --peer-key-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/peer.key               │
          │     --trusted-ca-file=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt               │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ✅ Firewall: port 2379 restricted to API server IP only             │
          │     ufw allow from 10.0.0.10 to any port 2379 proto tcp             │
          │     ufw default deny incoming                                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ✅ etcd data directory permissions:                                  │
          │     chmod 700 /var/lib/etcd                                          │
          │     chown etcd:etcd /var/lib/etcd                                    │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ✅ Encryption at rest (covers etcd):                                 │
          │     EncryptionConfiguration resource (see Chapter 19)               │
          │     --encryption-provider-config flag on API server                 │
          │                                                                      │
          │  ⚠️  NEVER expose etcd to the internet                              │
          │  ⚠️  NEVER share etcd certs between clusters                        │
          │  ⚠️  ALWAYS backup etcd before making changes                        │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.6  Linux Kernel Hardening Parameters (sysctl)
          - sysctl controls kernel parameters at runtime
          - Security-relevant parameters must be set on every node
          - CIS Benchmark specifies required kernel hardening values
          [CODE: Secure sysctl.conf for Kubernetes nodes]
          [CODE: Applying and verifying sysctl changes]

          [KERNEL HARDENING sysctl — CIS-required parameters]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # /etc/sysctl.d/99-kubernetes-cis.conf                               │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # IP forwarding (required for pod networking)                        │
          │  net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1                                              │
          │  net.ipv6.conf.all.forwarding = 1                                    │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Disable ICMP redirects (prevent MITM attacks)                      │
          │  net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects = 0                              │
          │  net.ipv4.conf.default.accept_redirects = 0                           │
          │  net.ipv6.conf.all.accept_redirects = 0                              │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Disable source packet routing                                     │
          │  net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_source_route = 0                           │
          │  net.ipv4.conf.default.accept_source_route = 0                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Enable TCP SYN cookie protection (DDoS mitigation)                │
          │  net.ipv4.tcp_syncookies = 1                                         │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Disable IP source routing                                         │
          │  net.ipv4.conf.all.send_redirects = 0                                │
          │  net.ipv4.conf.default.send_redirects = 0                             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Increase ASLR effectiveness (randomize memory addresses)          │
          │  kernel.randomize_va_space = 2                                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Apply: sysctl --system                                            │
          │  # Verify: sysctl -a | grep -E "redirect|forward|syncookie"          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.7  Verify Platform Binaries & Least-Privilege Cloud IAM
          - CKS curriculum wording: "verify platform binaries before deploying" —
