            never run a kubectl/kubeadm/kubelet you haven't checksum-verified
          - Every Kubernetes release publishes sha512 checksums; compare BEFORE
            installing: a tampered binary = total cluster compromise
          - Also verify: container runtime binaries, CNI plugins, etcd — anything
            downloaded onto a node
          - Cloud IAM side: node instance roles get the absolute minimum
            (registry pull + logs write); NO wildcard actions, NO admin roles;
            require IMDSv2 so pods can't trivially steal node credentials

          [BINARY VERIFICATION FLOW — do this for every downloaded binary]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  1. Download binary:                                                 │
          │     curl -LO https://dl.k8s.io/release/v1.31.0/bin/linux/amd64/kubectl│
          │  2. Download its published checksum:                                 │
          │     curl -LO https://dl.k8s.io/release/v1.31.0/bin/linux/amd64/       │
          │     kubectl.sha256                                                   │
          │  3. Verify (must print "kubectl: OK"):                               │
          │     echo "$(cat kubectl.sha256)  kubectl" | sha256sum --check        │
          │  4. Mismatch? → DELETE the binary, fetch from the official source,   │
          │     investigate the mirror. NEVER install on mismatch.               │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Exam variant: compare a RUNNING binary against the release hash:    │
          │     sha512sum /usr/bin/kubelet  vs  published CHANGELOG checksum     │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: sha512sum verification of kubeadm/kubelet/kubectl v1.31 binaries]
          [TABLE: Minimum IAM permissions per provider (ECR/GCR/ACR pull, logs, KMS decrypt) — nothing else]

Chapter 17: Pod Security Standards — Admission-Level Enforcement
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    17.1  What Are Pod Security Standards (PSS)?
          - PSS replaced PodSecurityPolicy (PSP) in Kubernetes 1.25
          - Three security levels: Privileged, Baseline, Restricted
          - Applied at the NAMESPACE level via labels
          - Three enforcement modes: enforce, audit, warn
          [TABLE: PSS levels and what they allow/deny]

          [PSS THREE LEVELS — what each allows]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  PRIVILEGED (most permissive — only for trusted system pods) │
          │  → No restrictions. Full host access allowed.               │
          │  → Use for: CNI plugins, node monitoring daemons            │
          │  → NEVER for: user workloads like anihpj                   │
          │                                                              │
          │  BASELINE (medium — prevents known privilege escalation)    │
          │  → Disables: hostNetwork, hostPID, hostIPC                  │
          │  → Disables: privileged containers                          │
          │  → Allows: running as any user, most capabilities           │
          │  → Use for: workloads that need some flexibility            │
          │                                                              │
          │  RESTRICTED (most secure — strongly recommended)            │
          │  → All Baseline restrictions, plus:                         │
          │  → Requires: runAsNonRoot: true                             │
          │  → Requires: seccompProfile: RuntimeDefault                 │
          │  → Disables: all Linux capabilities (drop ALL)              │
          │  → Requires: read-only root filesystem (where possible)     │
          │  → Use for: anihpj, jobpost, and all user workloads         │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.2  Applying PSS to Namespaces
          - Labels on the namespace define which level applies
          - Three modes: enforce (block), audit (log), warn (UI warning)
          - You can mix modes: enforce=baseline, warn=restricted, audit=restricted
          [CODE: Namespace labels for all three PSS modes]
          [CODE: Testing that a violating pod is blocked by PSS]

    17.3  PSS for the anihpj Namespace
          - Apply restricted enforcement to anihpj-prod namespace
          - Apply baseline enforcement to anihpj-dev (more flexibility)
          - Test that anihpj pod spec complies with restricted level
          - Fix non-compliant fields: add runAsNonRoot, seccomp, drop ALL
          [CODE: Complete namespace PSS labels + compliant pod spec for anihpj]

          [PSS SELECTION FLOWCHART — which level should you choose?]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                   │
          │  START: I need to secure my namespace                             │
          │         │                                                         │
          │         ▼                                                         │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  Q1: Does this workload NEED host-level access?             ││
          │  │      (hostNetwork, hostPID, hostIPC, privileged?)           ││
          │  │  YES → Use PRIVILEGED (but ask: is there another way?)      ││
          │  │  NO  ↓                                                       ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │         │                                                         │
          │         ▼                                                         │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  Q2: Can the workload run as a non-root user?               ││
          │  │      (Does the Dockerfile have USER 1000?)                  ││
          │  │  NO  → Use BASELINE (allows root, blocks known exploits)    ││
          │  │  YES ↓                                                       ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │         │                                                         │
          │         ▼                                                         │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  Q3: Can the container run with read-only root filesystem?  ││
          │  │      (Writable dirs handled via emptyDir / PVC mounts?)     ││
          │  │  NO  → Use BASELINE (allows writable root fs)               ││
          │  │  YES ↓                                                       ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │         │                                                         │
          │         ▼                                                         │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  Q4: Can you drop ALL Linux capabilities?                   ││
          │  │      (Does the app need NET_ADMIN, SYS_ADMIN, etc.?)        ││
          │  │  NO  → Use BASELINE + specific capabilities allowed         ││
          │  │  YES ↓                                                       ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │         │                                                         │
          │         ▼                                                         │
          │  🎉  Use RESTRICTED — Maximum security for this workload!       │
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  RECOMMENDED FOR ANIHPJ:                                     ││
          │  │                                                               ││
          │  │  namespace: anihpj-prod                                      ││
          │  │    → RESTRICTED (production serving public job listings)     ││
          │  │                                                               ││
          │  │  namespace: anihpj-dev                                       ││
          │  │    → BASELINE (developers need some flexibility to debug)   ││
          │  │                                                               ││
          │  │  namespace: kube-system                                      ││
          │  │    → PRIVILEGED (CNI plugins, node exporters NEED host)      ││
          │  │    → But: apply NetworkPolicy to restrict who reaches them   ││
          │  │                                                               ││
          │  │  namespace: anihpj-monitoring                                ││
          │  │    → BASELINE (Prometheus + Grafana pods are trusted)        ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [PSS THREE LEVELS — side-by-side visual comparison]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                          │
          │  ┌─────────────────────┐ ┌─────────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐  │
          │  │  🔴 PRIVILEGED       │ │  🟡 BASELINE         │ │  🟢 RESTRICTED    │  │
          │  │  (Most permissive)   │ │  (Moderate security) │ │  (Maximum security)│  │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤ ├─────────────────────┤ ├───────────────────┤  │
          │  │                     │ │                     │ │                    │  │
          │  │  ✅ Allowed:        │ │  ❌ Blocked:        │ │  ❌ Blocked:       │  │
          │  │  • root user        │ │  • hostNetwork      │ │  • ALL in Baseline │  │
          │  │  • ALL caps         │ │  • hostPID          │ │  • runAsNonRoot=   │  │
          │  │  • hostNetwork      │ │  • hostIPC          │ │    false           │  │
          │  │  • hostPID          │ │  • privileged=true  │ │  • volume types:   │  │
          │  │  • hostIPC          │ │  • HostPath vols    │ │    HostPath, NFS,  │  │
          │  │  • ANY volume type  │ │  • SELinux options  │ │    etc.             │  │
          │  │  • ALL seccomp      │ │  • ProcMount=Unmask │ │  • ANY capabilities│  │
          │  │  • ALL AppArmor     │ │  • AppArmor options │ │  • seccomp!=set    │  │
          │  │                     │ │                     │ │  • privilegeEscal. │  │
          │  │  Use for:           │ │  Use for:           │ │  • SYN_ADMIN caps  │  │
          │  │  • kube-system pods │ │  • dev namespaces   │ │  • non-standard    │  │
          │  │  • CNI plugins      │ │  • trusted workloads│ │    procMount       │  │
          │  │  • storage drivers  │ │                     │ │  • AppArmor!=set   │  │
          │  │                     │ │                     │ │                    │  │
          │  │  ⚠️  NEVER use       │ │  ✅ Good starting   │ │  Use for:           │  │
          │  │  for user apps      │ │  point for apps     │ │  • production apps │  │
          │  │                     │ │                     │ │  • anihpj-prod     │  │
          │  └─────────────────────┘ └─────────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘  │
          │                                                                          │
          │  🔒 ENFORCEMENT MODES (can combine all three):                            │
          │  enforce = block the pod (hard stop)    │  audit = log violation only    │
          │  warn = show warning in API response    │  (use audit+warn first!)       │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.4  Official PSS Field-by-Field Reference — Exact Fields, Exact Values
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/security/pod-security-standards:
            the complete, exact field list per level. The exam checks specific
            fields, not just "is it restricted" — memorize this table, not just the
            concept.
          - ⚠️  PRECISION CORRECTION to 17.1: Seccomp restriction (blocking
            seccompProfile.type: Unconfined) is enforced starting at BASELINE, not
            only at Restricted. What Restricted actually ADDS beyond Baseline is
            just FOUR things: an allowed-volume-types whitelist,
            allowPrivilegeEscalation must be false, runAsNonRoot must be true, and
            (since v1.23) runAsUser must not be 0. Everything else — host
            namespaces, privileged, capabilities.add allowlist, hostPath, host
            ports, AppArmor, SELinux, procMount, seccomp, sysctls — is already
            locked down at Baseline. Restricted = Baseline + those 4 extra rules.

          [BASELINE — the complete list of what it restricts]
          ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ CONTROL                  │ ALLOWED VALUE                             │
          ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Host Namespaces          │ hostNetwork/hostPID/hostIPC: unset, false │
          │ Privileged Containers    │ securityContext.privileged: unset, false  │
          │ Capabilities (add)       │ unset, OR only from: AUDIT_WRITE, CHOWN,  │
          │                          │ DAC_OVERRIDE, FOWNER, FSETID, KILL,       │
          │                          │ MKNOD, NET_BIND_SERVICE, SETFCAP, SETGID, │
          │                          │ SETPCAP, SETUID, SYS_CHROOT               │
          │ HostPath Volumes         │ spec.volumes[*].hostPath: unset           │
          │ Host Ports               │ containers[*].ports[*].hostPort: unset, 0 │
          │ AppArmor                 │ unset, RuntimeDefault, Localhost          │
          │ SELinux type             │ unset, container_t, container_init_t,     │
          │                          │ container_kvm_t                           │
          │ /proc Mount Type         │ procMount: unset, Default                 │
          │ Seccomp                  │ seccompProfile.type: unset, RuntimeDefault│
          │                          │ Localhost   (Unconfined is BLOCKED here)  │
          │ Sysctls                  │ unset, OR only kernel/net.ipv4 safe list  │
          └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [RESTRICTED — ONLY the 4 rules it adds ON TOP of everything in Baseline]
          ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ CONTROL                  │ REQUIRED VALUE                            │
          ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Volume Types             │ MUST be one of: configMap, csi,           │
          │                          │ downwardAPI, emptyDir, ephemeral,         │
          │                          │ persistentVolumeClaim, projected, secret  │
          │ allowPrivilegeEscalation │ MUST be false                             │
          │ runAsNonRoot             │ MUST be true (pod OR every container)     │
          │ runAsUser (v1.23+)       │ MUST NOT be 0                             │
          └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [ADMISSION LABEL SYNTAX — exact format]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  pod-security.kubernetes.io/<MODE>: <LEVEL>                          │
          │                                                                      │
          │  <MODE>  = enforce | audit | warn                                    │
          │  <LEVEL> = privileged | baseline | restricted                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  metadata:                                                           │
          │    labels:                                                           │
          │      pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted                  │
          │      pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted                    │
          │      pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted                     │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: A pod spec that passes Baseline but FAILS Restricted — showing exactly which field fails]


Chapter 18: Security Contexts — Locking Down Containers
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    18.1  What Is a Security Context?
          - Security context: per-pod or per-container security settings in the YAML
          - Controls: user ID, group ID, filesystem, Linux capabilities, privilege
          - Pod-level: applies to all containers in the pod
          - Container-level: overrides pod-level for that specific container
          [CODE: SecurityContext fields — full annotated reference]

    18.2  Running as Non-Root — The Most Important Setting
          - By default: containers run as root (UID 0) inside the container
          - Root in container ≈ root on node (if container escapes)
          - runAsNonRoot: true → Kubernetes refuses to start root containers
          - runAsUser: 1000 → specify exact UID (matches Dockerfile USER instruction)
          [CODE: anihpj pod spec with runAsNonRoot and runAsUser]
          [CODE: Dockerfile USER instruction to match the pod spec UID]

          [ROOT vs NON-ROOT CONTAINER — security impact]
          ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
          │  ❌ ROOT CONTAINER           │   │  ✅ NON-ROOT CONTAINER       │
          │                              │   │                              │
          │  securityContext: {}         │   │  securityContext:            │
          │  → runs as UID 0 (root)      │   │    runAsNonRoot: true        │
          │                              │   │    runAsUser: 1000           │
          │  Container escape:           │   │    runAsGroup: 1000          │
          │  → attacker has root on node │   │                              │
          │  → can read all files        │   │  Container escape:           │
          │  → can install rootkits      │   │  → attacker is UID 1000     │
          │  → can modify system files   │   │  → very limited permissions │
          │  → can access host network   │   │  → cannot install rootkits  │
          └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

    18.3  Linux Capabilities — Dropping What You Don't Need
          - Linux capabilities split root power into granular privileges
          - Default container: gets ~14 capabilities (too many)
          - Best practice: drop ALL capabilities, add back only what's needed
          - Most web apps (like Django): need zero additional capabilities
          [TABLE: Common Linux capabilities — what they allow and who needs them]
          [CODE: Container spec with drop: ["ALL"] and add: [] for anihpj]

          [LINUX CAPABILITIES — required vs unnecessary for anihpj]
          | Capability      | Allows                               | Needed by anihpj? |
          | CAP_NET_ADMIN   | Modify network (iptables, routes)    | ❌ No             |
          | CAP_SYS_ADMIN   | Many privileged ops (very dangerous) | ❌ No             |
          | CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE | Bind ports < 1024              | ❌ Use port 8000  |
          | CAP_CHOWN       | Change file ownership               | ❌ No             |
          | CAP_KILL        | Send signals to other processes      | ❌ No             |
          | CAP_DAC_READ_SEARCH | Bypass file permission checks   | ❌ No             |
          | (none needed)   | Django on port 8000, no privileges  | ✅ Drop ALL       |

    18.4  Privilege Escalation — The allowPrivilegeEscalation Flag
          - allowPrivilegeEscalation: allows a child process to gain more privileges
          - Example: setuid binary that temporarily elevates to root
          - Always set to false for all user-facing containers
          [CODE: Pod spec with allowPrivilegeEscalation: false]

    18.5  Read-Only Root Filesystem — Immutability in Practice
          - readOnlyRootFilesystem: true → container cannot write to /
          - Forces all writes to explicitly mounted volumes or emptyDir
          - Prevents attackers from modifying binaries inside the container
          - Django apps: writable paths needed → /tmp, /app/media, /app/staticfiles
          [CODE: anihpj pod with readOnlyRootFilesystem and appropriate volume mounts]

          [CONTAINER ESCAPE & DEFENSE TREE — what attackers try, what stops them]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                   │
          │  🔴 ATTACKER INSIDE CONTAINER — tries to escape to host node     │
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  PATH 1: Exploit kernel vulnerability                      ││
          │  │  Attacker runs: exploit_linux_kernel.py                     ││
          │  │  → Needs dangerous syscalls (ptrace, mount, kexec_load)     ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: Seccomp profile blocks those syscalls      ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: gVisor/Kata sandbox (different kernel)    ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  PATH 2: Mount host filesystem                              ││
          │  │  Attacker runs: mount /dev/sda1 /mnt/host                   ││
          │  │  → Reads /etc/shadow from host node                         ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: cap drop ALL (no CAP_SYS_ADMIN)            ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: privileged: false (no host mounts)         ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  PATH 3: Access host network                                ││
          │  │  Attacker runs: tcpdump on host network interface           ││
          │  │  → Sniffs traffic of other pods                             ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: hostNetwork: false (pod has own namespace) ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: cap drop ALL (no CAP_NET_RAW)              ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  PATH 4: Write malicious binary                             ││
          │  │  Attacker writes: /usr/bin/backdoored-gunicorn              ││
          │  │  → Replaces the app binary with malware                     ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: readOnlyRootFilesystem: true (write fails) ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: AppArmor blocks writes to /usr/bin/        ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  PATH 5: Privilege escalation via setuid binary             ││
          │  │  Attacker runs: /bin/setuid-root-backdoor                   ││
          │  │  → Child process runs as root (UID 0)                       ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: allowPrivilegeEscalation: false             ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: runAsNonRoot: true (pod starts as non-root) ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                   │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  PATH 6: Access Docker socket                               ││
          │  │  Attacker mounts: /var/run/docker.sock                      ││
          │  │  → Creates new privileged container on host                 ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: never mount docker.sock in user pods!      ││
          │  │  🛡️  STOPPED BY: PSS Restricted (no hostPath)              ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                   │
          │  🏁  Every escape path is blocked by at least TWO layers.        │
          │      Defense-in-depth: even if one layer fails, another stops it. │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.6  Complete Security Context for anihpj — The Hardened Pod Spec
          [CODE: Full anihpj pod spec with all security context fields applied]

          [COMPLETE HARDENED SecurityContext — anihpj production pod]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  spec:                                                               │
          │    securityContext:              ← POD-LEVEL (applies to all ctrs)  │
          │      runAsNonRoot: true          ← refuse to run as root            │
          │      runAsUser: 1000             ← run as UID 1000                  │
          │      runAsGroup: 1000            ← primary group GID 1000           │
          │      fsGroup: 1000              ← volume ownership                  │
          │      seccompProfile:             ← kernel syscall filter            │
          │        type: RuntimeDefault      ← use runtime's default profile    │
          │                                                                      │
          │    containers:                                                       │
          │    - name: anihpj                                                   │
          │      image: registry.anihpj.com/anihpj:v2                          │
          │      securityContext:            ← CONTAINER-LEVEL (overrides above)│
          │        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false  ← no setuid escalation     │
          │        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true     ← immutable container fs   │
          │        capabilities:                                                 │
          │          drop: ["ALL"]           ← remove all linux capabilities    │
          │        appArmorProfile:          ← AppArmor sandbox                 │
          │          type: Localhost                                             │
          │          localhostProfile: anihpj-django                            │
          │                                                                      │
          │      volumeMounts:                                                   │
          │      - name: media                                                   │
          │        mountPath: /app/media     ← writable for uploads             │
          │      - name: tmp                                                     │
          │        mountPath: /tmp           ← Django needs /tmp                │
          │                                                                      │
          │    volumes:                                                          │
          │    - name: media                                                     │
          │      persistentVolumeClaim:                                         │
          │        claimName: anihpj-media-pvc                                 │
          │    - name: tmp                                                       │
          │      emptyDir: {}               ← ephemeral, cleared on pod restart │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [SECURITY CONTEXT FIELD QUICK REFERENCE — what each does]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
          │  Field                           │  Effect                         │
          ├──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
          │  runAsNonRoot: true              │  K8s refuses to start if image  │
          │                                  │  USER is root (UID 0)           │
          │  runAsUser: 1000                 │  Sets process UID inside ctr    │
          │  runAsGroup: 1000                │  Sets process primary GID       │
          │  fsGroup: 1000                   │  Volume files owned by GID 1000 │
          │  allowPrivilegeEscalation: false │  No child process can gain root │
          │  readOnlyRootFilesystem: true    │  / is read-only; writes → fail  │
          │  capabilities.drop: ["ALL"]      │  Remove all Linux capabilities  │
          │  capabilities.add: ["NET_BIND"]  │  Add specific cap back (avoid!) │
          │  seccompProfile.type: RuntimeDef │  Apply runtime's seccomp filter │
          │  appArmorProfile.type: Localhost │  Apply local AppArmor profile   │
          │  privileged: false (default)     │  No full host access            │
          └──────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

    18.7  Official SecurityContext Facts — Precedence, fsGroup, and Hidden Constraints
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/configure-pod-container/
            security-context: the exact precedence and constraint rules that
            explain WHY the anihpj spec in 18.6 is laid out the way it is.
          - PRECEDENCE: most fields exist at BOTH pod level (spec.securityContext)
            and container level (container.securityContext) — runAsUser,
            runAsGroup, runAsNonRoot, allowPrivilegeEscalation,
            readOnlyRootFilesystem, capabilities, seccompProfile, seLinuxOptions.
            When both are set, the CONTAINER-LEVEL value wins for that field only
            — other pod-level fields still apply. fsGroup, fsGroupChangePolicy,
            and supplementalGroups exist ONLY at pod level (no per-container override).
          - runAsGroup GOTCHA: if runAsUser is set but runAsGroup is omitted, the
            container's primary group defaults to root (GID 0) — NOT to
            runAsUser's value. Always set runAsGroup explicitly alongside runAsUser
            if you care about the group, not just the user.
          - allowPrivilegeEscalation is FORCED to true (your `false` is silently
            ignored) in two cases: the container runs privileged: true, OR it has
            CAP_SYS_ADMIN added. Setting false only means something on a
            non-privileged container without that capability — this is why 18.3's
            "drop ALL" and 18.4 must be applied TOGETHER, not either alone.
          - fsGroupChangePolicy (default: OnRootMismatch) controls a real
            performance/security tradeoff: OnRootMismatch only chmods a volume if
            its root doesn't already match fsGroup (fast, safe for reused PVCs);
            Always re-chmods the entire volume tree on every mount (slower, but
            guarantees consistency — matters for shared/multi-writer volumes).

          [PRECEDENCE MAP — which level wins, field by field]
          ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────────────────┐
          │ FIELD                      │ POD LEVEL?        │ CONTAINER OVERRIDES?│
          ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────┤
          │ runAsUser/runAsGroup       │ yes                │ yes, wins          │
          │ runAsNonRoot               │ yes                │ yes, wins          │
          │ allowPrivilegeEscalation   │ yes                │ yes, wins          │
          │ readOnlyRootFilesystem     │ yes                │ yes, wins          │
          │ capabilities               │ no (container only)│ n/a               │
          │ seccompProfile             │ yes                │ yes, wins          │
          │ appArmorProfile            │ yes                │ yes, wins          │
          │ fsGroup / fsGroupChangePolicy│ POD ONLY         │ cannot override    │
          │ supplementalGroups         │ POD ONLY           │ cannot override    │
          └────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────────────────┘
          [CODE: anihpj spec showing runAsGroup explicitly set to avoid the root-GID default gotcha]


Chapter 19: Secrets Management — Encryption at Rest
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    19.1  The Problem: Secrets in etcd Are Stored in Plaintext (By Default)
          - Kubernetes Secrets are base64-encoded, not encrypted
          - base64 is NOT encryption: echo "cGFzc3dvcmQ=" | base64 -d → "password"
          - Without encryption: anyone who can access etcd can read all secrets
          - Encryption at rest: secrets are encrypted before writing to etcd
          [DIAGRAM: Secret → API server → encrypted before etcd storage]

          [BASE64 ≠ ENCRYPTION — the common misconception]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                          │
          │  kubectl create secret generic db-creds                 │
          │    --from-literal=password=supersecret123               │
          │                                                          │
          │  kubectl get secret db-creds -o yaml                    │
          │  data:                                                   │
          │    password: c3VwZXJzZWNyZXQxMjM=    ← "encrypted"? ❌ │
          │                                                          │
          │  echo "c3VwZXJzZWNyZXQxMjM=" | base64 -d               │
          │  → supersecret123    ← anyone can decode this! 🔴       │
          │                                                          │
          │  WITHOUT etcd encryption at rest:                       │
          │  Direct etcd read → plaintext secret exposed            │
          │                                                          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    19.2  Enabling Encryption at Rest
          - Configure EncryptionConfiguration resource
          - Specify: which resources to encrypt (secrets), which provider (AES-CBC, AES-GCM)
          - Pass config file to API server: --encryption-provider-config flag
          - After enabling: NEW secrets are encrypted; OLD ones must be rotated
          [CODE: EncryptionConfiguration YAML for AES-GCM encryption]
          [CODE: API server flag to enable encryption]
          [CODE: Verifying encryption works — reading directly from etcd]

          [ENCRYPTION AT REST — configuration flow]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  Step 1: Create encryption key                               │
          │  head -c 32 /dev/urandom | base64  →  aGVsbG93b3JsZA==     │
          │                                                              │
          │  Step 2: Create EncryptionConfiguration                      │
          │  kind: EncryptionConfiguration                               │
          │  resources:                                                  │
          │    - resources: ["secrets"]                                  │
          │      providers:                                              │
          │        - aescbc:               ← encrypt with AES-CBC       │
          │            keys:                                             │
          │            - name: key1                                      │
          │              secret: aGVsbG93b3JsZA==  ← base64 key        │
          │        - identity: {}          ← fallback: allow plaintext  │
          │                                                              │
          │  Step 3: API server flag                                     │
          │  --encryption-provider-config=/etc/kubernetes/enc.yaml      │
          │                                                              │
          │  Step 4: Rotate existing secrets                             │
          │  kubectl get secrets -A -o json | kubectl replace -f -      │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    19.3  Encryption Providers — Which to Use
          - identity: no encryption (dangerous default — means plaintext)
          - aescbc: AES-CBC, 32-byte key, widely supported
          - aesgcm: AES-GCM, 16/24/32-byte key, better performance
          - secretbox: XSalsa20 and Poly1305, fast
          - kms: use external Key Management Service (AWS KMS, GCP KMS, Azure KeyVault)
          [TABLE: Encryption providers — comparison and recommendations]

    19.4  Secrets Management in anihpj — Best Practices
          - Django SECRET_KEY: in K8s Secret, not hardcoded in settings.py
          - Database password: in K8s Secret, mounted as env var
          - AWS credentials: use IAM roles / IRSA, not stored as Secrets
          - Enable encryption at rest for the anihpj namespace
          - Audit who can read secrets: kubectl get rolebindings -o yaml | grep secrets
          [CODE: Complete secure Secret management for anihpj configuration]

    19.5  Secret Rotation — Automated Patterns for the CKS Exam
          - Rotation limits blast radius: a leaked credential is only useful until
            the next rotation — static, never-rotated secrets are the real risk
          - Pods do NOT see updated Secret env vars — env values are injected at
            container start; volume-mounted Secrets DO refresh (kubelet sync, ~1min)
          - Standard rotation flow: update Secret → kubectl rollout restart
            deployment/anihpj (forces pods to pick up new values)
          - Automation options: Reloader (annotation-driven restarts), External
            Secrets Operator refreshInterval (Ch 20), cert-manager renewBefore
            for TLS, bound SA tokens rotate automatically (Ch 10)
          - etcd ENCRYPTION KEY rotation (exam-relevant sequence!):
            add new key FIRST in EncryptionConfiguration → restart API server →
            rewrite all secrets: kubectl get secrets -A -o json | kubectl replace -f -
            → remove old key → restart again

          [WHAT ROTATES HOW — mechanism per secret type]
          ┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ SECRET TYPE              │ ROTATION MECHANISM                       │
          ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ App cred (env var)       │ new Secret + rollout restart             │
          │ App cred (mounted)       │ update in place, ~60s kubelet sync       │
          │ TLS certificate          │ cert-manager renewBefore (auto)          │
          │ ServiceAccount token     │ bound tokens auto-rotate (Ch 10)         │
          │ etcd enc. key            │ add key, restart, rewrite all secrets    │
          └──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Rotate anihpj DB password — new Secret + rollout restart deployment/anihpj]
          [CODE: etcd encryption key rotation sequence — add key, restart, kubectl replace, remove old]

    19.6  Official EncryptionConfiguration Facts — the Provider-Ordering Rule
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/administer-cluster/encrypt-data:
            ONE rule underlies everything about encryption at rest, and it is why
            rotation works the way it does.
          - THE RULE: the FIRST provider listed is used for ALL new writes. EVERY
            provider in the list is tried IN ORDER for reads until one succeeds.
            This is why rotation puts the new key first (new writes use it
            immediately) while keeping the old key second (old data already
            encrypted with it can still be read and re-encrypted).
          - aescbc is explicitly flagged by the docs as weaker (vulnerable to a
            padding-oracle class of attack) — prefer aesgcm or an external kms
            provider for new configurations; aescbc mainly exists for legacy compat.
          - identity as the LAST fallback provider = "if nothing else can decrypt
            it, treat it as plaintext" — NEVER put identity FIRST (that disables
            encryption for all new writes) and be careful having it present at all
            in production (it's an escape hatch, not a safety net).
          - Wildcard resource matching (v1.27+): resources: ['*.apps'] or ['*.*']
            lets one provider config cover whole API groups; more specific entries
            must be listed before wildcards, and overlapping wildcards are invalid.
          - Custom Resources (v1.26+) can be encrypted too — but only if explicitly
            listed or wildcard-matched; existing CR data stays unencrypted until
            its next write (same "only new writes" rule applies to plain Secrets).
          - CRITICAL FAILURE MODE: if you remove a key while data still encrypted
            with it exists, and NO remaining provider can decrypt it, that data
            becomes PERMANENTLY INACCESSIBLE via the API (not a graceful error —
            requests for that object simply fail). This is exactly why the
            rotation sequence in 19.5 always re-encrypts BEFORE removing the old key.

          [PROVIDER LIST ORDER — the one rule that explains everything]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  providers:                                                          │
          │    - aesgcm: {keys: [{name: key-new, secret: ...}]}  ← WRITES use    │
          │        this one, always, no matter how many providers follow         │
          │    - aescbc: {keys: [{name: key-old, secret: ...}]}  ← READ fallback │
          │        only (old data still encrypted with this key stays readable)  │
          │    - identity: {}                                     ← LAST resort,│
          │        treats anything undecryptable as already-plaintext            │
          │                                                                      │
          │  READ:  try provider 1 → fail → try provider 2 → fail → provider 3  │
          │  WRITE: ALWAYS provider 1, unconditionally                           │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: EncryptionConfiguration with wildcard '*.apps' matching + explicit secrets/configmaps]


Chapter 20: External Secret Management — Vault and Cloud KMS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    20.1  Why Kubernetes Secrets Alone Are Not Enough for Enterprise
          - Kubernetes Secrets are accessible to anyone with API + RBAC access
          - Rotation requires redeploying pods or restarting
          - No access history: who read this secret and when?
          - External secret managers solve all of these problems
          [COMPARISON TABLE: K8s Secrets vs Vault vs AWS Secrets Manager]

    20.2  HashiCorp Vault — Enterprise-Grade Secret Management
          - Vault stores, leases, and rotates secrets
          - Dynamic secrets: generates a new DB password per pod, auto-expires
          - Kubernetes auth method: pod presents ServiceAccount token, Vault verifies
          - Vault Agent Sidecar: injects secrets into pod filesystem automatically
          [DIAGRAM: Pod → ServiceAccount token → Vault → dynamic secret → app]

    20.3  External Secrets Operator (ESO)
          - Syncs secrets from AWS Secrets Manager / GCP Secret Manager / Vault
          - Creates K8s Secrets from external store automatically
          - Best of both worlds: external management + K8s native Secret API
          [CODE: ExternalSecret resource YAML for anihpj using AWS Secrets Manager]

    20.4  Implementing Vault for anihpj
          - Store Django SECRET_KEY and DB password in Vault
          - Configure Vault Kubernetes auth for the anihpj-sa ServiceAccount
          - Use Vault Agent sidecar to inject secrets into the anihpj pod
          [CODE: Vault policy for anihpj, Vault Agent sidecar injection]


Chapter 21: Container Runtime Sandboxing — gVisor and Kata Containers
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    21.1  The Container Isolation Problem
          - All containers on a node share the same Linux kernel
          - A kernel exploit in one container = game over for entire node
          - Sandboxed runtimes add an extra layer between container and kernel
          - gVisor: intercepts syscalls in userspace (Google's solution)
          - Kata Containers: lightweight VM per container (hardware isolation)
          [DIAGRAM: Traditional container vs gVisor vs Kata isolation layers]

          [THREE LEVELS OF CONTAINER ISOLATION]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                            │
          │  TRADITIONAL CONTAINER          gVisor                    │
          │  ┌──────────────┐               ┌──────────────┐         │
          │  │  Container   │               │  Container   │         │
          │  │  App + libs  │               │  App + libs  │         │
          │  └──────┬───────┘               └──────┬───────┘         │
          │         │ syscalls                      │ syscalls        │
          │         ▼                               ▼                 │
          │  ┌──────────────┐               ┌──────────────┐         │
          │  │ Linux Kernel │               │  Sentry      │         │
          │  │  (shared)    │               │ (user-space  │         │
          │  └──────────────┘               │  kernel sim) │         │
          │                                 └──────┬───────┘         │
          │  Isolation: None                       │ minimal syscalls  │
          │  (all containers share                 ▼                 │
          │   the same kernel)              ┌──────────────┐         │
          │                                 │ Linux Kernel │         │
          │                                 │  (shared)    │         │
          │                                 └──────────────┘         │
          │                                 Isolation: HIGH           │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.2  RuntimeClass — Selecting the Runtime in Kubernetes
          - RuntimeClass resource defines which container runtime to use
          - Apply per-pod: spec.runtimeClassName: gvisor
          - Different workloads can use different runtimes in same cluster
          [CODE: RuntimeClass YAML for gVisor]
          [CODE: Pod spec using RuntimeClass for untrusted workloads]

          [TRADITIONAL vs gVisor vs KATA CONTAINERS — full comparison]
          ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
          │  FEATURE               │  gVisor (runsc)    │  Kata Containers   │
          ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
          │  Isolation mechanism   │  User-space kernel │  Lightweight VM    │
          │                        │  (Sentry)          │  (QEMU/Firecracker)│
          │  Kernel shared?        │  Partially (safe)  │  No — own kernel   │
          │  Performance overhead  │  Low-Medium (~10%) │  Medium (~20%)     │
          │  Startup time          │  Fast (~same)      │  Slightly slower   │
          │  Syscall compatibility │  High (~300 syscls)│  Full compatibility│
          │  Memory overhead       │  Low               │  Higher (VM)       │
          │  Best for              │  General workloads │  High security/    │
          │                        │  + untrusted code  │  multi-tenant      │
          │  K8s RuntimeClass      │  handler: runsc    │  handler: kata     │
          │  Supported on          │  Linux x86-64      │  Linux x86-64/arm  │
          └────────────────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

    21.3  When to Use Sandboxed Runtimes
          - User-uploaded code execution: always sandbox
          - Untrusted third-party containers: sandbox
          - Multi-tenant clusters: different RuntimeClass per tenant
          - anihpj: user file uploads handled by media container → sandbox it

          [DECISION FLOWCHART — which runtime for which workload?]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  Is this container running code from untrusted/unknown users?       │
          │    YES → Use: gVisor (quick) or Kata (maximum security)             │
          │    NO  ↓                                                             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Is this a multi-tenant cluster (different customers' pods)?         │
          │    YES → Use: Kata Containers (hardware isolation per tenant)        │
          │    NO  ↓                                                             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Is this a system/privileged workload (CNI, monitoring, logging)?   │
          │    YES → Use: default runtime (runc) — these need full syscalls     │
          │    NO  ↓                                                             │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Is this a standard business workload (like anihpj Django)?         │
          │    YES → Use: default runtime (runc) + Seccomp + AppArmor           │
          │            (sandboxed runtimes are optional extra protection)        │
          │                                                                      │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.4  Multi-Tenancy Isolation Patterns
          - CKS curriculum: "understand and implement isolation techniques
            (multi-tenancy, sandboxed containers, etc.)"
          - Soft multi-tenancy = trusted teams share a cluster, separated by
            Namespace + RBAC + NetworkPolicy + ResourceQuota + PSS
          - Hard multi-tenancy = untrusted tenants; requires sandboxed runtimes
            (gVisor/Kata, section 21.1-21.3), separate node pools, or separate
            clusters entirely — a shared kernel is NOT a security boundary
          - The isolation stack, per tenant namespace: PSS Restricted label +
            default-deny NetworkPolicy + ResourceQuota/LimitRange (stop noisy-
            neighbor DoS) + dedicated ServiceAccounts with no cross-namespace RBAC
          - anihpj example: give jobpost-prod, jobpost-staging, and a third-party
            analytics tenant their own namespaces, each fully isolated

          [ISOLATION STRENGTH LADDER — weakest to strongest]
          ┌──────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
          │ TECHNIQUE                    │ ISOLATES            │ TRUST LEVEL      │
          ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
          │ Namespace + RBAC             │ API access          │ soft (trusted)   │
          │ + NetworkPolicy              │ network paths       │ soft             │
          │ + ResourceQuota/LimitRange   │ CPU/mem/objects     │ soft             │
          │ + PSS Restricted             │ pod privileges      │ soft/medium      │
          │ + gVisor / Kata runtime      │ the kernel          │ hard (untrusted) │
          │ Separate node pool / cluster │ everything          │ hardest          │
          └──────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────┘
          [CODE: Full per-tenant isolation bundle for a jobpost tenant namespace]


Chapter 22: mTLS and Service Mesh Security
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    22.1  Why Pod-to-Pod Traffic Is Insecure by Default
          - HTTP between pods: plaintext (anyone on the node can sniff it)
          - An attacker with pod access can read inter-service traffic
          - Solution: mutual TLS (mTLS) — every pod gets a cert, all traffic encrypted
          [DIAGRAM: Without mTLS (plaintext) vs with mTLS (encrypted both directions)]

    22.2  Mutual TLS (mTLS) — How It Works
          - Both parties (client AND server) present certificates
          - The client verifies the server is who it claims to be
          - The server verifies the client is who it claims to be
          - If either cert is invalid → connection refused

          [mTLS HANDSHAKE — anihpj → postgres step by step]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  anihpj pod (client)                postgres pod (server)           │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 1. ClientHello                     │                        │
          │        │ ─────────────────────────────────▶ │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 2. ServerHello + Server Cert       │                        │
          │        │ ◀───────────────────────────────── │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 3. anihpj verifies postgres cert   │                        │
          │        │    (signed by Istio cluster CA?)   │                        │
          │        │ ─────────────────────────────────▶ │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 4. ServerCertificateRequest         │                        │
          │        │ ◀───────────────────────────────── │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 5. anihpj sends its own cert       │                        │
          │        │ ─────────────────────────────────▶ │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 6. postgres verifies anihpj cert   │                        │
          │        │    ✅ Both sides authenticated!    │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │        │ 7. Encrypted channel established   │                        │
          │        │ ◀──────────── TLS ───────────────▶ │                        │
          │        │                                    │                        │
          │  If anihpj cert is missing or invalid:      │                        │
          │  → Connection REFUSED (mTLS strict mode)    │                        │
          │  → Attacker pod without cert → blocked ✅   │                        │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [REGULAR TLS vs mTLS — key difference]
          ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  Regular TLS       │  Only server proves its identity             │
          │  (HTTPS websites)  │  Client is anonymous (any browser can visit) │
          ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  mTLS              │  BOTH server AND client prove their identity │
          │  (pod-to-pod)      │  Unauthorized pods cannot connect at all     │
          └────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.3  Istio — Service Mesh for Automatic mTLS
          - Istio injects a sidecar proxy (Envoy) into every pod
          - Sidecar handles mTLS automatically — no app code changes
          - PeerAuthentication: enforce mTLS for all pods in a namespace
          - AuthorizationPolicy: control which services can talk to which
          [CODE: PeerAuthentication YAML to enforce mTLS in anihpj namespace]
          [CODE: AuthorizationPolicy — only allow anihpj to call postgres]

    22.4  Securing anihpj with Istio mTLS
          - Enable mTLS in STRICT mode for anihpj-prod namespace
          - Create AuthorizationPolicy: allow ingress-gateway → anihpj
          - Create AuthorizationPolicy: allow anihpj → postgres only
          - Verify with: istioctl analyze and Kiali dashboard
          [CODE: Complete Istio security setup for anihpj]

    22.5  Pod-to-Pod Encryption — Istio mTLS + Cilium WireGuard
          - CKS curriculum: "implement Pod-to-Pod encryption (Cilium, Istio)" —
            two valid, exam-relevant answers, know when to reach for each
          - Istio (sidecar mesh): encrypts L7 traffic with per-workload identity
            (SPIFFE), gives AuthorizationPolicy — best when you also want traffic
            policy, observability, and canary routing
          - Cilium WireGuard / IPsec: transparent node-to-node encryption in the
            CNI, no sidecars, near-zero app overhead — best when you just need the
            wire encrypted with minimal moving parts
          - Both satisfy the requirement; the mesh adds policy, the CNI adds speed
          - Verify Istio mTLS: istioctl authn tls-check / PeerAuthentication STRICT.
            Verify Cilium: cilium status | grep Encryption ; cilium encrypt status

          [ISTIO mTLS vs CILIUM WIREGUARD — pick the right tool]
          ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
          │ ASPECT                 │ ISTIO mTLS (sidecar)     │ CILIUM WIREGUARD     │
          ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
          │ Layer                  │ L7, per-workload         │ L3/L4, node-to-node  │
          │ Identity               │ SPIFFE per service       │ node keys            │
          │ Overhead               │ sidecar per pod          │ in-kernel, minimal   │
          │ Extra powers           │ authz, routing, tracing  │ just encryption      │
          │ Enable                 │ PeerAuthentication STRICT│ enable-wireguard:true│
          │ Best when              │ you want full mesh policy │ you want speed/simple│
          └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Cilium WireGuard enablement + cilium encrypt status verification]
          [CODE: Istio PeerAuthentication STRICT for anihpj-prod + tls-check]
