Chapter 23: Image Security — Scanning and Minimizing Attack Surface
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    23.1  Why Container Images Are a Security Risk
          - Images contain everything: OS, runtime, libraries, your code
          - A Python image may include packages with known CVEs (vulnerabilities)
          - Base images grow stale — what was secure yesterday may not be today
          - Pulling from Docker Hub: you don't know what's inside that image
          [ILLUSTRATION: A container image as a city neighborhood — some streets safe, some dangerous]

          [IMAGE CVE EXAMPLE — why scanning matters]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  anihpj:v1 (built 6 months ago)                             │
          │  Base: python:3.10-slim                                     │
          │                                                              │
          │  $ trivy image anihpj:v1                                    │
          │                                                              │
          │  Total: 23 vulnerabilities (CRITICAL: 2, HIGH: 8, MED: 13) │
          │                                                              │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
          │  │  CVE-2023-38545  CRITICAL  curl/libcurl              │  │
          │  │  CVSS: 9.8 — Remote code execution via SOCKS5        │  │
          │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
          │  │  CVE-2023-4911   HIGH      glibc / looney tunables  │  │
          │  │  CVSS: 7.8 — Local privilege escalation             │  │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
          │                                                              │
          │  Fix: rebuild with python:3.12-slim (updated base image)   │
          │  Result: anihpj:v2 → CRITICAL: 0, HIGH: 1, MED: 4         │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.2  Minimizing Image Attack Surface
          - Use minimal base images: distroless, alpine, slim variants
          - distroless: contains only your app and runtime — no shell, no package manager
          - Multi-stage builds: compile in one image, run in a minimal image
          - Remove: curl, wget, bash, nc, git from final production images
          [CODE: Multi-stage Dockerfile for anihpj using distroless final stage]
          [TABLE: Base image comparison — size, packages, attack surface]

          [BASE IMAGE COMPARISON — anihpj options]
          | Base Image          | Size    | Has Shell | Has Pkg Mgr | CVE Count | Recommendation     |
          | python:3.12         | 1.0 GB  | ✅ bash   | ✅ apt      | Many      | Dev/testing only   |
          | python:3.12-slim    | 130 MB  | ✅ bash   | ✅ apt      | Less      | General production |
          | python:3.12-alpine  | 22 MB   | ✅ sh     | ✅ apk      | Minimal   | Preferred          |
          | gcr.io/distroless   | 20 MB   | ❌ None   | ❌ None     | Very few  | Maximum security   |

    23.3  Trivy — Image Vulnerability Scanner
          - trivy: scan images for CVEs, misconfigurations, secrets
          - Integrates with CI/CD pipelines — fail builds with CRITICAL CVEs
          - Can scan: images, filesystems, Git repos, Kubernetes clusters
          [CODE: Trivy commands — image scan, output formats, severity thresholds]
          [CODE: GitHub Actions step to scan anihpj image in CI pipeline]

    23.4  Private Container Registries — Controlling Image Sources
          - Prevent pods from pulling images from untrusted public registries
          - Use private registry: AWS ECR, GCP Artifact Registry, Azure ACR
          - imagePullSecrets: provide registry credentials to pods
          - Admission controller: deny images not from approved registries
          [CODE: Creating imagePullSecret and using in pod spec]

          [🔄 SECURE SUPPLY CHAIN PIPELINE — end-to-end from code to cluster]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                          │
          │  💻 DEVELOPER           🏗️  CI/CD PIPELINE            ☸️  KUBERNETES      │
          │  ───────────           ────────────────────          ───────────────      │
          │                                                                          │
          │  ┌──────────┐         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │ git push │────────▶│  GATE 1: STATIC ANALYSIS                     │   │
          │  │ of code  │         │  • kube-linter (YAML linting)                │   │
          │  │ + Docker │         │  • kubesec (security scoring)                │   │
          │  │   file   │         │  • conftest (policy checks)                  │   │
          │  └──────────┘         │  • SAST scanners (code vulnerabilities)     │   │
          │                       └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘   │
          │                                              │ ✅ PASS                   │
          │                                              ▼                           │
          │                       ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │                       │  GATE 2: IMAGE BUILD & SCAN                  │   │
          │                       │  • docker build (multi-stage)               │   │
          │                       │  • hadolint (Dockerfile linting)            │   │
          │                       │  • trivy image (CVE scanning)               │   │
          │                       │  • dockle (image best practices)            │   │
          │                       └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘   │
          │                                              │ ✅ 0 CRITICAL CVEs        │
          │                                              ▼                           │
          │                       ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │                       │  GATE 3: SIGN & PUSH                          │   │
          │                       │  • cosign sign (cryptographic signing)        │   │
          │                       │  • syft (generate SBOM)                       │   │
          │                       │  • docker push to private registry            │   │
          │                       └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘   │
          │                                              │ ✅ IMAGE SIGNED           │
          │                                              ▼                           │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  GATE 4: KUBERNETES ADMISSION (runtime gate)                     │   │
          │  │                                                                   │   │
          │  │  ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐ │   │
          │  │  │ Kyverno          │   │ OPA/Gatekeeper  │   │ PodSecurity     │ │   │
          │  │  │ verifyImages     │   │ approved registry│   │ Admission       │ │   │
          │  │  │ (checks cosign   │   │ (blocks docker.io)│  │ (enforce PSS)  │ │   │
          │  │  │  signature)      │   │                  │   │                 │ │   │
          │  │  └─────────────────┘   └─────────────────┘   └─────────────────┘ │   │
          │  │        │                       │                    │              │   │
          │  │        ▼                       ▼                    ▼              │   │
          │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │   │
          │  │  │  ✅ ALL CHECKS PASS → Pod admitted → runs on cluster    │     │   │
          │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                                                          │
          │  🔍 GATE 5: CONTINUOUS MONITORING (never stops)                          │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  Trivy Operator (scans running images for NEW CVEs)              │   │
          │  │  Falco (detects anomalous runtime behavior)                      │   │
          │  │  Audit Logs (records every API action)                           │   │
          │  │  kube-bench CronJob (weekly CIS benchmark)                       │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                                                          │
          │  🛡️  If ANY gate fails → pipeline STOPS → image NEVER reaches cluster  │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.5  Image Digest Pinning — Provenance You Can Trust
          - Tags are MUTABLE: anihpj:v1 today can point to a different image
            tomorrow (an attacker who pushes to the registry can swap it silently)
          - Digests are IMMUTABLE: image@sha256:<hash> is content-addressed — the
            bytes cannot change without changing the hash
          - Pin production workloads to digests; combine with imagePullPolicy so
            the cluster never quietly pulls a re-tagged image
          - imagePullPolicy semantics the exam tests:
            Always = pull every start; IfNotPresent = use node cache if present;
            Never = only node-cached. :latest defaults to Always (avoid :latest)
          - Enforce digest-only images at admission with Kyverno/OPA (Ch 24, 26)

          [TAG vs DIGEST — why pin]
          ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ REFERENCE                  │ SECURITY PROPERTY                        │
          ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ anihpj:latest              │ ❌ mutable, ambiguous, pulls Always       │
          │ anihpj:v1.2.3              │ ⚠️  mutable — tag can be re-pushed        │
          │ anihpj@sha256:ab12...       │ ✅ immutable — exact bytes, verifiable    │
          └────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Resolve tag → digest (docker/crane) and pin it in the anihpj Deployment]
          [CODE: Kyverno policy — reject images that use a tag instead of a digest]

    23.6  Permitted Registries — Whitelisting Trusted Image Sources
          - Restrict which registries the cluster may pull from — block docker.io
            and any unknown source; allow only your private registry + vetted bases
          - Three enforcement points: OPA/Gatekeeper ConstraintTemplate, Kyverno
            ClusterPolicy, or the built-in ImagePolicyWebhook (Ch 24)
          - anihpj policy: allow ONLY registry.anihpj.io/* and gcr.io/distroless/*;
            deny everything else at admission before the pod is created
          - Pair with imagePullSecrets so pulls from the private registry authenticate
          - This is the CKS "secure your supply chain — permitted registries" task

          [PERMITTED-REGISTRY ENFORCEMENT — where to put the gate]
          ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ TOOL                   │ HOW IT ALLOW-LISTS REGISTRIES                │
          ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Kyverno ClusterPolicy  │ validate image starts-with allowed prefixes  │
          │ OPA/Gatekeeper         │ ConstraintTemplate (Rego) on image field     │
          │ ImagePolicyWebhook     │ API server calls external allow/deny service │
          └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Kyverno ClusterPolicy — allow only registry.anihpj.io + distroless]


Chapter 24: Admission Controllers & Image Policy Webhook
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    24.1  What Are Admission Controllers?
          - Admission controllers intercept API requests AFTER authn/authz
          - Can MUTATE (modify) or VALIDATE (allow/deny) requests
          - Configured as plugins on the API server
          - Key security admission plugins you must know for CKS
          [DIAGRAM: Request → authn → authz → mutating admission → validating admission → persist]

          [CRITICAL ADMISSION CONTROLLERS FOR CKS]
          | Plugin                  | Type      | What It Does                             |
          | NodeRestriction         | Validating| Limits kubelet access to own node/pods   |
          | PodSecurity             | Validating| Enforces Pod Security Standards (PSS)    |
          | LimitRanger             | Validating| Enforces resource limits in namespaces   |
          | ResourceQuota           | Validating| Enforces namespace resource quotas       |
          | ServiceAccount          | Mutating  | Auto-assigns default ServiceAccount      |
          | MutatingAdmissionWebhk  | Mutating  | Custom webhook (external policy engine)  |
          | ValidatingAdmissionWebhk| Validating| Custom webhook (OPA/Gatekeeper, Kyverno) |
          | ImagePolicyWebhook      | Validating| External image approval policy           |

    24.2  Dynamic Admission Control — Webhooks
          - Mutating webhooks: modify the request before it's saved
          - Validating webhooks: approve or reject the request
          - External webhook server: receives the request, returns allow/deny
          - OPA Gatekeeper and Kyverno implement validating webhooks
          [CODE: ValidatingWebhookConfiguration YAML template]
          [DIAGRAM: Request → K8s API → webhook call → external policy server → allow/deny]

    24.3  OPA Gatekeeper — Policy as Code for Kubernetes
          - OPA: Open Policy Agent — a general-purpose policy engine
          - Gatekeeper: OPA for Kubernetes, integrates via webhook
          - ConstraintTemplate: defines the policy (in Rego language)
          - Constraint: applies the policy to specific resources
          [CODE: ConstraintTemplate — deny images not from approved registry]
          [CODE: Constraint — apply policy to anihpj namespace]

          [OPA GATEKEEPER — anihpj image policy]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # ConstraintTemplate: require approved registries           │
          │                                                              │
          │  # Deny image if not from:                                   │
          │  # - registry.anihpj.com                                    │
          │  # - gcr.io/anihpj-project                                  │
          │                                                              │
          │  package k8srequiredregistry                                 │
          │                                                              │
          │  violation[{"msg": msg}] {                                  │
          │    container := input.review.object.spec.containers[_]      │
          │    not starts_with(container.image, "registry.anihpj.com/") │
          │    not starts_with(container.image, "gcr.io/anihpj/")      │
          │    msg := sprintf("Image not from approved registry: %v",   │
          │                   [container.image])                         │
          │  }                                                           │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    24.4  Kyverno — Simpler Kubernetes-Native Policies
          - Kyverno: policy engine using YAML (no Rego needed)
          - Types: validate, mutate, generate, verify image
          - Easier learning curve than OPA for K8s-specific policies
          [CODE: Kyverno ClusterPolicy — require image digest (not mutable tags)]
          [CODE: Kyverno policy — require securityContext on all pods]

          [OPA GATEKEEPER vs KYVERNO — choosing the right policy engine]
          ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
          │  FEATURE               │  OPA Gatekeeper          │  Kyverno                │
          ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
          │  Policy language       │  Rego (functional lang)  │  YAML (K8s-native)      │
          │  Learning curve        │  Steep (new language)    │  Easy (just YAML)        │
          │  Policy types          │  Validate                │  Validate, Mutate,      │
          │                        │                          │  Generate, VerifyImages  │
          │  Image verification    │  External webhook needed  │  Built-in verifyImages   │
          │  Audit mode            │  ✅ Yes                   │  ✅ Yes                  │
          │  Policy reports        │  ConstraintStatus         │  PolicyReport CRD       │
          │  CKS exam              │  ✅ Know Rego basics      │  ✅ Know YAML structure  │
          │  Ecosystem             │  CNCF graduated           │  CNCF incubating        │
          └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

          [ADMISSION CONTROL FLOW — OPA/Kyverno in the request pipeline]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  kubectl apply -f anihpj-pod.yaml                                   │
          │       │                                                               │
          │       ▼                                                               │
          │  API Server → AuthN ✅ → AuthZ ✅                                   │
          │       │                                                               │
          │       ▼                                                               │
          │  Mutating Admission Webhooks (run first)                             │
          │  → Kyverno mutate: add default securityContext if missing            │
          │       │                                                               │
          │       ▼                                                               │
          │  Validating Admission Webhooks (run after mutation)                  │
          │  → OPA Gatekeeper: image from approved registry? ✅/❌              │
          │  → Kyverno validate: runAsNonRoot set? ✅/❌                        │
          │       │                                                               │
          │       ▼ all passed                                                    │
          │  Pod created ✅                                                       │
          │                                                                      │
          │  If ANY validating webhook returns DENY:                             │
          │  → 403 Forbidden: "Image not from approved registry"                │
          │  → Pod is NOT created                                               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    24.5  Official Admission Controller Facts — Defaults and the Re-Validation Rule
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/reference/access-authn-authz/
            admission-controllers: what's on BY DEFAULT, the exact flags, and one
            subtle mutation-order detail that explains "phantom" webhook re-calls.
          - MANY security-relevant plugins are ALREADY ENABLED by default on a
            standard cluster — a common exam mistake is trying to --enable-
            admission-plugins=PodSecurity when it's already on. Verify first:
            ps aux | grep kube-apiserver | grep admission-plugins
          - Flag syntax is a comma-separated list, and --enable-/--disable- can be
            combined in one command: --enable-admission-plugins=NodeRestriction
            --disable-admission-plugins=AlwaysAdmit
          - THE RE-VALIDATION RULE (subtle, exam-relevant): admission runs in two
            phases — ALL mutating admission first, THEN all validating admission.
            But if ANY mutating webhook changes the object, the API server
            RE-RUNS every previously-called mutating webhook against the newly
            mutated object (to let earlier webhooks react to later changes) before
            moving to validation. This is why chains of mutating webhooks can
            re-trigger each other — always design mutating webhooks to be
            idempotent (safe to run more than once on the same object).
          - Request rejection is immediate and total: if ANY single controller in
            either phase rejects the request, the WHOLE request fails right there
            — no partial admission, no "some fields applied."

          [DEFAULT-ENABLED ADMISSION CONTROLLERS — security-relevant subset]
          ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ PLUGIN                       │ ENABLED BY DEFAULT?                  │
          ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ NamespaceLifecycle            │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ LimitRanger                   │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ ServiceAccount                │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ PodSecurity                   │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ ResourceQuota                 │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ MutatingAdmissionWebhook      │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ ValidatingAdmissionWebhook    │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ ValidatingAdmissionPolicy     │ ✅ yes                                │
          │ NodeRestriction               │ ❌ NOT default — you must enable it  │
          │ ImagePolicyWebhook            │ ❌ NOT default — you must enable it  │
          └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️  NodeRestriction and ImagePolicyWebhook — the two most CKS-relevant
          security plugins — are NOT on by default. Explicitly enabling
          NodeRestriction is one of the most common CKS exam tasks.
          [CODE: --enable-admission-plugins=NodeRestriction,ImagePolicyWebhook + verify with ps aux]


Chapter 25: Static Analysis — Scanning Manifests Before Deployment
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    25.1  Why Static Analysis of YAML Matters
          - Catch security misconfigurations before they reach the cluster
          - Shift security left: find problems at development time, not production
          - Tools: kube-linter, kubesec, conftest (OPA), checkov
          [DIAGRAM: Developer → git push → CI pipeline → static analysis → cluster]

    25.2  kube-linter — Kubernetes YAML Security Analysis
          - Scans Kubernetes manifests for security issues and misconfigurations
          - Detects: containers running as root, missing resource limits, missing probes
          - Can be run locally or in CI/CD pipeline
          [CODE: Running kube-linter on anihpj manifests]
          [CODE: GitHub Actions workflow with kube-linter gate]

    25.3  kubesec — Security Risk Analysis for K8s Manifests
          - Scores your manifest security (0-10 scale)
          - Checks: security context, capabilities, privilege escalation, seccomp
          - Can run as a webhook or CLI tool
          [CODE: kubesec scan command and interpreting output]

    25.4  conftest — Testing YAML with OPA Rego Policies
          - Write OPA Rego policies, test against any YAML/JSON
          - Not Kubernetes-specific — can test Dockerfiles, Terraform too
          - Great for custom organizational policies
          [CODE: Rego policy — deny any pod without securityContext]
          [CODE: conftest test command and output]

    25.5  Integrating Static Analysis into anihpj CI/CD
          - Step 1: kube-linter on every PR — fail if critical issues
          - Step 2: kubesec score must be ≥ 4 before merge
          - Step 3: conftest validates against organizational policies
          - Step 4: Trivy scans the built image — fail on CRITICAL CVEs

          [ANIHPJ SECURITY CI/CD PIPELINE — complete gate visualization]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  Developer pushes code → opens PR                                   │
          │         │                                                            │
          │         ▼                                                            │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  GATE 1: YAML SECURITY (kube-linter + kubesec + conftest)   │   │
          │  │  ✅ No privileged containers                                 │   │
          │  │  ✅ securityContext set on all containers                    │   │
          │  │  ✅ kubesec score ≥ 4                                        │   │
          │  │  ❌ FAIL → PR blocked, developer must fix                   │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                 │ ✅                                 │
          │                                 ▼                                    │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  GATE 2: DOCKERFILE SECURITY (hadolint + conftest)          │   │
          │  │  ✅ No COPY . . without .dockerignore                       │   │
          │  │  ✅ Non-root USER instruction present                       │   │
          │  │  ✅ No ADD from URLs (use COPY instead)                     │   │
          │  │  ❌ FAIL → PR blocked                                       │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                 │ ✅ PR merged                       │
          │                                 ▼                                    │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  GATE 3: IMAGE SCAN (trivy image)                           │   │
          │  │  ✅ CRITICAL CVEs = 0                                        │   │
          │  │  ✅ HIGH CVEs ≤ 2 (with exceptions documented)              │   │
          │  │  ❌ FAIL → image not pushed to registry                     │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                 │ ✅                                 │
          │                                 ▼                                    │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  GATE 4: IMAGE SIGNING (cosign)                             │   │
          │  │  ✅ cosign sign with CI/CD key or keyless OIDC              │   │
          │  │  ✅ SBOM generated and attached                             │   │
          │  │  ❌ FAIL → deploy blocked                                   │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                 │ ✅ Signed + verified              │
          │                                 ▼                                    │
          │  Deploy to cluster → Kyverno verifyImages checks signature ✅       │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 26: Image Signing — Cosign and Sigstore
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    26.1  Why Image Signing Matters
          - You build anihpj:v2 — but how does the cluster KNOW it's your image?
          - An attacker could push a malicious image to your registry with the same tag
          - Image signing: cryptographic proof that you (and only you) built this image
          - Verify at admission time: only run signed images from trusted identities
          [ANALOGY: Image signing = wax seal on a letter — tamper-evident, identity-proving]

    26.2  Cosign — Signing and Verifying Container Images
          - Part of the Sigstore project (open-source, CNCF backed)
          - cosign sign: creates a digital signature stored in the registry
          - cosign verify: checks the signature before pulling/running
          - Works with existing registries (ECR, GCR, ACR, Docker Hub)
          [CODE: cosign key generation, signing, and verification workflow]
          [CODE: Signing anihpj:v2 in the CI/CD pipeline with cosign]

          [COSIGN WORKFLOW — signing anihpj images]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                              │
          │  CI/CD Pipeline:                                             │
          │                                                              │
          │  1. docker build -t registry.anihpj.com/anihpj:v2 .        │
          │  2. docker push registry.anihpj.com/anihpj:v2               │
          │  3. cosign sign --key cosign.key registry.anihpj.com/anihpj:v2│
          │     → Signature stored in registry as                        │
          │       registry.anihpj.com/anihpj:sha256-<digest>.sig        │
          │                                                              │
          │  At cluster admission (Kyverno verifyImages):               │
          │  kubectl apply -f anihpj-deploy.yaml                        │
          │    → Kyverno calls: cosign verify --key cosign.pub image    │
          │    → ✅ Signature valid → pod created                       │
          │    → ❌ Signature invalid → pod rejected                    │
          │                                                              │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.3  Kyverno verifyImages — Enforcing Signed Images in Kubernetes
          - Kyverno policy type: verifyImages
          - Specifies the public key and which images require verification
          - Integration with cosign, notation, and other signing tools
          [CODE: Kyverno ClusterPolicy with verifyImages for anihpj]

    26.4  Keyless Signing with Sigstore (OIDC-based)
          - Keyless: no long-lived private keys to manage or rotate
          - Uses OIDC identity (GitHub Actions identity, Google SA) as the signer
          - Signature recorded in Rekor (public transparency log)
          - Best practice for CI/CD pipelines where key management is hard
          [CODE: cosign sign --keyless in a GitHub Actions workflow]


Chapter 27: Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) and SLSA
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    27.1  What Is an SBOM and Why Does It Matter?
          - SBOM: a complete list of every component in a software artifact
          - Like a nutrition label — but for software: lists all packages + versions
          - Enables: rapid CVE impact assessment ("are we affected by Log4Shell?")
          - Required by: US Executive Order on cybersecurity (2021), NTIA guidance
          [ILLUSTRATION: anihpj:v2 SBOM — Django 5.x, Pillow 10.x, psycopg2 2.9.x, etc.]

    27.2  Generating SBOMs for anihpj Images
          - Tools: Syft (Anchore), Trivy (--format cyclonedx), Docker sbom
          - Formats: CycloneDX (JSON/XML), SPDX (SBOM standard)
          - Generate in CI/CD pipeline, store alongside image in registry
          [CODE: syft anihpj:v2 -o cyclonedx-json > anihpj-v2.sbom.json]
          [CODE: Attaching SBOM to image using cosign attest]

    27.3  SLSA — Supply Chain Levels for Software Artifacts
          - SLSA (salsa): framework for supply chain integrity assurance
          - 4 levels (0-3): increasing guarantees about build provenance
          - Level 1: unsigned provenance; Level 3: hardened build environment
          - Prevents: unauthorized code modifications, compromised build systems
          [TABLE: SLSA levels and requirements]
          [DIAGRAM: SLSA Level 2 implementation for anihpj build pipeline]

    27.4  Build Provenance & in-toto Attestations
          - Provenance answers "WHERE did this artifact come from, and HOW was it
            built?" — a signed, machine-readable record of the build
          - in-toto attestation = a signed statement binding an image digest to
            metadata (source commit, builder identity, build steps, materials)
          - SLSA provenance is expressed as an in-toto predicate; cosign attaches
            and verifies it: cosign attest / cosign verify-attestation
          - The chain: source commit → CI build → attestation signed by the builder
            → admission verifies attestation before the pod runs
          - anihpj: GitHub Actions generates provenance, cosign signs it keyless
            (OIDC), Kyverno verifyImages checks it — no unverified image runs

          [PROVENANCE CHAIN — from commit to admission]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  git commit (source) ──▶ CI builds image + records build steps       │
          │        │                                                             │
          │        ▼                                                             │
          │  in-toto attestation (SLSA predicate) ──▶ cosign attest (signs it)   │
          │        │                                                             │
          │        ▼                                                             │
          │  registry stores image + signature + attestation                    │
          │        │                                                             │
          │        ▼                                                             │
          │  admission (Kyverno/cosign verify-attestation) ──▶ allow / DENY      │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: cosign attest a SLSA predicate for anihpj + cosign verify-attestation]

    27.5  Supply Chain CI/CD Integration — Artifact Repository Security
          - CKS curriculum: "understand your supply chain (SBOM, CI/CD, artifact
            repos)" — the pipeline itself is an attack surface
          - Secure the artifact repository: authenticated pushes only, immutable
            tags / retention, vulnerability scanning on push, RBAC on who can
            publish, and signing enforced before promotion to prod
          - Secure the CI/CD system: least-privilege runner credentials, no long-
            lived registry tokens (use OIDC), pin action/tool versions by digest,
            isolate build runners, protect signing keys (keyless OIDC ideal)
          - The full anihpj gate order: lint → scan (Trivy) → SBOM (syft) →
            sign (cosign) → attest (SLSA) → push to registry.anihpj.io → admission
            verifies signature + attestation → deploy

          [SECURE PIPELINE STAGES — control at every hop]
          ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ STAGE              │ CONTROL                                         │
          ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Source             │ signed commits, branch protection, no secrets   │
          │ Build              │ pinned tools, isolated runner, OIDC creds       │
          │ Scan               │ Trivy fail-on HIGH/CRITICAL                     │
          │ SBOM               │ syft generates SPDX/CycloneDX                   │
          │ Sign + attest      │ cosign (keyless) + SLSA provenance             │
          │ Artifact repo      │ authN push, immutable tags, RBAC, retention    │
          │ Deploy             │ admission verifies signature + attestation      │
          └────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Hardened GitHub Actions workflow for anihpj — full gate chain to registry.anihpj.io]
