Chapter 28: Kubernetes Audit Logging — Every API Call Recorded
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    28.1  What Is Audit Logging and Why Is It Non-Negotiable?
          - Audit log: a record of every request made to the Kubernetes API server
          - Records: who made the request, what they did, when, from where, on what
          - Without audit logs: you cannot prove what happened during an incident
          - Required by: PCI-DSS, SOC 2, HIPAA, GDPR for cluster environments
          [SCENARIO: Security incident — how audit logs tell the story of the attack]

    28.2  Configuring an Audit Policy (CKS Exam — Must Know)
          - Audit policy: YAML file defining what gets logged and at what level
          - Levels: None, Metadata, Request, RequestResponse
          - Rules: match resources, verbs, users, namespaces
          - More specific rules take priority (first match wins)
          [CODE: audit-policy.yaml — full CKS exam-level policy]
          [CODE: API server flags to enable audit logging to file]

          [AUDIT POLICY — CKS EXAM READY TEMPLATE]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  apiVersion: audit.k8s.io/v1                                │
          │  kind: Policy                                                │
          │  rules:                                                      │
          │    # Don't log node/system routine calls                    │
          │    - level: None                                             │
          │      users: ["system:nodes"]                                 │
          │      verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                        │
          │                                                              │
          │    # Log secret access at metadata level (not values!)      │
          │    - level: Metadata                                         │
          │      resources:                                              │
          │      - group: ""                                             │
          │        resources: ["secrets", "configmaps"]                 │
          │                                                              │
          │    # Log pod changes in full (RequestResponse)              │
          │    - level: RequestResponse                                  │
          │      resources:                                              │
          │      - group: ""                                             │
          │        resources: ["pods"]                                  │
          │      verbs: ["create", "update", "delete"]                 │
          │                                                              │
          │    # Default: log everything else at metadata               │
          │    - level: Metadata                                         │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.3  Reading and Analyzing Audit Logs
          - Audit logs are JSON lines written to /var/log/kubernetes/audit.log
          - Key fields: user.username, objectRef.resource, verb, responseStatus
          - Use: jq, grep, or a SIEM tool (Elasticsearch, Splunk, Datadog)
          [CODE: jq queries to extract suspicious activity from audit logs]
          [EXAMPLE: Reading audit logs to trace an attacker's actions]

    28.4  Shipping Audit Logs to External Systems
          - Local file: suitable for exam/testing
          - Webhook backend: ship to SIEM/logging platform in real-time
          - Cloud logging: GCP Audit Logs, AWS CloudTrail, Azure Monitor
          [CODE: Audit webhook configuration to ship to Elasticsearch]

    28.5  Detecting Suspicious Activity from Audit Logs
          - Red flags to watch for in audit logs:
            • Access to secrets by unexpected users/SAs
            • exec into pods (pods/exec verb) unexpectedly
            • Creating ClusterRoleBindings (privilege escalation)
            • Accessing /etc/kubernetes/ paths
            • Deleting audit log entries (tampering indicator)
          [TABLE: Suspicious audit log patterns + queries to detect them]

          [📋 AUDIT LOGGING ARCHITECTURE — how it flows from API to storage]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                          │
          │  👤 kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml                                     │
          │         │                                                                │
          │         ▼                                                                │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
          │  │  🧠 kube-apiserver with --audit-policy-file set                  │    │
          │  │  1. Authentication → 2. Authorization → 3. Admission → 4. etcd │    │
          │  │                              │                                     │    │
          │  │                              ▼                                     │    │
          │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │    │
          │  │  │  📋 AUDIT EVENT CREATED                                   │    │    │
          │  │  │  { timestamp, user, verb, resource, responseCode, ... }  │    │    │
          │  │  └────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘    │    │
          │  └───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┘    │
          │                              │                                            │
          │              ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐                           │
          │              ▼               ▼               ▼                            │
          │  ┌─────────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌─────────────────────┐           │
          │  │ 📄 Local File   │ │ 🌐 Webhook    │ │ ☁️  Cloud Native     │           │
          │  │ /var/log/k8s/   │ │ → Elasticsearch│ │ → GCP/AWS/Azure     │           │
          │  │ audit.log       │ │ → Splunk/SIEM │ │    Managed Audit   │           │
          │  │ CKS default!    │ │ Prod recommended│ │                     │           │
          │  └─────────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └─────────────────────┘           │
          │                                                                          │
          │  🔍 KEY jq QUERIES FOR CKS EXAM:                                         │
          │  cat audit.log | jq 'select(.objectRef.resource=="secrets")'            │
          │  cat audit.log | jq 'select(.objectRef.subresource=="exec")'            │
          │  cat audit.log | jq 'select(.responseStatus.code==403)'                 │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.6  Official Audit Facts — Levels vs Stages (the Exam's Favorite Confusion)
          - Straight from kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/debug/debug-cluster/audit: the
            exam frequently swaps "level" and "stage" in wrong-answer options —
            they control two completely different things.
          - LEVEL = how much detail is captured (defined per rule, in the policy).
            STAGE = at which point in the request lifecycle an event fires
            (built-in to Kubernetes, not something you choose per rule — though you
            CAN exclude specific stages globally or per-rule with omitStages).
          - The audit Policy resource has exactly ONE mandatory field: rules. A
            policy with rules: [] (zero rules) is REJECTED as illegal — you must
            have at least a catch-all "- level: Metadata" if nothing else.
          - Rules are evaluated TOP TO BOTTOM, first match wins — this is why
            28.2's template puts the narrow "don't log system:nodes" rule FIRST
            and the broad catch-all LAST; reversing the order would make the
            specific rules unreachable.
          - Flag precision: --audit-log-path enables the FILE backend (omitting it
            disables file logging even if --audit-policy-file is set);
            --audit-log-maxage (days), --audit-log-maxbackup (file count),
            --audit-log-maxsize (MB) control rotation. The log backend defaults to
            blocking mode; the separate webhook backend (--audit-webhook-config-file)
            defaults to batch mode — opposite defaults, easy to mix up.

          [LEVEL — exactly what gets captured, no more no less]
          ┌────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ LEVEL           │ CAPTURES                                              │
          ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ None            │ nothing — event is dropped entirely                   │
          │ Metadata        │ user, timestamp, verb, resource, response code — NO  │
          │                 │ request body, NO response body                       │
          │ Request         │ Metadata + the REQUEST body (e.g. the YAML you sent) │
          │                 │ — still no response body; N/A for non-resource URLs  │
          │ RequestResponse │ Metadata + REQUEST body + RESPONSE body — heaviest,  │
          │                 │ N/A for non-resource URLs                             │
          └────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [STAGE — WHEN in the request lifecycle an event is generated]
          ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ STAGE               │ WHEN IT FIRES                                    │
          ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ RequestReceived      │ as soon as the audit handler gets the request,  │
          │                      │ before it's passed down the handler chain      │
          │ ResponseStarted      │ response headers sent, body not yet (only for   │
          │                      │ long-running requests like watch)               │
          │ ResponseComplete     │ response body fully sent — no more bytes coming │
          │ Panic                │ the handler chain panicked                       │
          └────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Policy using omitStages: ["RequestReceived"] to halve event volume without losing detail]


Chapter 29: Falco — Runtime Threat Detection in Real-Time
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    29.1  What Is Falco and Why It's CKS Critical?
          - Falco: open-source, CNCF project for cloud-native runtime security
          - Monitors: system calls, network connections, file access in real-time
          - Generates alerts when containers behave suspiciously
          - Unlike audit logs (API-level), Falco sees INSIDE containers at the OS level
          - CKS exam: Falco rules, falco.yaml, alert output formats
          [DIAGRAM: Falco architecture — kernel module/eBPF → event stream → rules → alerts]

          [FALCO vs AUDIT LOGGING — complementary tools]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  AUDIT LOGGING                 FALCO                        │
          │  ─────────────                 ─────                        │
          │  Sees: API server calls        Sees: Kernel syscalls        │
          │  "kubectl exec into pod"       "shell spawned in container" │
          │  "secret read by user X"       "cat /etc/shadow inside pod" │
          │  "deployment updated"          "network conn to external IP"│
          │                                "binary written to /etc/"    │
          │                                                              │
          │  After attacker gets IN:       BEFORE/WHILE attacker acts:  │
          │  audit shows how they got in   Falco shows what they DO     │
          │                                                              │
          │  TOGETHER: complete picture of attack lifecycle              │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.2  Falco Architecture
          - Falco collects events from the Linux kernel via:
            • Kernel module (traditional, efficient)
            • eBPF probe (modern, safer, no kernel module needed)
          - Events match against rules defined in YAML files
          - Alerts sent to: stdout, file, Slack, Elasticsearch, webhooks

          [FALCO ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM — event flow from kernel to alert]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  🐧 LINUX KERNEL                                                     │
          │  Container makes a syscall (e.g., open /etc/shadow)                │
          │         │                                                            │
          │         │ kernel event stream                                        │
          │         ▼                                                            │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  FALCO DRIVER (choose one):                                  │   │
          │  │  • Kernel module (kmod): fast, needs kernel build headers    │   │
          │  │  • eBPF probe: safe, no recompile needed, CO-RE compatible  │   │
          │  │  • Modern eBPF (libbpf): newest, best for production        │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                 │ structured events                  │
          │                                 ▼                                    │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  FALCO ENGINE                                                 │   │
          │  │  Loads rules from: /etc/falco/falco_rules.yaml              │   │
          │  │                    /etc/falco/falco_rules.local.yaml         │   │
          │  │  Matches event against rule conditions                       │   │
          │  └──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘   │
          │                                 │ rule matched!                      │
          │                                 ▼                                    │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
          │  │  OUTPUT CHANNELS (configured in /etc/falco/falco.yaml)      │   │
          │  │  → stdout (default — good for kubectl logs falco-pod)       │   │
          │  │  → file  (/var/log/falco/falco.log)                         │   │
          │  │  → HTTP webhook (Slack, PagerDuty, SIEM)                    │   │
          │  │  → gRPC (falcosidekick for fan-out to multiple outputs)    │   │
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.3  Falco Rules — Writing and Reading Them
          - Rule = condition + output + priority
          - Condition: uses a filter expression (who, what, where)
          - Output: the alert message format
          - Priority: EMERGENCY, ALERT, CRITICAL, ERROR, WARNING, NOTICE, INFO, DEBUG
          [CODE: falco.yaml — key configuration fields]
          [CODE: Default Falco rules file explained — top 10 rules]

          [FALCO RULE ANATOMY — custom rule for anihpj]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  - rule: Unexpected Shell in anihpj Container               │
          │    desc: Detect a shell being spawned in the anihpj pod.   │
          │          Django containers should never spawn shells.       │
          │    condition: >                                              │
          │      spawned_process and                                    │
          │      container.name = "anihpj" and                         │
          │      proc.name in (shell_binaries)                          │
          │    output: >                                                 │
          │      Shell spawned in anihpj container!                    │
          │      (user=%user.name cmd=%proc.cmdline container=%container.name)│
          │    priority: CRITICAL                                        │
          │    tags: [anihpj, shell, CKS]                               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.4  Built-In Falco Rules You Must Know for CKS
          - Terminal shell in container
          - Read sensitive file (e.g., /etc/shadow, /etc/passwd)
          - Write below etc (write to /etc inside container)
          - Contact K8s API server from container
          - Launch privileged container
          - Container running as root

          [TOP 10 DEFAULT FALCO RULES — condition and what they detect]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  RULE NAME                      │ DETECTS                          │
          ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
          │  Terminal shell in container    │ bash/sh spawned inside a ctr    │
          │  Read sensitive file untrusted  │ cat /etc/shadow, /etc/passwd    │
          │  Write below etc               │ any write to /etc/ inside ctr   │
          │  Run shell untrusted           │ shell not in expected processes  │
          │  Netcat remote code execution  │ nc with -e flag (backdoor)      │
          │  Contact K8s API server from   │ curl/wget to kubernetes.default  │
          │    container                   │ (pod trying to reach K8s API)   │
          │  Launch privileged container   │ new container with privileged:t  │
          │  Clear log activities          │ rm/truncate of log files         │
          │  Create files below /dev       │ unusual writes to /dev/*         │
          │  Unexpected outbound conn to   │ connection to known malware C2C  │
          │    non-approved destination    │ IPs or unusual external IPs      │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [FALCO RULE FIELDS QUICK REFERENCE — for writing exam rules]
          ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  FIELD                   │  EXAMPLE VALUE                        │
          ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  spawned_process         │ true/false (is this a new process?)   │
          │  container.name          │ "anihpj" "postgres" "redis"           │
          │  container.image         │ "registry.anihpj.com/anihpj:v2"      │
          │  proc.name               │ "bash" "sh" "nc" "python3" "cat"     │
          │  proc.cmdline            │ "cat /etc/shadow"                     │
          │  fd.name                 │ "/etc/shadow" "/proc/sys/..."         │
          │  user.name               │ "root" "anihpj" "nobody"             │
          │  evt.type                │ "open" "read" "write" "execve"        │
          │  k8s.ns.name             │ "anihpj-prod" "kube-system"          │
          │  shell_binaries (macro)  │ bash, sh, zsh, ksh, dash, etc.       │
          └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.5  CKS Exam Tasks: Modifying Falco Rules
          - Task type: a container is doing X, write a Falco rule to detect it
          - Task type: a Falco rule is noisy, add an exception
          - Task type: change the alert output format or priority
          - Task type: identify what's triggering a Falco alert from output
          [CODE: Adding exception macros to reduce false positives]
          [CODE: Changing alert output to JSON format for SIEM]
          [CODE: Restarting Falco after rule changes]

    29.6  Behavioral Analytics — Detecting Anomalies Beyond Signature Rules
          - CKS curriculum: "perform behavioral analytics to detect malicious
            activities" — going past fixed rules to spot what's ABNORMAL
          - Signature detection = "alert when X happens" (a shell spawns in a
            container). Behavioral detection = "alert when behavior deviates from
            this workload's normal baseline"
          - Build a baseline of normal for anihpj: which processes run, which
            files it touches, which network peers it talks to — then flag drift
          - Falco is still the exam tool: profile-style rules + drift detection
            (unexpected process, outbound connection to a new IP, write outside
            expected paths) approximate behavioral analytics
          - Layer signals: Falco (syscalls) + audit logs (API) + NetworkPolicy
            logs (traffic) → correlate to reconstruct the phases of an attack

          [SIGNATURE vs BEHAVIORAL — two complementary lenses]
          ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ SIGNATURE (rule-based)     │ BEHAVIORAL (baseline drift)              │
          ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ "alert if shell in pod"    │ "alert if pod does anything unusual"     │
          │ catches KNOWN bad          │ catches NOVEL / zero-day bad             │
          │ low false positives        │ needs a good baseline first              │
          │ Falco default rules        │ Falco profiles + anomaly correlation     │
          └────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Falco rule set that flags anihpj deviations — unexpected process/egress/file write]


Chapter 30: Detecting and Responding to Security Incidents
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    30.1  The Incident Response Lifecycle in Kubernetes
          - Detect → Contain → Investigate → Eradicate → Recover → Learn
          - Detection: Falco alerts, audit log anomalies, monitoring alerts
          - Containment: isolate the compromised pod with NetworkPolicy
          - Investigate: copy pod state before deleting (forensic snapshot)
          [DIAGRAM: Incident response timeline — detection to resolution]

          [INCIDENT RESPONSE FLOWCHART — Kubernetes security event]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🚨 ALERT: Falco detects shell in anihpj pod               │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                           ▼                                  │
          │  1. CONFIRM: Is this legitimate?                             │
          │     kubectl describe pod anihpj-xxx                         │
          │     kubectl logs anihpj-xxx                                 │
          │     kubectl exec anihpj-xxx -- ps aux                      │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                    ❌ NOT legitimate                         │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                           ▼                                  │
          │  2. CONTAIN: Apply emergency NetworkPolicy (deny all)       │
          │     kubectl apply -f emergency-deny-all.yaml                │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                           ▼                                  │
          │  3. INVESTIGATE: Capture pod state                          │
          │     kubectl get pod anihpj-xxx -o yaml > forensic.yaml     │
          │     kubectl exec anihpj-xxx -- cat /proc/net/fib_trie      │
          │     kubectl exec anihpj-xxx -- ls /tmp /root /home         │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                           ▼                                  │
          │  4. ERADICATE: Delete compromised pod                       │
          │     kubectl delete pod anihpj-xxx --force                  │
          │     # Deployment will recreate clean pod from registry      │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                           ▼                                  │
          │  5. RECOVER + LEARN: Update policy + add detection rule     │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.2  Isolating a Compromised Pod Without Deleting It
          - Remove the pod from Service selectors: remove the label
          - Apply emergency deny-all NetworkPolicy to the pod's namespace
          - The pod is isolated but still running for forensic investigation
          [CODE: kubectl label pod to remove it from Service target]
          [CODE: Emergency NetworkPolicy — deny all traffic for one pod]

          [EMERGENCY POD ISOLATION — step-by-step command sequence]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # STEP 1: Identify the compromised pod                               │
          │  kubectl get pods -n anihpj -o wide                                   │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # STEP 2: Remove pod from Service (stops traffic immediately)        │
          │  kubectl label pod <pod-name> -n <ns> app-                           │
          │  # ↑ removes label "app" from the pod                                 │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # STEP 3: Apply emergency deny-all NetworkPolicy                    │
          │  cat <<EOF | kubectl apply -f -                                      │
          │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                                     │
          │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                                  │
          │  metadata:                                                            │
          │    name: emergency-deny-all                                           │
          │    namespace: anihpj                                                  │
          │  spec:                                                                │
          │    podSelector:                                                       │
          │      matchLabels:                                                     │
          │        app: anihpj                                                    │
          │    policyTypes:                                                       │
          │    - Ingress                                                          │
          │    - Egress                                                           │
          │  EOF                                                                  │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # STEP 4: Verify isolation                                           │
          │  kubectl exec <legit-pod> -- ping <compromised-pod-IP>              │
          │  # → Should FAIL (NetworkPolicy blocks)                               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.3  Forensic Investigation of a Kubernetes Pod
          - Capture: pod spec, environment variables, mounted secrets
          - Inspect: running processes, network connections, filesystem changes
          - Review: audit logs for the pod's ServiceAccount token usage
          - Preserve: container filesystem image for offline analysis
          [CODE: Pod forensic command sequence]

          [POD FORENSICS CHECKLIST — everything to capture before deletion]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # Full pod spec (with runtime state)                                 │
          │  kubectl get pod <name> -n <ns> -o yaml > pod-forensic.yaml         │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Environment variables (may contain injected secrets!)              │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- env > env-forensic.txt                        │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Running processes                                                  │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- ps aux > ps-forensic.txt                      │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Network connections (who is the pod talking to?)                   │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- netstat -tlnp > net-forensic.txt              │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- ss -tlnp >> net-forensic.txt                  │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Filesystem changes (what did the attacker modify?)                 │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- ls -laR /tmp > /tmp-files.txt               │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- cat /proc/1/cgroup > cgroup-info.txt         │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Mounted secrets                                                   │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- mount | grep secret                           │
          │  kubectl exec <pod> -- cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/token     │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # Audit logs (from control plane)                                   │
          │  grep <sa-name> /var/log/kubernetes/audit/audit.log                 │
          │                                                                      │
          │  # CONTAINER CHECKPOINT (if using containerd/CRI-O with criu)        │
          │  crictl checkpoint --export=/tmp/checkpoint.tar <container-id>       │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.4  Common Attack Scenarios & CKS Responses
          - Shell spawn in container → Falco detects + audit log confirms
          - Crypto-mining pod → Resource spike + HPA alert + image scan
          - Privilege escalation → Seccomp blocks + PSI Restricted rejects
          - Lateral movement → NetworkPolicy default-deny blocks path
          - Secret exfiltration → Audit log shows secret access + Falco alert
          [MATRIX TABLE: Attack Type → Detection Method → Response Action → CKS Tool]

          [ATTACK → RESPONSE PLAYBOOK — quick reference for CKS exam]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ATTACK             │ DETECTED BY     │ IMMEDIATE ACTION            │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
          │  Shell in container  │ Falco rule:     │ 1. kubectl exec -- ps aux  │
          │                      │ "Shell in Pod"  │ 2. Check: authorized user?  │
          │                      │                 │ 3. If not: isolate + delete │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
          │  Crypto-miner        │ High CPU alert  │ 1. kubectl top pod          │
          │                      │ + new process   │ 2. Check image source        │
          │                      │                 │ 3. Delete + block registry  │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
          │  Privilege escalation│ Falco: setuid   │ 1. Check SecurityContext     │
          │  (setuid binary)     │ syscall alert   │ 2. Block setuid binaries     │
          │                      │                 │ 3. no_new_privs: true       │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
          │  Secret access       │ Audit log:      │ 1. Check who accessed it     │
          │                      │ "get secrets"   │ 2. Rotate all secrets in ns  │
          │                      │                 │ 3. Encrypt at rest verify    │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
          │  Lateral movement    │ NetPol violation│ 1. Verify NetworkPolicy      │
          │  (pod→pod access)    │ logs + Falco    │ 2. Tighten allow rules       │
          │                      │                 │ 3. Implement default-deny    │
          ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
          │  Malicious image     │ Trivy scan:     │ 1. Delete all pods from img  │
          │                      │ CRITICAL CVEs   │ 2. Block registry in OPA     │
          │                      │                 │ 3. Verify image signing      │
          └──────────────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

    30.5  Incident Response Communication & Post-Mortem
          - Who to notify: Security team, DevOps lead, CISO (if data breach)
          - What to document: Timeline, detection method, containment steps
          - Post-mortem: What failed? What worked? How to prevent recurrence?
          [TEMPLATE: Kubernetes Security Incident Report]

    30.6  Identifying Attack Phases — MITRE ATT&CK for Kubernetes
          - CKS curriculum: "investigate and identify phases of attack and bad
            actors within the environment" — you must place evidence on a timeline
          - MITRE ATT&CK for Containers is the shared vocabulary: every alert maps
            to a TACTIC (the attacker's goal at that moment). Naming the phase tells
            you what already happened and what comes NEXT — so you contain ahead of it
          - The investigative loop: collect evidence (audit logs, Falco, NetPol
            logs) → map each event to a tactic → order them → find the entry point
            and the current furthest phase → contain forward of it
          - anihpj worked example: Falco "shell in pod" (Execution) + audit "get
            secrets" (Credential Access) + NetPol deny to postgres (Lateral Movement
            attempt) = attacker is mid-chain, pre-exfiltration → isolate NOW

          [MITRE ATT&CK TACTICS — K8s signal → phase → what's next]
          ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
          │ TACTIC (phase)       │ K8s EVIDENCE / SIGNAL       │ TYPICAL NEXT PHASE  │
          ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
          │ Initial Access       │ anonymous API hit, exposed  │ Execution           │
          │                      │ dashboard, exploited pod    │                     │
          │ Execution            │ Falco: shell/exec in pod    │ Persistence / PrivEsc│
          │ Persistence          │ new CronJob/DaemonSet, added│ Defense Evasion     │
          │                      │ SA token, modified image    │                     │
          │ Privilege Escalation │ Falco: setuid, hostPath,    │ Credential Access   │
          │                      │ privileged pod created      │                     │
          │ Defense Evasion      │ audit log deleted, Falco    │ Credential Access   │
          │                      │ stopped, logs cleared       │                     │
          │ Credential Access    │ audit: get secrets, read    │ Discovery / Lateral │
          │                      │ SA token, IMDS call         │                     │
          │ Discovery            │ audit: list pods/nodes/     │ Lateral Movement    │
          │                      │ secrets --all-namespaces    │                     │
          │ Lateral Movement     │ NetPol deny logs, pod→pod   │ Collection          │
          │                      │ connections to postgres     │                     │
          │ Collection/Exfil     │ large egress, DB dump,      │ Impact              │
          │                      │ outbound to unknown IP      │                     │
          │ Impact               │ crypto-miner CPU, ransom,   │ (goal reached)      │
          │                      │ resource deletion           │                     │
          └──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

          [THE DETECTION SURFACE — "detect threats within ... apps, networks,
           data, users and workloads" mapped to the tool that sees each]
          ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ THREAT SURFACE     │ WHAT WATCHES IT                                 │
          ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Infrastructure/node│ kube-bench, node auditd, host IDS               │
          │ Applications/pods  │ Falco (syscalls), Trivy Operator (running CVEs) │
          │ Networks           │ NetworkPolicy logs, CNI flow logs, service mesh │
          │ Data (secrets/etcd)│ audit log (secret access), encryption at rest   │
          │ Users/identities   │ audit log (who did what), RBAC review           │
          │ Workloads          │ admission events, PSS violations, drift alerts  │
          └────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          [CODE: Reconstruct an attack timeline for anihpj by correlating audit + Falco logs]


Chapter 31: Immutable Containers — Read-Only Filesystems
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    31.1  Why Immutability Is a Security Property
          - An attacker who can write to the container filesystem can:
            • Replace binaries with backdoored versions
            • Write malware to /etc/cron.d
            • Modify configuration to open backdoors
          - Immutable container: filesystem is read-only, nothing can change
          - Changes must go through the CI/CD pipeline and image rebuild
          [ILLUSTRATION: Immutable container = sealed shipping container — locked after packing]

          [READ-WRITE vs READ-ONLY CONTAINER — the security difference]
          ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
          │  ❌ WRITABLE FILESYSTEM       │   │  ✅ READ-ONLY FILESYSTEM       │
          │                               │   │                               │
          │  Attacker gets shell:         │   │  Attacker gets shell:         │
          │  → wget malware               │   │  → wget malware → ❌ FAIL     │
          │  → chmod +x malware           │   │  → chmod +x → ❌ FAIL         │
          │  → ./malware (backdoor)       │   │  → Can't write to /etc        │
          │  → echo */5 * * * * cmd > cron│   │  → Can't modify binaries      │
          │                               │   │                               │
          │  Result: PERSISTENT breach    │   │  Result: Pod restart = clean   │
          │  Pod restart = still infected │   │  Attack survived 0 seconds     │
          └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

    31.2  Enforcing Read-Only Filesystems in Kubernetes
          - securityContext.readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
          - Pods with writable files: use emptyDir volumes for /tmp, runtime dirs
          - Django media uploads: mount a PVC at /app/media
          [CODE: anihpj pod spec with readOnlyRootFilesystem and volume mounts]

          [Django anihpj with readOnlyRootFilesystem — complete pod spec]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  spec:                                                                │
          │    containers:                                                        │
          │    - name: anihpj                                                    │
          │      image: registry.example.com/anihpj:v2.1.0                       │
          │      securityContext:                                                 │
          │        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true   ← 🔐 THE KEY FIELD             │
          │        runAsNonRoot: true                                            │
          │        runAsUser: 1000                                                │
          │        capabilities:                                                  │
          │          drop: ["ALL"]                                                │
          │      volumeMounts:                                                    │
          │      - name: tmp                                                     │
          │        mountPath: /tmp          ← Writable for runtime needs         │
          │      - name: media-uploads                                           │
          │        mountPath: /app/media    ← Writable for user uploads          │
          │      - name: django-cache                                            │
          │        mountPath: /var/cache/django ← Writable for cache             │
          │    volumes:                                                           │
          │    - name: tmp                                                       │
          │      emptyDir: {}               ← Ephemeral, deleted with pod        │
          │    - name: media-uploads                                              │
          │      persistentVolumeClaim:     ← PVC for persistent uploads         │
          │        claimName: anihpj-media                                       │
          │    - name: django-cache                                               │
          │      emptyDir:                                                       │
          │        sizeLimit: 128Mi                                              │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    31.3  Using Pod Security Standards to Enforce Immutability
          - PSS Restricted level includes readOnlyRootFilesystem in best practices
          - Combine with Kyverno: require readOnlyRootFilesystem in all pods
          [CODE: Kyverno policy enforcing readOnlyRootFilesystem cluster-wide]

    31.4  Verifying Immutability at Runtime — Prove It, Don't Assume It
         - CKS curriculum: "ensure immutability of containers AT RUNTIME" — a
           readOnlyRootFilesystem field in YAML is a CONFIGURATION; immutability is
           only real once you've verified the running pod actually enforces it
         - Three-step verification loop every CKS candidate should drill: (1)
           inspect the applied spec, (2) attempt a live write and confirm it fails,
           (3) continuously monitor for drift/write attempts with Falco
         - When a workload legitimately needs to write somewhere (cache, tmp,
           uploads), that path must be an explicit emptyDir/PVC mount — NEVER a
           relaxed readOnlyRootFilesystem: false "to make it easier"
         - Falco closes the loop: even a correctly-configured immutable pod should
           be watched, because a kernel-level exploit could still attempt a write —
           Falco's "Write below etc" / "Write below binary dir" rules catch exactly
           that attempt regardless of what the YAML says

         [THE VERIFY LOOP — configure → attempt → detect]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. INSPECT — did the field actually apply?                          │
         │     kubectl get pod anihpj -o jsonpath=                              │
         │       '{.spec.containers[0].securityContext.readOnlyRootFilesystem}' │
         │     → must print: true                                               │
         │                         │                                            │
         │                         ▼                                            │
         │  2. ATTEMPT — try to write live, expect FAILURE                      │
         │     kubectl exec anihpj -- touch /etc/malicious                      │
         │     → expected: "Read-only file system" error                       │
         │     kubectl exec anihpj -- touch /tmp/ok-write-here                  │
         │     → expected: SUCCEEDS (the explicit writable mount still works)   │
         │                         │                                            │
         │                         ▼                                            │
         │  3. DETECT — Falco watches for write attempts regardless             │
         │     Falco rule: "Write below etc" / "Write below binary dir"         │
         │     → any write ATTEMPT (even a blocked one) generates an alert      │
         │       because a blocked write attempt is itself a signal             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WHEN A POD "NEEDS" TO WRITE — the right vs wrong fix]
         ┌────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ ❌ WRONG                   │ ✅ RIGHT                               │
         ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
         │ readOnlyRootFilesystem:    │ keep readOnlyRootFilesystem: true,     │
         │ false (disable it)         │ add an explicit emptyDir/PVC mount     │
         │                            │ ONLY for the specific path needed      │
         │ mount the whole / as       │ mount just /tmp, /app/media, or        │
         │ writable                   │ /var/cache/django — nothing broader    │
         └────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘
         [CODE: Full verify loop — kubectl inspect + exec write attempt + Falco alert check for anihpj]


Chapter 32: Compliance and Continuous Security Monitoring
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    32.1  Continuous Security Scanning — Keeping the Cluster Clean
          - One-time hardening is not enough — new vulnerabilities appear daily
          - Continuous scanning: periodically check images, manifests, configurations
          - Tools: Trivy Operator (runs in cluster, scans automatically)
          - Alert on new CRITICAL CVEs without waiting for next release
          [DIAGRAM: Trivy Operator — continuous cluster-wide vulnerability management]

    32.2  Security Benchmarking in CI/CD
          - Run kube-bench as part of cluster provisioning
          - Run on a schedule (nightly) to detect configuration drift
          - Alert when a previously-passing check now fails
          [CODE: kube-bench as a Kubernetes CronJob]

    32.3  Policy Compliance with OPA/Gatekeeper
          - Gatekeeper audit mode: check existing resources against policies
          - Constraint status reports violations without blocking existing pods
          - Use audit to find and remediate existing violations
          [CODE: kubectl get constraint -o jsonpath to get violation counts]

    32.4  Security Metrics and Dashboards
          - Expose security metrics to Prometheus
          - Grafana dashboards: CVE counts, policy violations, Falco alert rates
          - SLO for security: e.g., "zero CRITICAL CVEs in production images"
          [DASHBOARD SCREENSHOT DESCRIPTION: Security metrics dashboard layout]

Chapter 33: Complete Security Hardening of anihpj/jobpost
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    33.1  The Starting Point — An Unsecured anihpj Cluster
          - Baseline: anihpj running with default settings, no security hardening
          - Security score before hardening: kube-bench, kubesec, Trivy results
          - Attack surface analysis: what an attacker could do right now
          [ILLUSTRATION: Before/after security hardening comparison cards]

          [KUBERNETES SECURITY MATURITY LADDER — from insecure to CKS-hardened]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                      │
          │  🔴 LEVEL 0: INSECURE (default K8s — do not run anihpj like this!)  │
          │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
          │  ❌ Pods run as root             ❌ No NetworkPolicy                 │
          │  ❌ Secrets in plaintext env     ❌ No Seccomp/AppArmor              │
          │  ❌ No audit logging             ❌ Anonymous API access             │
          │  ❌ No image scanning            ❌ Overpermissive RBAC              │
          │  📊 kubesec score: 0-2/10                                          │
          │                                                                      │
          │       │  ▲  ADD: RBAC + runAsNonRoot + NetworkPolicy                │
          │       │                                                              │
          │       ▼                                                              │
          │  🟡 LEVEL 1: BASIC SECURED (minimum for any cluster)                │
          │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
          │  ✅ RBAC with least privilege   ✅ runAsNonRoot: true                │
          │  ✅ PSS Baseline enforced       ✅ Default-deny NetworkPolicy        │
          │  ✅ Ingress with TLS            ✅ Private container registry         │

          │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
          │  ✅ PSS Restricted enforced      ✅ Seccomp: RuntimeDefault         │
          │  ✅ AppArmor custom profiles      ✅ Encryption at rest enabled     │
          │  ✅ Read-only root filesystem     ✅ Drop ALL capabilities          │
          │  ✅ Audit logging configured      ✅ Falco runtime detection        │
          │  ✅ Immutable containers          ✅ NodeRestriction enabled         │
          │  📊 kubesec score: 7-8/10                                          │
          │                                                                      │
          │       │  ▲  ADD: Image signing + SBOM + OPA policies + mTLS         │
          │       │                                                              │
          │       ▼                                                              │
          │  🔵 LEVEL 3: ENTERPRISE (continuous security, GitOps-driven)        │
          │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
          │  ✅ cosign image signing         ✅ Kyverno verifyImages            │
          │  ✅ SBOM generated per build     ✅ SLSA Level 3 provenance        │
          │  ✅ OPA/Kyverno policies as code  ✅ mTLS for all pod traffic       │
          │  ✅ Sandboxed runtimes for untrust✅ Vault for external secrets     │
          │  ✅ Continuous Trivy Operator    ✅ Security dashboards in Grafana  │
          │  ✅ Automated incident response  ✅ GitOps: ArgoCD syncs policies   │
          │  📊 kubesec score: 9-10/10                                         │
          │                                                                      │
          │  🎓 This guide takes you:  Level 0 → Level 1 → Level 2 (CKS) →     │
          │     Level 3 (production best practices you'll be ready for)         │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    33.2  Step 1 — Secure the Image and Supply Chain
          - Build minimal image using python:3.12-alpine as base
          - Multi-stage build: compile in full image, run in slim final stage
          - Trivy scan: achieve zero CRITICAL CVEs before push
          - Sign image with cosign in CI pipeline
          - Enforce image signature verification via Kyverno
          [CODE: Hardened Dockerfile for anihpj]
          [CODE: GitHub Actions pipeline with Trivy scan + cosign sign]

    33.3  Step 2 — Harden the Pod Security Context
          - runAsNonRoot: true (create Django user in Dockerfile)
          - runAsUser: 1000, runAsGroup: 1000
          - allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
          - readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
          - capabilities: drop ALL
          - seccompProfile: RuntimeDefault
          - Apply AppArmor profile to anihpj container
          [CODE: Complete hardened pod spec for anihpj]

    33.4  Step 3 — Namespace and Admission Control
          - Apply PSS Restricted enforcement to anihpj-prod namespace
          - Apply PSS Baseline to anihpj-dev namespace
          - Deploy OPA Gatekeeper with image registry policy
          - Deploy Kyverno with verifyImages for cosign
          [CODE: Namespace labels and Gatekeeper/Kyverno policies]

    33.5  Step 4 — RBAC and Service Accounts
          - Create dedicated ServiceAccounts for each workload
          - Disable automountServiceAccountToken for anihpj, postgres pods
          - Create minimal Roles and RoleBindings for CI/CD and operators
          - Audit with kubectl auth can-i to verify least privilege
          [CODE: Complete RBAC manifest set for anihpj in production]

    33.6  Step 5 — Network Isolation
          - Apply default-deny-all NetworkPolicy to anihpj-prod
          - Allow: ingress-controller → anihpj (port 8000)
          - Allow: anihpj → postgres (port 5432)
          - Allow: anihpj → redis (port 6379)
          - Allow: all pods → kube-dns (port 53, UDP)
          - Allow: monitoring → all pods (port 9090)
          [CODE: All NetworkPolicy manifests for anihpj]

    33.7  Step 6 — Secrets Encryption
          - Enable encryption at rest for Secrets in etcd
          - Rotate all existing Secrets after enabling encryption
          - Configure Vault integration for Django SECRET_KEY
          - Verify: direct etcd read returns encrypted (not plaintext) data
          [CODE: EncryptionConfiguration and rotation commands]

    33.8  Step 7 — Runtime Monitoring
          - Deploy Falco with custom rules for anihpj
          - Create Falco rule: alert on shell in anihpj container
          - Create Falco rule: alert on unexpected outbound connections
          - Configure audit policy to log secret access and pod exec
          [CODE: Custom Falco rules + audit policy for anihpj]

    33.9  Comparing Before and After — The Security Scorecard
          [BEFORE vs AFTER TABLE: security properties changed]
          | Security Property          | Before Hardening  | After Hardening    |
          | Image CVEs (CRITICAL)      | 2                 | 0                  |
          | Image signing              | ❌ Not signed     | ✅ Signed + verified|
          | Runs as non-root           | ❌ root (UID 0)   | ✅ UID 1000        |

Chapter 34: Incident Response — Simulating and Reacting to an Attack
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    34.1  Simulated Attack Scenario — anihpj Under Attack
          - Attacker scenario: CVE in old Pillow version allows file read
          - Attacker reads /etc/passwd from inside container
          - Lateral movement attempt: curl to postgres, other pods
          - Privilege escalation attempt: write to /etc/cron.d
          [STEP-BY-STEP ATTACK NARRATIVE with kubectl commands the attacker uses]

    34.2  Detection — How Each Security Layer Catches It
          - Falco: detects read of /etc/passwd and unexpected curl
          - Audit log: detects pod exec (initial access)
          - NetworkPolicy: blocks lateral movement to postgres
          - readOnlyRootFilesystem: blocks write to /etc/cron.d
          - Trivy Operator: alerts on CVE in running image
          [TIMELINE: Attack step → security layer that detected/blocked it]

    34.3  Response — Containing and Recovering the anihpj Service
          - Isolate the compromised pod (NetworkPolicy label removal)
          - Capture forensic evidence (pod state, logs, network connections)
          - Eradicate: force-delete compromised pod
          - Recover: new pod starts from hardened image automatically
          - Rebuild: fix CVE in Pillow, rebuild, Trivy scan, cosign sign, redeploy
          [CODE: Complete incident response command sequence]

    34.4  Post-Incident — Strengthening the Security Posture
          - Add Falco rule: alert on ANY exec to anihpj pod
          - Tighten NetworkPolicy: block all outbound except postgres + redis
          - Update Trivy CI gate: maximum severity HIGH (not CRITICAL)
          - Schedule weekly kube-bench runs in CronJob
          [CODE: Updated Falco rules + NetworkPolicy after incident]


Chapter 35: Security Automation and GitOps for anihpj
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    35.1  GitOps Security — Why Security Should Live in Git
          - All Kubernetes manifests in Git: RBAC, NetworkPolicy, PSS labels
          - Security-as-Code: policy changes go through PR review + approval
          - Audit trail: every security change has a commit, author, timestamp
          - Drift detection: ArgoCD detects and alerts on manual cluster changes
          [DIAGRAM: GitOps security loop — PR → review → ArgoCD sync → enforce]

    35.2  Automating Security Scans in CI/CD (GitHub Actions)
          - On every PR: run kube-linter, kubesec, Trivy, conftest
          - On merge: cosign sign the image, update SBOM
          - On schedule: run Trivy Operator, kube-bench, Falco rule tests
          [CODE: Complete GitHub Actions workflow for anihpj security pipeline]

    35.3  Policy as Code — Keeping Policies in Version Control
          - OPA Gatekeeper ConstraintTemplates in Git
          - Kyverno ClusterPolicies in Git
          - Falco rules in Git (mounted as ConfigMap into Falco pod)
          - Audit policy in Git (mounted to kube-apiserver via hostPath)
          [CODE: ArgoCD Application resource for anihpj security policies]

PART 9: CKS EXAM PREPARATION                                              🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 36: CKS Exam Strategy — Time Management and Approach
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    36.1  Exam Format and Environment
          - 2 hours, approximately 15-20 performance-based tasks
          - Live terminal (PSI secure browser), 1-2 clusters to work on
          - Browser tabs allowed: kubernetes.io/docs, falco.org, Kubernetes GitHub
          - No external tools: no IDE, no AI, no personal notes
          - Pass score: 67% (roughly 10-13/15 tasks fully correct)

          [CKS EXAM AT A GLANCE — info card]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🎓 CKS EXAM DETAILS                                                 │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Full name:   Certified Kubernetes Security Specialist               │
          │  Provider:    Linux Foundation / CNCF                                │
          │  Duration:    2 hours                                                │
          │  Questions:   ~15-20 performance-based (hands-on terminal tasks)    │
          │  Pass score:  67%                                                    │
          │  Cost:        $395 USD (includes 1 free retake)                     │
          │  Validity:    2 years from pass date                                 │
          │  Prerequisite:Active CKA certification REQUIRED                     │
          │  Environment: PSI Secure Browser, remote proctor, webcam required  │
          │  Browser tabs:kubernetes.io, falco.org, github.com/falcosecurity    │
          │                                                                      │
          │  What to bring: government photo ID                                 │
          │  What NOT to bring: notes, second monitor, phone, food near desk   │
          │                                                                      │
          │  Schedule at: training.linuxfoundation.org                          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          [CKS vs CKA vs CKAD EXAM COMPARISON]
          ┌───────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────┐
          │  Feature          │  CKAD          │  CKA           │  CKS           │
          ├───────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
          │  Duration         │  2 hours       │  2 hours       │  2 hours       │
          │  Pass score       │  66%           │  66%           │  67%           │
          │  Cost             │  $395          │  $395          │  $395          │
          │  Difficulty       │  Moderate      │  Hard          │  Very Hard     │
          │  Prereq           │  None          │  None          │  Active CKA    │
          │  Q count          │  ~15-20        │  ~17           │  ~15-20        │
          │  Hands-on         │  ✅ Yes        │  ✅ Yes        │  ✅ Yes        │
          │  Primary focus    │  App deploy    │  Cluster ops   │  Security      │
          │  Validity         │  2 years       │  2 years       │  2 years       │
          └───────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────────┘

    36.2  Time Management — The 3-Pass Strategy
          - Pass 1 (first 45 min): quick scan all tasks, solve easy ones (1-3 min each)
          - Pass 2 (next 60 min): tackle medium difficulty tasks
          - Pass 3 (last 15 min): return to skipped tasks, verify answers
          [TIME ALLOCATION DIAGRAM: 120 min broken into 3 passes]

          [CKS TASK DIFFICULTY vs TIME ALLOCATION]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  QUICK (< 3 min each) — do these first:                   │
          │    • RBAC: create Role + RoleBinding                       │
          │    • Pod with specific securityContext                     │
          │    • Namespace with PSS labels                             │
          │    • kubectl auth can-i verification                       │
          │                                                            │
          │  MEDIUM (3-8 min each) — do these second:                 │
          │    • NetworkPolicy: default deny + specific allow          │
          │    • Falco rule: write custom rule                         │
          │    • Seccomp/AppArmor: apply profile to pod               │
          │    • Trivy: scan image, record CVE findings               │
          │    • Audit policy: configure and enable                   │
          │                                                            │
          │  HARD (8-15 min each) — do these last:                   │
          │    • Encryption at rest: configure + verify               │
          │    • OPA/Gatekeeper: write + apply ConstraintTemplate     │
          │    • ImagePolicyWebhook: configure admission               │
          │    • Full incident response scenario                       │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    36.3  Context Switching — The Most Important CKS Exam Habit
          - Each task specifies a cluster context to use
          - ALWAYS run: kubectl config use-context <name> before each task
          - Forgetting this = solving the right problem on the wrong cluster = 0 points
          - Tip: copy the context switch command from the task description
          [CODE: Context switch workflow for each CKS exam task]

    36.4  File Locations You Must Know (No Internet Search Needed)
          [TABLE: Critical file paths for CKS exam]
          | File                                    | Purpose                          |
          | /etc/kubernetes/manifests/              | Static pod manifests             |
          | /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml | API server config         |
          | /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml            | Kubelet configuration            |
          | /etc/falco/falco.yaml                   | Falco main config                |
          | /etc/falco/falco_rules.yaml             | Default Falco rules              |
          | /etc/kubernetes/audit-policy.yaml       | Audit policy                     |
          | /var/lib/kubelet/seccomp/               | Local seccomp profiles           |
          | /var/lib/etcd/                          | etcd data directory              |
          | /etc/kubernetes/pki/                    | Cluster PKI certificates         |
          | /etc/apparmor.d/                        | AppArmor profile directory       |


Chapter 37: Mock Scenarios & Practice Labs
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    37.1  Domain 1: Cluster Setup Practice Tasks
          - Task A: Run kube-bench on control plane, fix top 3 FAIL items
          - Task B: Apply NetworkPolicy set to isolate namespace
          - Task C: Configure Ingress with TLS for a test service
          - Task D: Secure Kubernetes Dashboard access with token + NetworkPolicy
          [DETAILED TASK INSTRUCTIONS + EXPECTED SOLUTION for each]

    37.2  Domain 2: Cluster Hardening Practice Tasks
          - Task A: Disable anonymous auth on API server, verify
          - Task B: Create audit policy, enable audit logging, verify log output
          - Task C: Create minimal RBAC for a given service account
          - Task D: Configure ServiceAccount with automount disabled
          [DETAILED TASK INSTRUCTIONS + EXPECTED SOLUTION for each]

    37.3  Domain 3: System Hardening Practice Tasks
          - Task A: Load AppArmor profile and apply to a pod
          - Task B: Apply seccomp RuntimeDefault profile to a pod
          - Task C: Harden kubelet: disable anonymous auth, enable Webhook
          - Task D: Remove unnecessary services from a node
          [DETAILED TASK INSTRUCTIONS + EXPECTED SOLUTION for each]

    37.4  Domain 4: Microservice Vulnerability Practice Tasks
          - Task A: Apply PSS Restricted to a namespace, fix violating pods
          - Task B: Add full security context to anihpj pod (non-root, drop ALL)
          - Task C: Enable encryption at rest for Secrets in etcd
          - Task D: Apply RuntimeClass gVisor to an untrusted workload
          [DETAILED TASK INSTRUCTIONS + EXPECTED SOLUTION for each]

    37.5  Domain 5: Supply Chain Security Practice Tasks
          - Task A: Scan an image with Trivy, report CRITICAL CVEs
          - Task B: Create OPA ConstraintTemplate to block unapproved images
          - Task C: Sign an image with cosign, configure Kyverno to verify it
          - Task D: Deploy ImagePolicyWebhook, test image rejection
          [DETAILED TASK INSTRUCTIONS + EXPECTED SOLUTION for each]

    37.6  Domain 6: Runtime Security Practice Tasks
          - Task A: Configure Falco audit policy — log secret reads
          - Task B: Write Falco rule to detect shell in specific container
          - Task C: Investigate a Falco alert, identify the suspicious process
          - Task D: Enable audit logging, find a suspicious kubectl exec from logs
          [DETAILED TASK INSTRUCTIONS + EXPECTED SOLUTION for each]

Chapter 38: Last-Mile Review — Quick Reference and Memory Aids
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    38.1  One-Page CKS Domain Cheat Sheet

          [CKS DOMAIN CHEAT SHEET — key commands + files per domain]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  DOMAIN 1: CLUSTER SETUP (10%)                                            │
          │  kube-bench run --targets=master                                          │
          │  vi /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml                         │
          │  kubectl apply -f networkpolicy.yaml                                      │
          │  kubectl create secret tls tls-secret --cert=tls.crt --key=tls.key       │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  DOMAIN 2: CLUSTER HARDENING (15%)                                        │
          │  # API server: --anonymous-auth=false --audit-log-path=...               │
          │  kubectl create role r1 --verb=get --resource=pods -n ns                 │
          │  kubectl create rolebinding rb1 --role=r1 --serviceaccount=ns:sa         │
          │  kubectl auth can-i get pods --as=system:serviceaccount:ns:sa            │
          │  kubectl patch sa default -p '{"automountServiceAccountToken":false}'     │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  DOMAIN 3: SYSTEM HARDENING (15%)                                         │
          │  aa-status | grep <profile-name>                                          │
          │  apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/<profile>                            │
          │  ssh <node> systemctl status apparmor                                    │
          │  # Pod: securityContext.appArmorProfile.type: Localhost                  │
          │  # Pod: securityContext.seccompProfile.type: RuntimeDefault              │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  DOMAIN 4: MICROSERVICE VULNERABILITIES (20%)                             │
          │  kubectl label ns <ns> pod-security.k8s.io/enforce=restricted            │
          │  # Pod: runAsNonRoot:true, allowPrivEscalation:false, drop:ALL           │
          │  # EncryptionConfiguration: aescbc provider → API server flag            │
          │  ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl get /registry/secrets/ns/name | hexdump -C       │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  DOMAIN 5: SUPPLY CHAIN SECURITY (20%)                                    │
          │  trivy image --severity CRITICAL image:tag                               │
          │  cosign sign --key cosign.key image@sha256:...                           │
          │  cosign verify --key cosign.pub image@sha256:...                         │
          │  kubectl apply -f constraint-template.yaml -f constraint.yaml            │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  DOMAIN 6: MONITORING, LOGGING & RUNTIME SECURITY (20%)                   │
          │  tail -f /var/log/kubernetes/audit.log | jq .                            │
          │  kubectl logs -n falco -l app=falco -f                                   │
          │  vi /etc/falco/falco_rules.local.yaml  # add custom rule                │
          │  kubectl rollout restart daemonset falco -n falco                        │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    38.2  Critical kubectl Commands for CKS
          [COMMAND TABLE: 40 most-used kubectl commands in CKS context]

          [CKS EXAM DAY MENTAL MODEL — your brain map during the exam]

          │  │  IS THIS ABOUT:     │     │  THEN USE THESE FILES:   │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  API Server?        │────▶│ /etc/kubernetes/manifests│       │
          │  │  (auth, audit,      │     │ /kube-apiserver.yaml    │       │
          │  │   admission)        │     │ --anonymous-auth=false   │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  kubelet?           │────▶│ /var/lib/kubelet/        │       │
          │  │  (node agent,       │     │ config.yaml              │       │
          │  │   auth config)      │     │ anonymous.enabled=false  │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  Pod Security?      │────▶│ kubectl label ns ...     │       │
          │  │  (PSS, seccomp,     │     │ securityContext:         │       │
          │  │   AppArmor, caps)   │     │ in pod YAML spec         │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  RBAC / SA?        │────▶│ kubectl create role ...  │       │
          │  │  (who can do what)  │     │ kubectl auth can-i ...   │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  NetworkPolicy?     │────▶│ kubectl apply -f np.yaml │       │
          │  │  (pod isolation)    │     │ kubectl run test -- ...  │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  Secrets / etcd?    │────▶│ EncryptionConfiguration  │       │
          │  │  (encryption)       │     │ etcdctl get ... | hexdump│       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  Image / Supply?    │────▶│ trivy image ...          │       │
          │  │  (CVE, signing)     │     │ cosign sign/verify ...   │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  OPA / Kyverno?     │────▶│ ConstraintTemplate YAML  │       │
          │  │  (admission policy) │     │ ClusterPolicy YAML       │       │
          │  ├─────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────┤       │
          │  │  Falco / Audit?     │────▶│ /etc/falco/falco.yaml    │       │
          │  │  (runtime detection)│     │ audit-policy.yaml        │       │
          │  └─────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────┘       │
          │                                                                   │
          │  🔑 GOLDEN RULE: Always check kubectl config current-context     │
          │     BEFORE starting each task!                                   │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          | Check PSS labels on namespace   | kubectl get ns <name> -o yaml | grep pod-security    |

          | Test RBAC                        | kubectl auth can-i get secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:ns:sa |
          | Disable SA automount             | kubectl patch sa default -p '{"automountServiceAccountToken":false}' |
          | Get Falco rules                  | kubectl get cm falco-rules -n falco -o yaml          |
          | Restart Falco after change       | kubectl rollout restart ds falco -n falco            |
          | Check seccomp on running pod     | kubectl get pod <name> -o jsonpath='{...seccompProfile}' |
          | Check AppArmor on node           | ssh node01 aa-status                                 |
          | Verify encryption at rest        | ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl get /registry/secrets/... | hexdump |
          | View audit logs                  | tail -f /var/log/kubernetes/audit.log | jq .           |
          | Scan image with Trivy            | trivy image --severity CRITICAL registry/image:tag    |
          | Verify cosign signature          | cosign verify --key cosign.pub registry/image:tag     |

    38.6  10-Week CKS Study Plan
          [WEEKLY STUDY PLAN]
          | Week | Focus                          | Practice                              |
          | 1    | Security fundamentals + 4Cs    | RBAC exercises, NetworkPolicy lab     |
          | 2    | Cluster Setup: CIS, kube-bench | kube-bench on local cluster, fix FAILs|
          | 3    | Cluster Hardening: API, RBAC   | Audit policy lab, SA hardening        |
          | 4    | System Hardening: AppArmor     | Write and apply AppArmor profiles     |
          | 5    | System Hardening: Seccomp      | Apply RuntimeDefault + custom profiles|
          | 6    | Microservice: PSS, SecContext  | PSS namespace labels, full secctx pod |
          | 6    | Microservice: Secrets encrypt  | Enable encryption at rest lab         |
          | 7    | Supply Chain: Trivy + images   | Scan, fix CVEs, multi-stage build     |
          | 7    | Supply Chain: OPA + cosign     | Write ConstraintTemplate, sign images |
          | 8    | Runtime: Falco + Audit         | Custom Falco rules, audit policy lab  |
          | 9    | Full mock exam #1              | killer.sh exam sim + review gaps      |
          | 9    | Targeted review of weak areas  | Focus on missed domain topics         |
          | 10   | Full mock exam #2 + polish     | killer.sh second attempt + light review|
          |      | Exam day! 🎯                    | Warm up with quick labs, then exam    |
